He'd carved this on the road, before the dock, before the symbol in the driftwood, before any of it.

His hands had known her posture before his eyes had seen it.

She sat with this. She turned the figure slowly, feeling the grain under her thumbs, the slight roughness where the knife had hesitated at the junction of the tucked arm and the body — as if even the blade had paused at that particular gesture and considered what it meant.

The disc pulsed once in her coat pocket. The low warm vibration she'd been feeling with increasing frequency, the metal doing something it had no business doing, responding to proximity or alignment or whatever physics governed ten-thousand-year-old mechanisms that were supposed to be inert. She pressed her hand against the pocket and the pulsing settled.

She looked at the carving again.

She thought: *he knows what I am.* Not the details — not the disc, not the hill, not the letter from a dead uncle about a father who'd walked east and hadn't come back. But the shape of it. The condition of being something you couldn't show. He'd found it in the wood before he'd found it in her, and he'd sent it to her without explanation because the carving was the explanation.

She set it on the table next to the disc.

Two objects. One she'd been given by a father who'd left. One she'd been given by a stranger who'd stayed in the same city. Both asked her to go somewhere.

---

Vane came back twenty minutes later. She read the room the way she read every room — entry, objects, exits, the specific arrangement of Maren's attention — and her eyes found the carving on the table and stayed there.

"Who sent it?" she said.

"The young man from the dock."

Vane picked it up without asking. She held it the way she held everything — with assessment, both hands, testing the weight. She turned it once, twice, brought it close to her face. Smelling it, Maren realized. Reading the carving the way she read everything else: through chemistry.

"Pine. Old cut — four weeks, maybe five. He's been handling it constantly." She turned it again. "The knife work is trained. Not professional, but someone taught him." She set it down. "He sent this as an introduction."

"Yes."

"Bold." Vane's eyes were on the carving but her attention was somewhere past it, in the place where she calculated risk. "He's telling you he's been thinking about you. That's either honest or it's a very good opening move."

Maren looked at the carving on the table, the tucked arm, and said nothing. Vane hadn't mentioned the arm. She'd read the wood and the scent and the sender's intent, and she'd missed the thing that mattered most — or rather, she'd never had reason to notice it, because Vane had never watched Maren the way a carver watches a subject. She knew the smell of Maren's fear and the chemical signature of her alertness. She did not know the specific geometry of how Maren held her body when she was protecting the arm that could get her killed.

This was not a thing Maren was going to explain. Not yet. The carving sat on the table between them and it contained a recognition that belonged to Maren and to the person who'd made it, and sharing it with Vane would turn it into information, and information was what Vane did with everything, and some things should be allowed to stay what they were for a while before they became useful.

"There's a message in the bag," Vane said. She hadn't looked in the bag. She'd smelled it — a faint chemical trace, ink on cloth, the particular sharpness of dock-district lamp oil. "Inside the cord."

Maren untwisted the cord. A strip of paper, folded once. Two words and a time: *Thursday. Afternoon.*

"The cooperage," Maren said.

"Probably." Vane sat down. "I'll walk the building tomorrow morning. The yard, the surrounding streets, whatever exits there are. I need to see it before you go in."

"All right."

"Not *all right* like you're humoring me. All right like you understand that I can't keep you safe in a place I haven't seen."

Maren looked at her. "All right," she said again, differently.

---

Vane came back from the cooperage the next morning with dirt on her boots and the particular expression she wore when a location had met her standards, which was to say: no expression at all, but an absence of the tension that meant *bad.*

"The yard is workable," she said. She sat down and drew on the back of the eastern road map with a stub of charcoal — quick lines, the spatial shorthand of someone who thought in exits and sight lines. "Bounded on three sides by the building and storage sheds. The fourth side is a wooden fence — slat construction, good visibility through. There's a gate on the far side. The gate opens onto a service alley running north to the river road. Three minutes at a walk, less if you run. Low wall at the midpoint, climbable."

Maren looked at the sketch. "You want to be in the alley."

"At the gate. I can see through the slats without being in the yard. If something goes wrong you come to me, not the other way around." She paused. "I want a signal. Something you can do without him noticing."

"Such as."

"You'll have the carving. Set it on the table at the start. If you pick it up and put it in your pocket, I come in."

Maren nodded. The operational architecture of this was familiar now — familiar as the room's dimensions and the alley's geography and the specific cadence of Vane's morning rounds. She'd spent eight days inside the structure of Vane's caution and had learned to read it the way you read weather: not as obstruction but as information about the conditions you were operating in.

"He's not dangerous," Vane said. "His smell is curiosity and something I can't place — a warmth that doesn't match anything in my reference set. Not threat. Not deception." She paused. "But I don't know him, and I don't know who knows him, and someone sending you a portrait of yourself made before you met is not a thing I can file and move on from."

Maren set her jaw and catalogued the argument the way she catalogued exits — noted, filed, not available for debate. "I'm going," she said.

"I know you're going. I knew you were going before you opened the bag." Vane looked at the carving on the table. "That's not the question. The question is whether you go prepared."

"I'm always prepared."

"You carry a knife on your left hip and you've never once adjusted for a draw." Vane said it without emphasis. A fact, laid alongside the other facts, but this one landed differently. "You carry it the way someone carries a tool. Not the way someone carries something they might need in the next three seconds."

Maren said nothing. The knife was her mother's — a good blade, well-balanced, the handle worn smooth by years of kitchen work before it became the thing Maren put on her belt when she left Ashwick. She'd thought of it as a knife. Not as a weapon. The distinction, apparently, was visible to someone who'd spent a decade making the distinction matter.

Vane stood up. "Show me where it sits."

Maren stood. She touched the knife at her hip — the familiar position, riding just behind the bone.

"Too far back," Vane said. "You'd have to reach behind yourself. That's a half-second you don't have if someone's in front of you." She came around the table and stood beside Maren, not touching, and pointed to a spot two inches forward on the belt. "Here. Just ahead of the hip bone. Your hand falls to it without your elbow having to move."

Maren shifted the knife. The new position felt wrong — too prominent, too accessible, too much like a statement about what she expected from the world.

"It feels exposed," she said.

"Good. That means you know it's there." Vane stepped back. "Draw it."

Maren drew. She drew the way she'd always drawn — reaching, gripping, pulling — and by the time the blade cleared the sheath Vane was looking at her with the flat assessment of someone watching a thing done incorrectly.

"Your thumb goes to the pommel first," Vane said. "Contact before you grip. You're finding it, then drawing it. Two motions instead of one." She took the knife from Maren's hand, replaced it in the sheath, and drew it in a single motion that was so fast Maren registered the blade's position before she registered the movement that put it there. "One motion. The hand knows where it is because you've already told it where it is. You set it in the right place on your belt so your hand doesn't have to search."

She handed the knife back.

"Again."

Maren drew. Better — faster — but her wrist turned at the wrong angle and the blade came up pointing inward.

"You're pulling it to yourself. Pull it away. The edge goes out. Your body is behind the blade, not in front of it."

Again. And again. Ten minutes of the same motion, the same correction, Vane adjusting Maren's grip twice and her elbow once, each adjustment delivered with the economy of someone who had been taught this way and saw no reason to teach it differently. No warmth in the instruction, no encouragement. Just the information and the expectation that the information would be used.

Maren's hand began to learn the new position. By the eighth draw, the motion was cleaner — not fast, not graceful, but correct, the blade arriving where it should in the orientation it should, her body behind it.

"Better," Vane said. It was the first word of evaluation she'd offered, and it arrived without inflection, a measurement rather than a compliment.

"I want you to practice that every morning," Vane said. She sat back down at the table and her hands went flat — the thinking-already-done gesture. "Twenty draws. When you can do it without looking at your hand, we'll work on the next thing."

She did not say what the next thing was. Maren suspected this was deliberate.

"Take the disc on Thursday," Vane said, returning to the cooperage as if the knife lesson had been a parenthetical. "If he knows the symbol, show him. If he doesn't, you've lost nothing. But don't show him the letter."

"Why not the letter?"

"Because the letter is the destination. The disc is the question. Give him the question first and see what he does with it." She tapped the charcoal sketch of the cooperage yard. "Anyone can follow a destination. You want to know if he's asking the same question."

---

The cooperage yard was smaller than Maren had expected — a working space, not a public one, bounded on three sides by the cooperage building and its storage sheds and on the fourth by the slatted fence Vane had drawn. Stacked barrel hoops leaned against the near wall. The ground was packed earth, darkened in patches where water had been used and dried unevenly. The smell was oak and vinegar and the faint sweetness of new wood soaking, and underneath it the river, two streets away, doing what rivers did.

She'd come through the front with a purchased hoop-set she had no use for, and the apprentice had nodded her through to the yard with practiced non-attention. She thought about the infrastructure of discretion, the way the cooperage functioned as a kind of lock, the purchase as the key, the apprentice as the mechanism that turned without caring what it let through.

The yard was empty. She was early.

She sat on an upturned barrel near the center and set the hoop-set beside her and waited. The afternoon was cool, the light coming in at the low angle that meant the season was turning toward the shorter days. She could hear the cooperage workers inside — the rhythmic knock of mallets on hoops, the creak of staves being bent, the ordinary sounds of people making things. Above the roofline, the sky was the particular gray-blue of a city afternoon, the color of air that had been breathed by too many people and was making the best of it.

She checked the gate. Closed but not latched. The service alley beyond it was visible through the slats — narrow, running north, the low wall at the midpoint exactly where Vane had sketched it.

Vane was out there. Maren couldn't see her and didn't expect to. She'd watched Vane leave the room an hour before the meeting, and the tremor in her hands had been worse today, and neither of them had said anything about it.

She waited. The knife sat two inches forward on her belt. She'd practiced the draw that morning, twenty times as instructed, and the new position was becoming less foreign — not natural yet, but no longer the wrong place, more like a place she hadn't known was right.

The yard door opened.

---

He was taller than she remembered from the dock. Or she'd been sitting then and the angle had compressed him, or the fading light had done something to scale, or memory had simply stored him at a size that fit the moment rather than the fact. He came into the yard and stopped just past the threshold and looked at her, and then his eyes moved from her to the yard to the gate and back to her, gathering information the way she gathered it, cataloguing without rushing. He was carrying a satchel over one shoulder, and his hands, when he set the satchel down on a barrel, moved with the particular confidence of someone who worked with them for a living. There was a small knife-cut on his left thumb, the kind you got from carving and not minding where the blade went.

He didn't speak immediately. He stood in the doorway and let the yard settle around him — the sound of it, the smell of it, her in it. Maren had arrived early to do the same thing from the other side. She let him finish.

"Maren," he said.

"Davan." She'd remembered his name the way she remembered the symbol on the disc's edge — completely, without effort, filed under *essential* before she'd had the chance to evaluate whether it was.

He sat on the barrel across from her. The space between them was perhaps four feet — close enough for conversation, far enough for a decision. He didn't rush to fill it with words. She'd spent three years around people who talked to cover silence, and the ones who didn't were the ones she'd learned to pay attention to.

"You got the carving," he said.

"Yes." She took it from her pocket and set it on the barrel between them. The pine figure, head tilted, one arm close. "You carved this before we met."

It wasn't a question. He looked at it and then at her and something worked through his expression — not embarrassment, not pride. He was reckoning with the fact that a thing he'd made had been understood, and the understanding was both welcome and slightly disorienting.

"On the road north," he said. "Weeks before the dock. My hands kept making that shape." He paused. "I tried to fix the arm three times. It came back."

Maren looked at the carving's tucked arm. She did not look at her own.

"Why did it come back?" she said.

"I don't know." He said it plainly, without the performance of mystery. "My hands find things before I do sometimes. They found you."

She sat with this. It should have been unsettling — someone carving her gesture into wood before they'd met, the intimacy of being known by a stranger's hands. It wasn't unsettling. It was the same quality she'd felt at the dock when he'd shown her the symbol in the driftwood — a piece fitting against a piece, the satisfying rightness of a joint cut true.

"You have the symbol," she said. "The inner orbit. You've been carving it."

"For months. Before I knew what it was." He leaned forward slightly, his forearms on his knees. "I've been watching the moons. The geometry matches — the path they take, the relationship between them. The symbol is counting something in orbit."

Maren took the disc from her coat. She held it in her palm for a moment — the weight of it, the warmth, the faint pulse she could feel through her skin like a second heartbeat running at a frequency slightly off from her own. Then she set it on the barrel next to the carving.

Davan looked at it. His hands moved toward it and stopped — not hesitation exactly, more like the pause of someone approaching something they've been moving toward for a long time and wanting to be present for the moment of arrival rather than rushing through it.

"May I?" he said.

She nodded.

He picked it up. He turned it slowly in his fingers, the way he'd turned the driftwood at the dock, with the attention of someone who read objects the way other people read faces. His thumb found the symbol on the edge and traced it, and he went still in the way of someone who has just heard something very clearly in a room they thought was silent.

"It's warm," he said. "Not — not temperature. Something else."

"I know."

He looked up at her. "You feel it too?"

"It pulses." She said it matter-of-factly, the way she said most things that mattered — without drama, without invitation to elaborate, just the fact laid down like a stone in a wall. "It's been doing it more since I got to the city. Stronger. More frequent."

He held the disc for another moment, then set it back on the barrel between them with the care of someone returning something borrowed.

"Tell me what you know," he said.

---

She told him.

Not everything. She told him what Vane had coached her to offer: the disc, the symbol, the fact of being hollow, the fact of being hunted. She told him what the disc was — or what she thought it was, which was a key to something in the eastern hills, a thing her father had known about and gone looking for and not come back from. She told him about the hill. She did not tell him about the letter or her father's name or the specific geography of the route east. She gave him the question, as Vane had said. Not the destination.

He listened completely, without interrupting, in the slow thorough way of someone who needed to walk all the way around a thing before he responded to it. When she mentioned being hollow his expression didn't change, but his hands went still on his knees — not pulling away, not reaching forward. Just still, in the way of someone absorbing something large.

When she finished, he was quiet for a while.

"Four," he said.

She waited.

"The maps in the cooperage — the old ones, with the margin notation. One has three beside the symbol. One has four. The one with four is older." He looked at the disc on the barrel. "Something changed from four to three. The symbol is the same geometry as the moons' orbit. Three moons."

"And four is what it was before," Maren said.

"Yes." He met her eyes. "Something fell. Something that was in orbit came down, and after that the count was three."

The disc pulsed once between them on the barrel. A low warm throb she felt through the wood, and from the way Davan's eyes moved to it she knew he'd felt it too.

"There's more," he said. "There's a group. People who've been working on this — the symbol, the margin notation, the question of what changed. A former archivist, a cartographer, a glassblower, a tanner who has a sealed container made of the same kind of material as —" He stopped. He looked at the disc again. "As that. Or close to it. The same refusal to behave like anything anyone's handled before."

"How long has the group been meeting?"

"Years. Before I arrived." He paused on this — a weight in the pause that she read correctly, the weight of someone acknowledging that he was new to a thing he was describing. "They've been circling the same question from different angles. Whether the symbol is astronomical. Whether the number counts something in the sky. Whether the bloodline's account of what they can and can't do is actually what it claims to be." He turned the carving on the barrel, a half-rotation, a habitual gesture — something his hands did while his mind worked. "There's an argument that keeps coming up. The bloodline says only they can interact with Founding-era objects. Only unchanged hands. They've held that position for as long as anyone can remember, and no one's challenged it."

"Because no one would," Maren said. "Who's going to walk up to the bloodline and say *let me try?*"

"That's exactly the argument. Whether no one's challenged it because it's true, or because the conditions for testing it have never existed." He was quiet for a moment. "I sat in the meeting and listened to them go back and forth about it, and the thing that stayed with me afterward wasn't the argument. It was the gap underneath it. This question that nobody can answer because nobody has ever been in a position to try."

He didn't say the rest. He didn't look at her hands. He didn't connect the gap to the girl sitting across from him who had no Chrysalis and a disc that pulsed when she held it. He let the shape of it sit in the yard between them, and Maren felt its weight — the specific gravity of an implication that both of them could see and neither of them was going to push into a conclusion, because conclusions were where things became irrevocable, and they were still sitting on barrels in a cooperage yard getting to know the shape of what they'd found.

The afternoon was turning. The light in the yard had shifted, the cooperage's shadow lengthening across the packed earth, the barrel hoops on the far wall catching the last direct sun and throwing thin curved shadows like the ribs of something opened up. Inside the cooperage, the mallet-sounds had slowed — the workers finishing for the day, the rhythm of making things winding down into the rhythm of putting things away.

"The hill," she said. "My father went east to find it. The disc opens something there. I don't know what."

"There's a mapmaker — Edvar — who's been working the eastern highlands question for twenty years. He has things I haven't been shown yet. I think there's more he's waiting to share." Davan turned the carving again. "The group meets on Thursdays. I could talk to Edvar. Or Pip — he's the one who arranged this. See if they'd be willing to hear what you have."

"You can't promise that."

"No." He said it without defensiveness. "I've been to one meeting. I'm not in a position to bring anyone in. But what you're carrying —" He looked at the disc. "They've been working around the edges of this for years. You have a piece they don't have. That counts for something, with the right people."

Maren thought about this. The honesty of it — the admission that he couldn't deliver what he hadn't earned, that his position in the group was provisional and new — told her more about him than the carving had. Anyone could make a promise. Fewer people could accurately describe the limits of what they could offer.

"Talk to them," she said. "If they're willing, I'll come."

---

The yard was in shadow by the time they finished. They had talked for the better part of two hours — not all of it the question, not all of it the hill. Some of it was simpler. Where he'd come from, where she'd come from. The road north and what it had cost them both. He told her about Amber Valley, and the Chrysalis Rite, and the light that lived under his skin — he said it plainly, the way you'd describe a weather pattern, something that happened to you that you were still learning to read. She told him about Ashwick, and the coal pouch, and the three years of performing a version of herself that had nearly become the only version. They traded these things the way you traded provisions on the road — not generously, not grudgingly, but with the practicality of two people who understood that shared information was shared weight.

He didn't ask about Vane. She appreciated this. It meant he either knew Vane was there and had decided not to make it a thing, or he didn't know and the not-knowing was beside the point.

She stood. He stood.

"The carving," he said.

She took it from her pocket and held it up. The pine figure, head tilted, one arm close. Her arm.

"I'm keeping it," she said.

Something eased in his face — a tension she hadn't known was there releasing, the way a held breath changes a face and only becomes visible in the letting go. "Good," he said.

He picked up his satchel and slung it over his shoulder, and the movement was easy and unhurried, the way everything about him was unhurried — not slow, not passive, but paced, the rhythm of someone who had learned that the things that mattered arrived in their own time and hurrying them only made them smaller.

He went to the yard door and opened it, and the cooperage's interior was dim behind him, the workers gone, the mallets silent. He turned back once, not to say anything, just to look — the yard and the barrels and the woman with the disc and the late afternoon light.

Then he looked past her. Toward the gate.

Maren turned.

Vane was there. Not through the gate — at it, visible through the slats, her shoulder against the fence post in the posture of someone who had been standing still for a long time and was very good at it. She wasn't looking at Maren. She was looking at Davan.

It was not a threat assessment — Maren knew what those looked like from Vane, the compound nose-and-eyes sweep that filed a person under *categories* in the first three seconds. This was something else. Vane looking at someone and taking the time to look, which was a thing she almost never did, because time spent looking was time not spent calculating, and Vane had built her life on the calculation. But she was looking. At the way he stood in the doorway. At his hands, which were still.

Davan looked back. He held the look the way he held everything — steadily, without force, letting it be what it was. Then the faintest warmth moved under the skin of his wrists, visible in the late light, and he didn't hide it. He let it be visible the way he'd let the carving be visible — here is what I am, do with it what you will.

Vane nodded. Once. The smallest possible movement, an acknowledgment that Maren couldn't fully read — not approval, not dismissal, something more private than either, something that belonged to whatever Vane's nose was telling her about the person standing in the doorway.

Davan returned the nod. Then he turned and went through the cooperage door and was gone.

---

Maren stood in the yard for a moment after he left. The disc was warm in her pocket. The carving was warm in her other pocket. She felt, for the first time since leaving Ashwick, like something had been added rather than subtracted — not safety, not certainty, but *company,* the specific weight of someone else carrying a piece of the same question.

She went to the gate. Vane had already started walking, moving north along the service alley with the unhurried gait of someone who had clocked every exit between here and the room and found them acceptable. Maren fell into step beside her.

They walked half a block in silence.

"Well?" Vane said.

"He's going to talk to his people. See if they'll hear me."

"Not certain, then."

"No. He's new to them. He said so."

Vane was quiet for a few strides. Her hands were in her coat pockets — hiding the tremor, or warming them, or both. The bark on her right arm was visible where her sleeve had ridden up, the dark wood-grain texture climbing past the elbow, the edges angrier than yesterday. She'd stood at that fence for two hours without moving, and the cost of that was probably something Maren would not be told about and would notice anyway.

"That's honest," Vane said. It was not a compliment. It was a filed observation — *he described his position accurately rather than inflating it* — entered into whatever ledger she maintained for people whose behavior she was tracking.

"He has pieces I don't have," Maren said. "A mapmaker with something he calls a face — a flat surface that responds to touch, shows an orbital display. Four shapes around a world. One damaged."

Vane's stride didn't change, but she turned her head slightly — the fraction of movement that meant her nose was working, pulling at the air as if the information itself had a scent.

"Four shapes," she said.

"And the old maps count four. And now there are three."

They walked in silence for a while. The evening was turning cool and the city was settling into its nighttime rhythms and the moons were rising over the eastern roofline, one by one, in their slow patient arcs. Three of them. Maren counted, as she'd been counting since the dock, since the road, since the first night she'd left Ashwick and looked up and seen three lights in the sky and felt something she couldn't name pulling at the center of her chest.

Three moons. And the number used to be four.

"The group argues about whether the bloodline's claim to the old objects is real," Maren said. "Whether only unchanged hands can work them, or whether that's just never been tested."

Vane said nothing for several steps. When she spoke, her voice was level and careful in the way it got when she was managing something that had gotten past the professional register and into the personal one. "That's not an academic question for you."

"No."

"No," Vane agreed. She didn't say anything else about it. They turned the corner onto the river road and the smell of the water came up to meet them, and Maren put her hand in her pocket and felt the carving and the disc side by side, pine and metal, and the disc pulsed once — warm, certain, reaching — and the carving sat beside it, wooden and still and carved in the shape of her own guarded arm by hands that had known her before she'd known them.

She did not look up at the moons.

Not yet.


## Chapter Seventeen — *[Aldric]*
### The Surveyor

---

Greenford was smaller than he'd expected. Not in the way of a village — it had weight, it had infrastructure, the stone bridges over the river spoke of prosperity and civic will — but in the way of a place that had decided what it was long ago and had not been asked to reconsider. The university district sat above the river on a gentle rise, its buildings the same gray-brown stone as the bridges, and the market square below it was quiet in the late afternoon, the stalls closing, the day's commerce folding itself away with the practiced efficiency of a town that did not stay up late.

Aldric dismounted in the square and led his horse to a public trough. His back ached from four days in the saddle — he was not a young man, and the road east from the Confluence had been less forgiving than he remembered, the last stretch through the river valley rutted and muddy from recent rain. He had slept at waypoint inns and eaten what was offered and spoken to no one about anything that mattered, and the discipline of this had cost him less than he'd feared — which troubled him. The lying was becoming easier.

He found Brennan's house on the second street above the square, a narrow-fronted building with a blue door and a window box containing herbs that had not been trimmed in some time. The door was answered before he knocked — Brennan had been watching from the upstairs window, apparently, which was either vigilance or the anxious habit of a man who had been waiting for someone to arrive and could not stop checking whether they had.

---

Brennan was shorter than Aldric had imagined. Twenty years of correspondence produced a mental image, and Aldric's image had been wrong in the specific way that handwriting misled — Brennan's dense leftward script suggested a larger frame, a man who took up space the way his sentences did. The actual Brennan was compact, sharp-featured, with close-cropped gray hair and the quick precise movements of someone who had spent decades in rooms full of fragile things and had learned to navigate without disturbing them.

His hands, when he gripped Aldric's in greeting, were dry and strong and slightly ink-stained at the fingertips. His eyes moved over Aldric's face with the frank assessment of a man who had been reading objects for thirty years and read people the same way — not rudely, but thoroughly, looking for the information the surface carried.

"You look tired," Brennan said.

"I am tired."

"Good. Tired men don't waste time with preamble." He stepped aside and gestured Aldric through. "I'll put water on. Your horse?"

"Trough in the square."

"The ostler two doors down will stable her for a half-mark a night. I'll send the boy." He was already moving through the narrow hallway, talking over his shoulder. "You brought the letter?"

"In my coat."

"Keep it there for now. I have things to show you that came after."

---

The house was what Aldric had expected from a man who studied material culture — which was to say, it was full of materials. Books occupied every horizontal surface and several vertical ones, stacked in arrangements that suggested a filing system comprehensible only to its architect. Maps hung on the walls of the main room — not framed, pinned, with annotations in Brennan's cramped hand trailing off the edges onto the plaster. A long worktable ran beneath the window, and on it lay a sequence of documents arranged in what appeared to be chronological order, each weighted at the corners with small smooth stones. The room smelled of hot wax and old vellum, and beneath that the faint mineral tang of the ink Brennan used — a specific scholar's scent Aldric had not met in years.

He stood in the doorway.

The low persistent unease that had been his companion on the road lifted, for the first time since the Confluence. Recognition. This was the room of a man who had been working on a question for a long time, and the room itself was the visible record of that work — the strata of inquiry, layer on layer, each map and note and weighted document representing a moment when something had been found or tested or discarded. He had a version of this room in his own mind. He had never seen it made physical before.

Brennan set a kettle on the stove and came back to the worktable and stood beside it with his hands flat on the surface — the way Aldric himself stood before the difficult. Grounding in the physical first.

"Sit down," Brennan said. "This will take the evening."

---

The twelve maps were arranged in two rows of six. Brennan had acquired them over a span of fifteen years, from sources he described with the vagueness of a man who had cultivated relationships that depended on discretion. Some were copies — hand-traced from originals he'd been permitted to examine under conditions that precluded removal. Others were originals, purchased or traded for or, in one case, rescued from a condemned university storage room three days before its contents were burned as a fire precaution.

All twelve showed the same thing.

"The notation appears in the margins of maps drawn from above," Brennan said. He was standing at the head of the table, pointing without touching, the scholar's instinct to preserve. "Not every such map — roughly one in four, from what I've been able to survey. But when it appears, it's always the same symbol, always in the margin, and always accompanied by a number."

Aldric leaned forward. The symbol was the inner orbit — the geometry he had been looking at for months, on the temple doorway, on the face in the sealed room, in the patterns his own thinking had begun to trace. In each map's margin it was rendered slightly differently — different hands, different implements, different degrees of haste — but the form was consistent. A path around a center. Something in motion around something fixed.

"The numbers," Aldric said.

"Three or four. In the oldest maps, four. In the most recent, three." Brennan moved along the table, touching the edge of each map's protective cover. "The transition happens here." He indicated a map roughly two-thirds of the way along the sequence. "This is the oldest map I have that shows three. The dating is contested — I'd put it at roughly nine thousand years, give or take a margin that would make a rigorous scholar uncomfortable. The maps showing four are all older than this. Every map showing three is younger."

Aldric looked at the transition point. A map drawn on something that was not quite paper — thicker, with a faint translucence, the material itself old in a way that paper did not get old. The symbol in its margin was drawn in a steadier hand than most, the number beside it a clear and careful three.

"Something changed," Aldric said.

"Something changed." Brennan looked at him. "In the sky. Between the date of the last four and the date of the first three. Something that was being counted went from four to three, and everyone who drew these maps afterward recorded the new number."

Aldric thought about the face in the undercroft. The blue-green world. The four shapes in orbit. One irregular. Damaged.

He did not say any of this. He looked at the maps and he thought it, and Brennan, watching him think it, let him arrive on his own.

"You've verified the dating?" Aldric said.

"As far as I'm able. The material composition is consistent within groups — the older maps use a substrate that matches Founding-era artifacts, the same gray material as the sealed containers. The younger ones are conventional paper or vellum. There's no clean way to assign absolute dates, but the relative sequence is solid. Four comes before three. Something that was counted went down by one."

Aldric reached toward the oldest map and stopped. "May I?"

"Carefully."

He lifted the protective cover. The map beneath was drawn on the gray material — smooth, cool, with the particular density of something manufactured by a process that no longer existed. The coastline it described was recognizably Gaius, but from an angle he had never seen applied to a map before: the full shape of the continent, rendered as if the cartographer had been standing very far above and looking down. Features he knew as local — the river delta at New Plymouth, the ridge country east of the Confluence — appeared as small details in a larger form, the way you might recognize a particular stone in a wall while seeing the entire wall for the first time.

In the lower margin, the symbol. Beside it, in a hand that was neither hurried nor decorative: four.

He set the cover back.

---

They ate a simple meal at the cleared end of the worktable — bread, hard cheese, a preserved fish that Brennan apologized for and then ate most of. Brennan talked as he ate, the way scholars talked, without clear boundaries between the professional and the personal, the question and the life organized around the question.

He had come to Greenford thirty years ago and stayed because the university's collection of pre-Chrysalis materials was better than anyone outside the institution understood. "It's a backwater archive," he said, "which is the best kind of archive, because it means the people who manage what gets remembered and what gets forgotten haven't paid attention to it in centuries." The first map with the notation had come to him twelve years ago. He had spent six years thinking he was wrong about what it meant, and four more becoming certain he wasn't. "Then I wrote to you."

"Because of who's watching," Aldric said.

Brennan set down his bread. His expression shifted — the scholarly animation folding back, the quicker wariness underneath showing through. "Yes."

"Your letter said someone with bloodline connections."

"Someone with access to my schedule, my correspondence log at the university office, and my borrowing records at three separate archives. That's not a curious colleague. That's a structured inquiry." He picked a crumb off the table, the small fastidious gesture of a man who did not like disorder. "I noticed it six months ago. It's possible it began before that and I simply wasn't watching for it."

Aldric thought about Crane. The pencil on the wrong side of the journal.

"I'm being watched as well," he said.

He told Brennan about Crane — not everything, not the sealed room or the face, but the review, the architectural questions, the pointed absence of certain other questions, the pencil. Brennan listened the way Aldric had expected him to listen: completely, without interruption, his hands still on the table, the full attention of a man accustomed to reading the evidence before forming the interpretation.

"The pencil could be nothing," Brennan said.

"Yes."

"But the review's timing and the scope of its mandate are not nothing."

"No."

Brennan was quiet for a moment. He looked at the maps on his worktable — the patient sequence of twelve perspectives on the same count, the oldest recording four, the youngest three, the transition between them marking a change that had been visible from above for nearly ten thousand years and invisible from below for exactly as long.

"I should tell you about the surveyor," he said.

---

His name was Reth. Brennan said it with particular care, the way you handled a name that had been entrusted to you under conditions. Reth had been a surveyor for the eastern territorial commission — government work, methodical, well-compensated. He had walked the eastern highlands mapping terrain for road planning and mineral assessment. He had done this for fifteen years and retired early and had not spoken about why.

Brennan had found him through the university's geological records — Reth's survey data had been filed, as was required, and Brennan had been reviewing highland topography in connection with the margin notation's implications when he'd noticed an anomaly in one of Reth's terrain reports. A formation described in language that was technically correct and emotionally absent in a way that drew attention to itself, the way a hole in a wall draws attention by the careful plaster around its edges.

"He described a ridge formation east of the highland plateau as 'an exposed mineral deposit of uncertain composition, approximately four hundred meters in lateral extent, partially obscured by vegetation and sediment accumulation,'" Brennan said. He was quoting from memory, the exact language clear in his voice. "There is nothing wrong with this description. Every word is accurate. And it tells you absolutely nothing about what he actually found."

"You contacted him."

"I wrote. He didn't respond. I wrote again. He didn't respond. I went to his house — he lives in the north end of the city, a quiet street, keeps to himself. He opened the door and looked at me the way you look at someone who has found the thing you've been pretending doesn't exist." Brennan paused. "He said no. He said he'd filed his report and the report was the end of it. I asked him why a mineral deposit required such careful language. He told me to leave."

"But he's agreed to see me."

"He has." Brennan leaned back. "When I told him a Keeper from the Confluence had traveled to Greenford to ask about what he'd found in the eastern highlands, something changed in his expression. Not softening — Reth doesn't soften. But the refusal shifted. He said: 'a Keeper?' And I said yes. And he was quiet for a while and then he said: 'Bring him. Not you. Him.'"

"Why?"

"Because you're an institution. To Reth, that means something. He filed his report with an institution — the territorial commission — and the commission filed it and forgot it. That was the agreement he made with himself: he'd done his duty, he'd reported what he'd found, and the rest was someone else's responsibility. But no one ever came. Forty years and no one followed up, no one asked, no one knocked on his door until I did, and I'm a scholar, which is the wrong kind of authority." Brennan looked at Aldric. "You're a Keeper. That's the right kind. He wants to tell someone who has the weight to do something with what he tells them."

Aldric considered this. The weight of it, the specific expectation being placed on a title he had carried for decades and was only now discovering the true dimensions of.

"When?" he said.

"Tomorrow morning. His house, not mine. He won't come here — too many books, he said, and I think he meant it literally. He doesn't trust rooms full of questions. He trusts rooms where people live."

---

Reth's house was on the north end, as Brennan had said — a stone cottage with a well-kept yard and a gate that opened silently on oiled hinges, the small signal of a man who maintained what was his. Aldric went alone. Brennan stood at the corner of the street and watched him walk the rest of the way, as agreed — present but not participating, a condition Reth had specified.

The door opened before he reached it. Reth was old — older than Aldric had pictured from Brennan's description, though it was hard to say by how much. His face had the weathered compression of a man who had spent decades outdoors reading terrain, the skin tightened over the bones by sun and wind into something like a geological feature itself. His hands were thick, scarred across the knuckles, resting at his sides with the stillness of someone who had learned to stand in one place for a long time and not mind it. His mutation was subtle — a faint iridescence to his skin in the morning light, the kind of change you might not notice indoors.

He looked at Aldric for a long time without speaking. Not the way Brennan read — for information and implication — but the way a surveyor read terrain, for what the surface told you about what was underneath.

"Keeper Voss," Reth said.

"Surveyor Reth."

Reth inclined his head, a small formal motion. "Nobody's called me that in twenty years." He stepped aside. "Come in. I'll say what I have to say and you'll leave and that will be the end of my part in it."

---

The house was sparse and orderly — a man living alone, keeping only what he needed, every object in its place with the discipline of someone who had spent professional years working from organized field kits. The air was clean and stone-cold, nothing domestic in it: no baking, no tallow, no sign that anyone ate here more than they needed to. A single lamp burned on the table, turned low. Aldric's fingers found each other before he could stop them — the pinch, the habitual reach for a flame that wasn't his to manage — and he pressed them flat against his thigh instead.

Reth sat across the table and placed his hands on the surface, palms down, and began.

"I walked the eastern highlands for the territorial commission. Forty-one years ago. The commission was mapping potential road routes through the highland plateau — trade access to the eastern marches, a thing that gets proposed every generation and never built. I was the third surveyor assigned to the eastern section, which included the foothills below the plateau and the broken country east of the tree line."

He spoke without inflection, the measured cadence of a report being delivered to an authority. This was, Aldric understood, what Reth had been waiting forty years to do: give his report to someone who would receive it.

"The terrain east of the tree line is rough. Erosion gullies, exposed rock, heavy scrub vegetation. Not difficult to walk but difficult to map accurately — the surface is deceptive. What looks like a ridge from one angle resolves into a different formation from another. I spent three weeks in that section, working methodically, marking stations, taking measurements."

He paused. His hands on the table did not move.

"On the fifteenth day I reached a formation I could not account for. The maps listed it as a ridge — an exposed sandstone ridge running roughly northwest to southeast, four hundred meters lateral extent, partially buried. The previous survey, which was sixty years old and done by a man who apparently did not walk the eastern face, had marked it as geology and moved on."

"It was not geology," Aldric said.

"No." Reth looked at him. "I'm a surveyor. I know what rock does. I know what sediment does. I know how weather shapes a formation over time, what the grain looks like, what the fracture patterns tell you. This formation had no grain. It had no fracture pattern. The surface, where the vegetation had been pulled away by wind or animal activity, was smooth. Not worn smooth. Manufactured smooth. A continuous surface without joint or seam for fifty meters before the sediment covered it again."

He stopped. The lamp on the table flickered — a draft from the window, the flame leaning and recovering. Aldric watched it and did not reach for it.

"I went back at dusk," Reth said. "Because I wanted to see how the light caught it. Surveyors do this — you check a formation at different times of day to understand the surface. At dusk the low light hit the exposed section at a raking angle and the surface —"

He stopped again. This time the stop was different. Not the pause of a man organizing his account but the arrest of someone running against the edge of what language could do with what he'd seen.

"It caught the light," he said. "Not the way rock catches light. The way metal catches it. A dull reflectance, uniform across the entire exposed section, the color of storm-cloud with something underneath it. And at certain angles — I moved along the formation, watching — at certain angles the light seemed to go into the surface rather than bouncing off it. As if the material were partially translucent. As if there were depth to it."

He looked at Aldric.

"Four hundred meters. Partially buried. Metallic. No geological explanation. No seam, no joint, no grain. Continuous manufactured surface running under centuries of sediment and root systems and soil. I stood there at dusk and I looked at it and I understood that I was looking at something that was not from the landscape. Something that had arrived in the landscape and the landscape had grown over it and nobody had noticed, or nobody had said."

The room was very quiet.

"I went back to my camp," Reth said. "I wrote my report. I used the language I used. I filed the report with the commission and I waited, and nobody said anything, and nobody came to ask, and I completed the rest of the survey and retired the following year and I have not gone back to the eastern highlands since."

"Why not?" Aldric asked.

Reth looked at him with an expression Aldric recognized because he had been wearing a version of it for two months — the expression of a man who had found something too large for the container he had available and had chosen to seal the container rather than find a larger one.

"Because I was a surveyor," Reth said. "I measured land. I filed reports. That was the scope of what I was. And what I found —" He spread his hands slightly, the first gesture he'd made. "What I found was not land. It was not in the scope. I did my duty. I filed my report. If someone with the authority to act on it had come, I would have told them what I'm telling you. Nobody came."

"I'm here now," Aldric said.

Reth studied him for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, with the finality of a man setting something down that he had been carrying for a very long time.

"Yes," he said. "You are."

---

He gave Aldric directions. Not a map — he would not draw one, would not create a document. He gave them verbally, from memory, with the precision of a man who had walked the terrain and retained every station marker and bearing in his head for four decades. East from New Plymouth along the highland road. The turn at the third river crossing. The climb through the tree line. The bearing from the lightning-split cairn that marked the old commission station. Eleven miles northeast of that, across two gully systems and through the heavy scrub, to the exposed section of the formation.

"The vegetation will be heavier now," Reth said. "Forty years of growth. But the surface is there. Whatever it's made of, it doesn't corrode and it doesn't break down. The plants grow over it but they don't grow into it. You'll see the edge where the scrub stops — a line, too clean, where nothing has taken root. That's where the surface starts."

Aldric committed the directions to memory. He did not write them down. Reth watched him not writing them down and appeared to approve.

"One more thing," Reth said. He had risen from his chair, the interview concluded, his body already orienting toward the door and the end of his obligation. But he stopped.

"At the southeastern extent of the formation, where the surface curves downward into the hillside — the thing goes into the ground, Keeper, it doesn't just sit on it — there is a section where the sediment has been cleared. Not by weather. By hands. Someone went there, not recently, but within the last generation. They cleared the sediment from a section of the curved surface and they left it cleared, and when I found it there were marks in the exposed surface. Not scratches. Depressions. Recesses, shaped with a precision that suggested they were part of the original construction. Small. About the size of a —"

He held up his hand. Thumb and forefinger, a circle. The size of a coin. The size of a disc.

Thirty years of pastoral training held his breath in and held his face still. Beneath that training: the word *wreckage* arriving in a place where he had always used *providence*. A lintel of doctrine cracking. He did not let it show. He did not move his hands from where they rested on the table.

"— and in one of those recesses," Reth said, "there was a mark. A symbol. Pressed into the surface or part of the surface, I couldn't tell which."

He didn't describe the symbol. He didn't need to. The shape of the silence between them was description enough, and Aldric knew — with the certainty of a man who had been counting to four for months and had just heard the count confirmed from a direction he hadn't anticipated — exactly what Reth had seen in the curved metal surface of something four hundred meters long that had come down from the sky ten thousand years ago and been waiting, under sediment and silence, for someone to come back.

"Thank you," Aldric said.

Reth walked him to the door. At the threshold he stopped and looked at Aldric with an expression that was neither warm nor cold — the expression of a man handing over a weight he had measured exactly, knowing what it would cost the person who took it.

"I did my part," Reth said. "Forty years ago. This is yours now."

He closed the door.

---

Brennan was waiting at the corner. He read Aldric's face the way he read everything — thoroughly, quickly, with the controlled urgency of a man who had been asking the question long enough to know when an answer had arrived.

"Well?" he said.

"It's real," Aldric said. "It's there."

They walked back to Brennan's house in silence. The morning had turned bright, the kind of autumn clarity that sharpened every edge and made distances look closer than they were. Students moved through the university district in loose groups, carrying books, talking about things that mattered to them with the pleasant urgency of people who had not yet discovered that some questions were dangerous.

At the house, Brennan closed the door and went to the worktable and stood there with his hands on the maps.

"What are you going to do?" he said.

"Go to New Plymouth. Then east."

"Alone?"

Aldric considered this. He considered the face in the undercroft at the Confluence, showing him four shapes around a blue-green world. Twelve maps counting down from four to three. A surveyor who had carried his report for forty years because no one had come to receive it. A recess in a metal surface, shaped like a coin, bearing a symbol he had been tracing in his mind for months.

And the phrase from Brennan's letter, carried west now without yet being fully legible: *for unchanged hands.*

"I don't know yet," he said. It was the most honest thing he'd said in weeks, and it arrived with the specific relief of honesty after a sustained period without it.

Brennan nodded. He did not push. He was a man who understood the value of letting an answer develop in its own time, and Aldric was grateful for this — for the space, for the patience, for the twenty years of correspondence that had produced, at the end of it, the simple ability to stand in a room together and not require more of each other than what was offered.

"Stay tonight," Brennan said. "Leave tomorrow. You'll want the rest."

"I'll want the rest," Aldric agreed.

---

He left Greenford the next morning, west toward New Plymouth, with the surveyor's directions held in his memory like a set of coordinates for something he had not yet decided to find. The road followed the river valley, wider here than the Confluence road, the landscape opening toward the lowlands and the distant suggestion of the coast. His horse moved at an easy pace and he let it, not hurrying. The autumn air was cold in his collar; the road dust rose under the hooves and settled; the saddle shifted with the horse's long strides beneath him, and he let his body take the rhythm.

He thought about Reth's hands on the table, palms down, delivering his report to the institution he'd been waiting for. The specific patience of a man who had filed his account and then waited forty years in the silence that followed, and what that patience had cost, and what it meant that the silence had not been an oversight but a system functioning as designed — the report filed, the report archived, the report never followed up, the question never asked, the answer never required.

He thought about his own institution and its own silences. The sealed room. The Restricted Collection. The careful management of what was known and what was permitted to be known, practiced for so long it had become invisible, the way a wall becomes invisible when you've walked past it every day for thirty years. *Providence*, the word his order used for what the faithful could not see into. He had used it a thousand times in sermons. It meant, in practice, *do not ask.*

The lamp tic found him twice on the road — once at a waypoint where he pinched a candle stub beside the register, once reaching for a lantern at the stable where he changed horses. Both times he caught himself, his thumb and forefinger closing on nothing or on flames that were not his, and both times he let go with the deliberate effort of a man unlearning a habit that had outlived its purpose. The habit was not about lamps. It had never been about lamps. It was about control — the small reliable control of a manageable thing, practiced in place of the larger, less manageable things that were beyond his reach. He understood this now in a way he had not understood it at the Confluence, and the understanding made the habit harder to perform rather than easier to break, which was the particular cruelty of self-knowledge in the presence of an entrenched behavior.

He was the next piece. The thought rode with him as the road straightened and the afternoon light turned long and golden. He had been counting to four for months and a stranger had just counted to four with him from the other side of the country, and the count was no longer his alone to keep.

The moons were visible in the late afternoon sky, pale against the blue, three of them at different heights in their slow orbits. He looked at them the way Reth must have looked at the formation in the highlands — with the knowledge that what he was seeing was not what everyone else saw, that the familiar shapes carried an unfamiliar weight, and that three was a number that described a loss.


## Chapter Eighteen — *Vane*
### The Bloom-Giver

---

The tin was light. She knew without opening it. Had known for
three days, the way you know a room's dimensions before the
lamp catches up — by the quality of the echo when nothing
comes back.

Vane sat on the edge of the narrow bed and held the tin in her
lap. Maren was out. Market run, she'd said, which meant
walking a pattern through the stalls near the fishmonger's
quarter where foot traffic was thick enough to dissolve a
silhouette. Good fieldcraft for someone who'd learned it from
watching rather than training. Maren picked things up. That
was the problem with her, or one of them.

The room was small and smelled of tallow and the particular
warmth Vane had stopped pretending she didn't track. Maren's
warmth. It threaded through everything now — the blanket on
the second bed, the chair back, the doorframe where she leaned
when she was thinking. Vane used it the way she used any
reliable signal. Orientation. Proximity. Threat absence.

She turned the tin over once and set it on the mattress.

The bark-skin on her right arm itched. Above the elbow now,
upper arm nearly to the shoulder, the grain creeping toward
the joint where the change would stop feeling like a sleeve
and start feeling like a shirt. She rolled back her sleeve and
looked at it in the grey light from the window — dark grain,
tight whorls, the edges angrier than last week. Redder. The
border where skin became something else was precise as a tide
line and about as negotiable. Under the grain she could feel
the arm, still: the muscle, the tendon, the pulse. Not gone.
Just wearing something she had not chosen. A tightness when
she flexed, as if a thin glove of bark had been drawn up and
stitched to her from the inside.

She rolled the sleeve down.

The contact's name was Deren. Older than Cade. Deeper current.
A name she'd picked up in Port Aelith from a woman who sold
information about information, which was the only kind of
broker Vane respected. He operated out of the docks district,
southside, where the warehouses backed up against the canal
retaining walls and the air tasted of brine and rotting hemp.

She'd sent word yesterday. The reply came back on a folded
slip tucked into the frame of the rooming house door: an
address, a time, and the figure. The figure was high. She
could meet it.

Vane checked the tin one more time with a brief precise motion,
thumb across the clasp. Confirmed what she already knew. Two
wafers left, maybe. Or one. Or the dust of one.

She pocketed it, pulled on her coat, and went out.

---

The docks district in late afternoon was a study in practiced
disinterest. Longshoremen finishing second shift. Cart traffic
thinning as the chandlers closed up. Women with baskets moving
toward home with the focused stride of people who'd stopped
finding the city interesting years ago. Vane matched their
pace. Coat collar up, hands in pockets, right hand curled
around the tin because the tremor was worse in open air and
she didn't need to advertise it.

The address was on Lintel Street, which ran parallel to the
canal before ducking under a stone footbridge and becoming
something narrower and less named. The buildings here were
old — pre-colonial stonework, the kind the city had paved over
and built on top of and then grudgingly preserved when the
foundations turned out to be the only thing keeping the canal
bank from sliding. Vane counted doorways. Third from the
bridge. Iron-banded. No marking.

She knocked the pattern from the slip.

The door opened inward. The man behind it was short, heavy
through the shoulders, with the flat affect of someone whose
job was to be a surface between the street and whatever lay
behind him. He looked at her hands. She showed them, palms
up, and he stepped aside.

Stone stairs going down. Of course. The canal district was
built on top of the old quarter, and the old quarter was built
on top of what came before that, and at some point you stopped
calling it a basement and started calling it what it was. Vane
went down.

The smell hit her on the seventh step.

Copper-flower. Bloom. But not the refined note she knew from
the wafers — not the compressed, preserved version that sat
on the tongue like a petal pressed in a book. This was the
smell at volume. At source. The way a tannery smells nothing
like a leather glove. It filled the stairwell like a
temperature, warm and thick and unmistakably biological.

Her hands went flat against the walls. Both of them. The
tremor had stopped, which it did sometimes when her body
decided to be afraid before she gave it permission.

She kept going down.

The corridor at the bottom was stone, low-ceilinged, lit by
two oil lamps mounted in iron brackets. She knew this
corridor. She'd been seeing it for months — in the visions
that came with the Bloom episodes, the involuntary ones, the
flashes she'd catalogued and cross-referenced and filed
without ever asking the obvious question about where the
images were coming from. This was the corridor. The lamps were
in the right places. The ceiling was exactly as low as she'd
seen it, close enough to brush her hair if she stood straight.

A door at the end, ajar. Behind it, a room.

Vane stopped walking.

She could hear someone breathing. Not Deren — Deren would be
upstairs or elsewhere, the way suppliers were always elsewhere
when the product was being shown. The breathing was slow and
thick and had a quality to it she'd heard once before. In
Cade's back room in Harrowgate. Through a wall. *If she
survives the first month.*

She pushed the door open.

---

The room was eight paces by six. She measured it because
measuring was what she did when she couldn't do anything else.
Stone walls, stone floor, stone ceiling at the height she
already knew. A pallet against the far wall. A drain in the
floor. A table with collection apparatus — glass, copper
tubing, clay vessels sealed with wax. The lamps in here were
turned low.

The person on the pallet was hollow.

Vane knew this the way she'd learned to know it over the past
months — by the quality of the skin, by the particular
translucency at the temples and inner wrists, by the faint
luminescence that hollows carried like a watermark. This
person's luminescence was dim. Banked. There were puncture
marks at the inside of each elbow and at the hollow of the
throat, the skin around each one angry and faintly raised,
the way skin went when the body was fighting something that
had been put into it and would not leave. Sweat beaded at
the hairline despite the stone cold. A strand of dark hair
had come loose from where someone had tied it back and lay
across her cheek. The features under it were ordinary:
young, with a narrow nose and a small white scar at the
corner of the mouth, the kind you got as a child from
falling wrong onto a step. Vane looked. She made herself
look.

*Junie,* the thought arrived, unbidden — not a name she knew
this woman by, not a name at all, just the kind of name a
farmhand girl might have had before someone took her
somewhere.

Female. Young, or had been. Breathing the slow thick breath of
induced sleep. Restraints at the wrists, padded with cloth
that had been changed recently — Deren or whoever ran this
kept things clean, which was worse, somehow, than filth would
have been. The copper-flower smell was overwhelming here. It
came from the skin. From the pores. The secretions that
hollows produced during sleep, the ones Vane had spent years
consuming in pressed wafer form with a cup of tea in the
morning like it was a habit and not a transaction.

She sorted. The operational part of her brain did what it
always did — inputs, outputs, cost structure, product
architecture — because that was the only way to stand in this
room and keep standing. The collection apparatus was designed
for continuous harvest. Condensation trays positioned along
the body. Copper tubing running to a settling vessel. The
wax-sealed clay pots on the table were the intermediate
product — raw secretion before pressing and refinement. The
wafers in her tin had started in a room like this. On a body
like this.

She had known this.

Not the address. Not the face. But the fact. She'd known since
Harrowgate, since Cade's back room, since the phrase that had
lodged in her like a splinter — *if she survives the first
month* — and she'd kept buying. She'd known since the Bloom
episodes had started showing her this exact ceiling, this
exact quality of lamplight, this exact breathing, and she'd
catalogued the visions and cross-referenced them and never
once followed the thread to its source because following the
thread would have required standing where she was standing now.

The woman's chest rose and fell. The condensation trays
caught the light. Her fingers twitched once in sleep and
were still.

Vane turned around and walked back up the stairs.

---

The man at the door looked at her as she came up. His face did
the thing faces do when a transaction hasn't been completed —
the slight recalibration, the question that isn't asked because
asking would be unprofessional.

"Changed my mind," Vane said.

He opened the door. She walked through it.

Lintel Street was exactly as she'd left it. The canal smelled
like a canal. Cart wheels on cobblestone. A woman with a
basket of dried cod walked past without looking at her. The
footbridge threw a shadow across the road that moved with the
late sun.

Vane crossed the street and stood against the wall of the
opposite building. The stonework was cold through her coat.
She put her hands in her pockets and found the tin and held it
and stayed.

The tremor came back. Fine, at the fingertips, the way it
always started. The bark-skin on her right arm pulsed once,
a slow deep itch that she'd stopped scratching weeks ago
because scratching didn't help and because the texture under
her nails made her think of things she didn't need to think
about.

The street changed around her the way streets do. Afternoon
foot traffic thinned. A lamplighter came through, then
doubled back, then came through again on the other side. The
shadow of the footbridge migrated across the cobblestones and
climbed the wall to her left. Vane tracked all of it — exits,
sightlines, density patterns — because the operational part
of her brain didn't stop working just because the rest of her
had. The rest of her was in the room. The rest of her was
standing in the doorway looking at the condensation trays.

A cost-benefit analysis. She was good at those.

Cost of continued use: the body on the pallet. Not
abstractly — she'd had the abstraction for months and it
hadn't stopped her. Concretely. That specific ceiling. That
specific breathing. The cloth on the restraints changed by
someone who kept things clean. The small white scar at the
corner of the mouth.

Benefit of continued use: the tracking sense. The ability to
find Maren, to read hollow proximity, to do the job she'd
assigned herself.

Cost of stopping: the episodes. The shaking. The loss of the
tracking sense, probably, though she didn't know the timeline.
Operational degradation at the worst possible moment, with
Horst in the city and Maren depending on her ability to stay
functional. The bark-skin she didn't know about. Nobody knew
what long-term Bloom use did, because long-term Bloom users
either stopped or stopped being something you could ask.

She held the tin in her pocket and felt its weight, which was
almost nothing.

*If she survives the first month.*

Vane pushed off the wall and walked.

---

She took the long way back. Not for countersurveillance,
though she ran the pattern anyway — three turns, a doubled
block, a pause at a vendor's stall she didn't see. The long
way back was for the same reason she'd stood against the wall:
she needed to finish the calculation before she arrived, because
she wouldn't do it in front of Maren, and she wouldn't do it
after, and if she didn't do it now it wouldn't get done.

The calculation was simple. She'd made it complicated for
months with operational justifications and tactical necessity
and the professional categories she used to keep things sorted,
but it was simple. She was consuming a substance made from a
captive person's body to track another person she was supposed
to be protecting. The tracking target and the production source
were the same kind. Hollow. Maren's kind. And Vane had been
using one to find the other and calling it fieldwork.

She crossed through the market square. The stalls were closing.
A fishmonger was packing ice around the unsold catch and the
smell of it cut through everything for a moment — salt and
cold and clean — and then the copper-flower was back, ghost
of it, memory of it, she couldn't tell. It didn't matter. She
could still taste the last wafer she'd taken, three days ago,
on the back of her tongue. She would probably taste it for a
while.

The rooming house was on a quiet street near the old seminary.
Vane let herself in, climbed the stairs, and stood outside the
door for a count of ten. Not composing herself. There was
nothing to compose. She was standing in the place where a
decision had been made and checking whether it was still there.

It was.

She went in.

---

Maren was at the small table by the window, mending a tear in
her sleeve with a needle and thread she'd bought at the
market. She looked up when Vane came in. Read something. Went
back to the needle for a moment, then set it down.

"You went to the docks."

Not a question. Maren had a way of stating things that made
them available for correction without requiring it. Vane had
noted this too, the way she noted other reliable signals.

"I went to a Bloom contact." Vane closed the door. Hung her
coat. Sat on the edge of her bed, which put her across from
Maren with the width of the room between them. "Different from
Cade's network. Older supplier. The address was a harvest
site."

She said it flat because flat was how it came out. Maren's
hands went still on the table.

"The substance is made from hollows." Vane's voice was level,
the way it got when she was delivering a field report that
contained information no one was going to enjoy. "Captive
hollows. The secretions produced during induced sleep.
Collected, refined, pressed into wafers. I've known the
general mechanism for months. Today I saw the specific
production."

Maren didn't speak. Her eyes were steady on Vane's face with
that quality of attention that was itself a kind of silence —
active, receiving, not filling the space.

"One person," Vane said. "In a stone room under the canal
district. Female. Young. Restrained. Sedated. Needle marks
at both arms and the throat. They put something into her and
collect what her body makes fighting it." She paused.
Breathed once. "The room matched the visions I've been having
during episodes. I've been seeing the source for months and
cataloguing it as sensory noise."

Maren's expression didn't change, which meant it had changed
and she was keeping it still. Vane could read that now. She
could read a lot of things about Maren now, and some of that
was the Bloom, and she didn't want to think about what it meant
that the tool she'd used to read Maren was made from someone
like Maren.

"I didn't buy any," Vane said. "I didn't free the person. I
stood in the street for —" She stopped. Checked. "A while."

"What did you decide?"

Maren's voice was even. Not gentle — Maren had the sense not
to be gentle right now, which was one of the things about her
that Vane had stopped trying to file under a professional
heading.

"I'm stopping."

Two words. They sat in the room like stones dropped on a table.

"The tracking sense may degrade. I don't know the timeline.
The tremor will get worse before it gets better. The episodes
will intensify. I'll be less operational for a period I can't
estimate. This is a poor time for it, with Horst in the
Merchant Quarter, and I'm doing it anyway."

She laid it out the way she would have laid out a logistics
problem — sequenced, with known variables and acknowledged
unknowns. Maren watched her lay it out and didn't interrupt.

"What's in your pack," Maren said. Still not a question.

Vane looked at her. "The tin. Two wafers left, maybe less."

"And you're keeping it."

"For now."

This was the part that could have become something else.
Maren could have reached for her hand. Could have said
something about courage or the right choice or any of the
phrases that people used to make decisions feel like they'd
already been completed just because they'd been spoken aloud.
She didn't. She picked up the needle, looked at it, set it
down again.

"All right," Maren said.

"There's a gap," Vane said, and then stopped because she
didn't usually explain herself and didn't know why she was
starting now. But Maren was watching with that quality of
attention, and the words came out anyway. "Between deciding
and executing. I've decided. The tin is still in my pack. I
haven't destroyed it. I'm going to sit with that for —" She
turned her right hand over on her knee and looked at the
bark-skin where it vanished under her sleeve. "Until I don't
need to anymore."

Maren nodded once.

The room was quiet. Tallow candle, thread on the table, the
two narrow beds, the window with its view of the seminary wall.
Vane's pack sat against the wall by the door with the tin
inside it, weighing almost nothing, containing almost nothing,
representing a supply chain that ended in a stone room with a
low ceiling and a woman who breathed the slow thick breath of
someone kept asleep so that what her body produced could be
scraped off and sold to people like Vane.

The tremor was back in her fingertips. She pressed her hands
flat on her knees and held them there.

Maren went back to the mending. The needle moved through the
cloth in small precise stitches, and the sound it made was the
sound of thread pulled through fabric, which was a quiet sound,
and it was enough.

---


## Chapter Nineteen — *[Davan]*
### What Edvar Keeps

---

The shop smelled of linseed and old paper — not the sharp chemical bite of fresh oil but the settled, sweet undertone of decades of it soaked into wood and plaster. The walls themselves had absorbed the work done here. Davan thought of tree rings, how a trunk held its years in layers you could only see once it was cut.

Edvar let him in without speaking. That was new. The first three visits had each begun with some small exchange at the threshold — a comment about the weather, or the state of the harbor road, or once a dry observation about the quality of Davan's boots. Tonight the mapmaker simply stepped aside and gestured him through.

The front room was as Davan remembered: the broad drafting table under the north-facing window, the racks of pigment jars along the far wall sorted by hue, the rolled charts in their vertical cubbies like a library built sideways. Tools hung from pegs in an order that was not alphabetical or chronological but made sense to the hand that reached for them — compass, ruling pen, divider, stylus, each within the arc of a seated man's arm. A workspace shaped by forty years of the same motions.

But Edvar walked past the drafting table. Past the pigment racks. He went to the back wall, where a curtain separated the shop from his living quarters, and held it aside.

Davan had not been past the curtain before.

"Boots are fine," Edvar said, reading the hesitation. "Floor's just stone."

The back room was smaller than Davan expected. A cot against one wall, made up with the tight corners of a man who lived alone and saw no reason to be loose about it. A washstand. A shelf of books — not many, but handled often, their spines softened. A single chair near a cold hearth. The room of someone who spent most of his life in the front half of the building and came here only to sleep.

There was a trunk at the foot of the cot. Iron-banded, the wood dark with age, a lock that Edvar opened with a key he wore on a cord inside his shirt. Davan watched him kneel — slowly, one knee at a time, the way a man in his sixties negotiated with a floor — and lift the lid.

Inside, nested in a wrapping of oiled canvas, was something flat and dark.

Edvar lifted it out with both hands. He set it on the cot and folded the canvas back, and the object was roughly the size of a serving tray — rectangular, perhaps two handspans by three, and thin. The surface was dark. Not black, exactly. Not the black of ink or iron or char. A dark that seemed to go down into the material rather than sit on top of it. Light from the single lamp touched it and did not come back.
