Davan's skin pulsed once, low amber, at the wrists. He was aware of it and did not try to suppress it. He had learned weeks ago that suppressing made it worse.

"Twenty years," Edvar said. He was still kneeling beside the cot, looking at the face with an expression Davan could not immediately read. Then he could: it was the look of a man opening a door he had kept locked, unsure whether the air on the other side would still be breathable. "I found it in a building collapse on the eastern ridge. The old quarter — before the fire took most of it. There was a cellar that had been sealed. Stones had come down and broken the floor open, and it was in the rubble."

He did not say *I knew what it was.* He did not say *I didn't know what it was.* The omission sat between those two statements like a third option: *I knew it was something.*

"You can touch it," Edvar said.

Davan knelt beside him. The stone floor was cold through his trousers. Something registered — not cold, not warm, but present. The same quality as Corvel's container at the cooperage meeting, the proximity-sense that was not temperature but registered in the same place temperature did. Something in the face was aware of the space around it, or held a residue of awareness, the way Edvar's front room held the smell of linseed.

He reached out and put his fingertips on the surface.

---

The dark lit.

Not all at once. It began at the point of contact — a pale light spreading from where his fingers pressed, cold and precise, nothing like lamplight. Lamplight was warm and uneven and alive. This was something else. This was light that had been made, designed, given exact properties and held to them. It spread across the surface in a wash that took perhaps three seconds, and then the entire face of the object was illuminated from within.

Davan did not pull his hand away. His skin-light had gone to blue at his throat and was climbing toward something else — building at his collarbones, not yet arrived.

The image assembled itself.

First: a sphere. Blue-green, not large — the size of his closed fist, rendered in the cold light at the center of the dark surface. It turned slowly, and he could see the suggestion of landmasses, oceans, the marbled texture of atmosphere. A world. He knew it the way he knew his own palm lines — not because he had studied it, but because he had always been standing on it.

Then the shapes appeared.

They came in from the edges, or rather the image widened to include them — the perspective pulling back as if the viewer were rising, lifting away from the surface of the blue-green sphere to reveal what moved around it. Orbiting. Shapes in fixed paths, each tracing a line that the cold light rendered as a faint curve.

One. Large, steady, its path a clean circle.

Two. Smaller, closer to the world, moving faster.

Three. Between the other two, its orbit slightly tilted.

Four.

The fourth shape. Smaller, as if further away. As if it had always been the most distant of them. Its orbit was wider than the others, a long slow track around the blue-green world, and its shape — where the first three were rendered as distinct forms, each with enough detail to suggest a body with surface and mass — the fourth was reduced. A point that was almost a shape. Something that had been there when this record was made, caught at the limits of whatever instrument or eye had captured the arrangement.

Davan's skin went green-gold.

Not a pulse. Not the quick flash that came with a sudden feeling and faded. This was sustained — a glow that started at his wrists and moved up his forearms and across his collarbones and settled. It was the color of recognition, the register his body kept for the moments when something he had already known made itself visible. Like the carvings. Like the symbol he had been cutting into pine and cedar for months without knowing what it was, his hands working out a shape that his mind had not yet assembled.

Four.

He counted them again. One, two, three, four. The three he had always known — the three that anyone who looked up at night could count, the moons that moved through the sky in their ancient arrangement. And the fourth. Smaller, further, captured in a record that was older than anything he had ever touched.

"Four," he said. His voice was steady. The green-gold held.

Edvar was watching him, not the face. Davan could feel the quality of that attention — the mapmaker studying him the way he studied coastlines, looking for the features that confirmed or denied what his measurements had predicted.

"The margin notations," Davan said. "The old maps. The ones that show four."

"Yes."

"They weren't wrong."

"No."

Davan looked at the face again. The four shapes continued their orbits, the cold light tracing paths that had been fixed into whatever this material was — fixed and held and waiting for a touch to release them. He thought about how long they had been waiting. Twenty years in Edvar's trunk. Before that, sealed in a cellar beneath a building that had collapsed. Before that —

The fourth shape. Smaller, as if further away. But not further away, not really. The other three were still in the sky. He saw them every night. Whatever this record held was the arrangement as it had been, and the arrangement now held only three.

Something that had been in orbit was no longer in orbit.

"It came down," Davan said.

---

The silence that followed was not empty. It was the silence of a room in which something had been said that could not be unsaid, and both men in it understood this, and neither moved to fill the space with words that would only dilute what the words had done.

Edvar lowered himself from kneeling to sitting. His back found the side of the cot. He looked at the face — still lit, still turning, the four shapes in their patient loops — and something crossed the mapmaker's face: the expression of a man whose half-thought had just been finished by someone else's voice. Davan knew that look. He'd worn it.

"Twenty years," Edvar said again. But this time the words carried a different weight. Not *I have kept this for twenty years.* Rather: *For twenty years I have known what this meant and have not let myself say it.*

"You saw the four," Davan said. "Every time you opened the trunk. You counted them."

"Yes."

"And you looked up at the sky and counted three."

Edvar's hands were resting on his knees. They were the hands of a man who worked with fine instruments — steady, deliberate, the fingers long and spotted with age. They did not tremble. But there was something coming off the mapmaker that was not visible in his body — a vibration in the emotional register, the frequency of something long held finally being set down.

"I told myself it could be an error in the recording," Edvar said. "A flaw in the material. An artifact of age. I had seventeen plausible explanations for why the record showed four when the sky showed three, and I tested each of them against every old chart and margin notation I could find, and none of them held." He paused. "The fourth shape is consistent across every source I've found that predates the Quiet Century. After that period, every source shows three. The transition is clean. Whatever happened, happened within a single generation."

"Within living memory," Davan said. "Someone saw it happen."

"Someone saw it happen. And then, apparently, everyone agreed not to record it."

They sat with that. The cold light of the face painted them both in blue-white, and Davan's green-gold glow mixed with it, casting the small room in colors that did not belong to any lamp or hearth. He thought about the shape he had been carving. The orbital symbol. His hands had known, working it out of wood the way water worked its way through stone — not by force but by patience, by returning and returning to the same surface until the shape emerged.

The fourth shape on the face was not damaged. Not from this angle, this recording. It was simply distant and small, a body on a wide orbit, far enough out that the instrument or eye that had made this record could barely resolve it. But it was there. It was whole. It was in the sky.

And then it was not.

"Something that size," Davan said slowly, "coming down."

"Yes."

"The impact."

"I've spent years on the calculations." Edvar's voice had shifted — not louder or faster, but denser, the way a man spoke when he was finally using words he had rehearsed in solitude for decades. "The mass required to sustain an orbit of that period. The energy of a descent. Even a partial descent — even a fragment. The numbers are beyond what I have language for. Beyond what anyone in the cooperage group has language for, and several of them are better mathematicians than I am."

"But you've never told them."

"No."

"Why?"

Edvar looked at him. The expression was complex — layered, like the linseed smell, built up over years. Davan read it as best he could. There was protectiveness in it, and something like shame, and beneath both of those a loneliness so deep and so long-held that it had become structural. Part of the foundation. The man had built his life around a room that held a secret he could not share because sharing it meant completing a thought he could not face alone.

"Because I am a mapmaker," Edvar said. "I draw what is there. I record coastlines and elevations and the positions of landmarks relative to fixed points. I do not speculate. I do not theorize. I observe and I render and I verify." He looked down at his hands. "And for twenty years I have been in possession of an observation I cannot render on any map. A thing that was in the sky and is no longer in the sky. And the only conclusion that the evidence supports is that it came down, and that when it came down, it —"

He stopped.

Davan waited. The green-gold at his wrists pulsed once, gently, and then held steady again.

"— it changed everything," Edvar finished. "The Quiet Century. The gaps in the records. The things we've lost and can't account for losing. If something of that mass struck the surface or broke apart in descent, the consequences would have been —" He shook his head. Not a negation. A man reaching the limit of what solitary thought could carry. "I couldn't complete the sentence. I could never complete the sentence. I would get to that point and stop and wrap it up and put it back in the trunk."

"For twenty years."

"For twenty years."

Davan did not say *I understand.* He did not say *that must have been difficult.* He sat beside the mapmaker on the cold stone floor and let the silence hold what words would have only reduced. The face still turned between them, the four shapes in their ancient, patient orbits, and outside the shop the real sky held three.

---

The lamp had burned down by half before either of them spoke again. Davan had spent the time in the way he spent most of his thinking time — letting his attention move across the details of the room, the textures and proportions, the way the cold light from the face interacted with the warm light from the wick. His thoughts circled. The orbital symbol he'd been carving. The margin notations — *four* written in faded ink on charts so old the paper crumbled at the edges. The cooperage group's arguments about bloodline capacity and unchanged hands. Maren's disc, warm with something that was not temperature. The gray container Corvel had shown them, the one nobody could open, made of a material nobody could identify.

The container.

The thought arrived before he had words for it. A connection forming the way a shape formed under his knife — not all at once, but by the slow removal of everything that was not the shape, until what remained was clear.

"The container," he said.

Edvar turned his head. He had been looking at the face, or through it, in the way of a man whose eyes were focused on something that was not in the room.

"Corvel's container," Davan said. "The gray material. The way it absorbs light instead of reflecting it."

He put his hand on the surface of the face again. Not to activate it — it was still lit — but to feel the material itself. The substance of it. The way it took the light from the lamp and did not give it back. The way it sat in his hand with a density that was not stone, not metal, not wood, not anything he had worked with.

"It's the same," he said.

He looked at Edvar.

The mapmaker's expression was one Davan would remember for a long time afterward. He had seen many faces in the weeks since arriving in New Plymouth — the guarded scrutiny of Corvel, the sharp intelligence of the cooperage group members, Maren's complex stillness at the yard. But he had not seen this. This was the face of a man who had been sitting alone with a question for two decades, turning it over in his solitude, knowing what it meant and unable to say it — and who was now looking at someone who had walked into his shop and, in the space of an evening, asked the question he had been waiting for.

Not surprise. Not relief, exactly. Something older than either. The recognition of company after a very long time alone.

"Yes," Edvar said.

"The same material."

"I believe so. I have believed so since Corvel first brought it to the group, eight years ago. I held it in my hands and I felt —" He gestured at the face. "This. The same quality. The same way it sits against the skin. He found it in the marshland south of the ridge, half-buried in clay that the geological surveys suggest was laid down during the Quiet Century."

"During the impact window."

The words landed in the room like stones dropped into still water. Davan heard himself say them and understood that he had completed another of Edvar's sentences — not because he was presumptuous, but because the sentence had been waiting a long time to be finished and the man who had started it could not do it alone.

"During the impact window," Edvar repeated. Quietly. Testing the words in his own mouth. "Yes."

"So the container isn't from here."

"No. I don't think it is."

"And this —" Davan's fingers rested on the face, the cold light washing over his knuckles, his green-gold glow mixing with the blue-white. "This isn't from here either."

"I found it in a sealed cellar. Old construction — older than the colony records for that district. The building above it was already a ruin when it collapsed. Whatever was down there had been down there for a very long time."

They looked at each other across the lit surface of the face, and the full architecture of what they were sitting with settled into place. Not a revelation. Not the dramatic unveiling of a hidden truth. Something quieter and heavier: the slow, thorough understanding that the world they stood on had been struck by a piece of its own sky, and that the strike had left debris, and that the debris was not rock.

The face was a made thing. The container was a made thing. Whatever had occupied that fourth orbital position had not been a natural body — not a moon of stone and ice, tumbling through space on a path set by gravity alone. It had been something else. Something that could produce a flat dark surface that responded to touch and rendered light with a precision that no lampmaker or glassblower on this world could match.

The green-gold at Davan's collarbones flared and held. The recognition-register, his body's way of acknowledging what his mind was still assembling. He did not fight it. He let it burn.

"The cooperage group," he said after a while. "The arguments about capacity. Unchanged hands. All of it."

"All of it," Edvar said. "Every question they've been circling for years. Where the bloodline abilities come from. Why some people carry them and others don't. Why the oldest records show things that the newer records have forgotten." He folded his hands together in his lap. "If something fell — something made, something that was not a moon — then the question isn't just what it did to the surface when it landed. The question is what it was doing in orbit before it fell. And what it left behind."

"What it left in us," Davan said.

Edvar closed his eyes. Just for a moment. When he opened them, the tiredness in his face was the tiredness of twenty years of solitude laid down all at once, and beneath it was something that might have been gratitude, if gratitude could be that heavy.

"I have never said any of this to another person," he said. "I need you to understand that."

"I understand."

"I have maps and margin notations and geological surveys and this —" he gestured at the face, still patiently displaying its ancient record, "— and I have spent two decades building a case that I could never present. Because presenting it would mean saying what you just said. That something came down. That it was made. That it changed everything. And I am a mapmaker, Davan. I draw coastlines. I am not the person who says these things."

"No," Davan agreed. "But you're the person who found the evidence. And you kept it. And you waited."

"I waited."

"For someone to ask the right question."

Edvar looked at him with that expression again — the one Davan would carry with him when he left tonight, out into the dark streets where three moons hung in a sky that had once held four. The expression of a man whose long patience had been, finally, answered.

"Yes," the mapmaker said. "For someone to ask the right question."


## Chapter Twenty — *[Maren]*
### The Restricted Collection

---

The hoop-set cost four marks, which was robbery for bent iron and a handful of rivets. Maren paid without comment. The apprentice at the counter — sixteen, maybe seventeen, with the permanently reddened hands of someone who spent his days around steam — took the coins and nodded her toward the back without looking up from his ledger.

She'd been told the nod was the signal. Davan had explained it twice, the second time with the patient repetition of someone who understood that details kept people alive. Buy the hoop-set. Wait for the nod. Go through the door behind the stave rack. Down.

Davan went first. The stairs were narrow, stone-edged, built for moving barrels not people. The air changed three steps down — oak shavings and vinegar replaced by mineral cold, the particular dampness of stone that never saw sun. She catalogued it automatically: one exit behind her, none visible ahead, walls close enough to touch both sides with her elbows bent.

The cooperage sat two streets from the river, on a lane where the smell of the workshop covered everything else. She'd noted the approaches on the walk over. Three ways out of the lane. Vane was outside, near the gate, doing whatever Vane did when she waited — which was stand very still in a place where she could see a long way and think about angles. Maren had not asked her to come. Vane had simply been there when she left, and had walked beside her without explaining, and had stopped at the gate with a nod that meant *I'll be here*.

The basement opened up at the bottom of the stairs. Low ceiling, stone floor dark at the edges where the damp crept in. Two lamps on a table in the center, their light reaching about as far as the first ring of chairs and failing after that. The corners were suggestions. The room smelled like tallow and cold earth and, faintly, the chemical bite of tanning solution — someone's clothes, not the room itself.

Six people. She counted them before she counted anything else.

---

A woman at the head of the table, seated, both hands flat on the wood. Fifty or near it. Gray threaded through dark hair pulled back with no concession to style. She had the posture of someone who had spent years bending over texts and had eventually decided to stop bending — spine straight, chin level, the overcorrection become habit. That would be Renner. Davan had described her as *the one who runs it*, which Maren had understood to mean *the one nobody has successfully argued down*.

To Renner's left, a woman with the forearms of someone who worked with heat. Glassblower's arms — ropey, scarred at the wrists where the radiant burn caught you if you leaned wrong. Thirty, maybe younger. She sat with her hands in her lap, which was a choice. People who worked with their hands and then sat on them were making a point about patience. Asha.

Across the table, a square-built man with chemical stains worked into the creases of his knuckles that no amount of washing would remove. Tanner. Corvel. In front of him on the table sat an object Maren's gaze caught on and held: a smooth gray container, forearm-length, the surface so uniformly dark it looked less like a thing and more like the absence of a thing. The lamplight reached it and stopped. Did not reflect. Did not slide off. Simply ended.

She made herself look past it.

An older man in the far chair — sixties, weathered in the way of someone who spent time outdoors but thought indoors. Ink on his fingers, but old ink, in the cuticles. Mapmaker's hands. Edvar. He watched her come down the stairs with an expression that gave nothing and took everything in, and she recognized it because it was the expression she wore in rooms full of strangers. Professional assessment.

A young woman beside Edvar, also with ink stains — fresher ones, on the side of her writing hand where the heel dragged. Cartographer's apprentice, or assistant, or whatever the relationship was.

And Pip. She knew Pip already, or knew of him — Davan's friend from the docks district, the one who found things. Twenty-three and built like a question mark, all forward lean and nervous energy. He gave her a nod when she reached the bottom of the stairs. It was the most welcoming gesture she'd received so far.

Davan stepped to the side, giving the room a clear view of her. "This is Maren," he said. "She's the one I told you about."

The silence that followed was not hostile. It was evaluative. Maren stood in it the way she stood in all silences — with her weight even, her hands at her sides, her face offering exactly as much as she chose to offer. Three years of performing a version of herself that wasn't quite true had given her a very precise understanding of what her face did when she let it do nothing. Nothing was usually the right choice.

"Sit," Renner said. Not an invitation. An allocation of resources — there was a chair, Maren was standing, the inefficiency offended her.

Maren sat.

---

The questioning began without preamble.

"Davan says you have an object," Renner said. "Davan says a number of things. I'd like to hear what you say."

"I have an object," Maren said.

Renner's mouth pressed into something almost approving and almost impatient — the mouth of someone who had been used primarily for precision and had lost the knack of signaling anything softer.

"He also says you're hollow."

The word landed on the table between them like a stone dropped from a modest height. Not thrown. Just released. Maren noted the reactions without turning her head: Asha's hands shifted in her lap. Corvel looked at the object in front of him, then back at Maren. The young cartographer's pen stopped moving. Edvar did nothing, which told her Edvar already knew.

"Yes," Maren said.

"How long?"

"Always. I'm unchanged."

"And you've been concealing it for —"

"Three years."

Renner processed this — the slight narrowing of focus as she fitted the new piece into an existing framework and discovered the framework needed to flex. "Three years in a city that tests for the Chrysalis at employment, at housing registration, at —"

"Yes."

"How?"

"I'm very good at not being tested."

Another silence. This one had a different texture — not evaluative but recalibrating. Maren had just told a room full of strangers that she'd committed what amounted to three years of continuous civic fraud. The fact that she said it without drama, without invitation to elaborate, just the fact laid down like a stone in a wall — it registered differently with each of them. Pip looked impressed. Asha looked thoughtful. Renner looked like she was revising an estimate upward.

"Show me the object," Renner said.

Maren didn't reach for it yet. "I want to understand what I'm showing it to."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning Davan told me what this group studies. Founding-era material. The gap between what the bloodline claims and what the evidence supports. I want to know what question you're trying to answer before I show you what I might be the answer to."

The room shifted. Not physically — no one moved — but the quality of attention changed. Maren had just demonstrated that she'd listened to Davan, processed what he'd told her, and arrived at an inference that took most of them to the edge of comfortable territory. She hadn't said *I have something valuable*. She'd said *I understand the shape of the problem you're working on, and I know where I fit in it*.

Renner leaned back in her chair. The wood creaked. "All right," she said, and for the first time her voice carried something other than interrogation. Something that sounded, distantly, like the beginning of respect.

---

Renner talked. She talked the way she apparently always talked — in complete sentences, sourced, with the faint impatience of someone who'd had this argument before and won it every time and was tired of winning because winning hadn't moved anything forward.

The bloodline held that Founding-era objects responded to Chrysalis-touched physiology. That the change written into every person by the moons' influence was also a key, a compatibility layer — a *worthiness*, in the word the temple used. The objects left behind were meant for the changed, and only the changed.

"And the evidence for this," Renner said, "is that every person who has ever successfully operated a Founding-era mechanism has been Chrysalis-touched."

"Which is everyone," Asha said, from her chair. Quiet, but with the timing of someone who'd placed this particular stone in this particular wall before. "Everyone is Chrysalis-touched. So that evidence is —"

"Circular," Renner said. "Yes. Thank you, Asha, I was getting there."

"You were getting there slowly."

"I was getting there *thoroughly*." Renner turned back to Maren. "No unchanged person has ever been in a position to try. Not part of civic life. Not given access to archives. Not brought into contact with recovered objects. The test case doesn't exist."

"Hasn't existed," Asha corrected. Her eyes were on Maren.

The correction sat there. Renner did not look at Asha. She looked, for a fraction of a second longer than the argument required, at the dark surface of Corvel's container. Maren caught it. Renner was not just arguing the bloodline's epistemology; she was arguing herself. Thirty years of archival work inside the temple. Four years of nights and cooperage meetings after. If the test worked, Renner would not only be vindicated. She would be one of the people who had helped the story hold. The lamplight caught a muscle in her jaw and let it go.

"Four years ago," Renner continued, and her voice was the same voice, but something else moved underneath it now, "I found a margin notation in a pre-crash navigational text. The standard astronomical tables list four major orbital bodies. The notation — in what appears to be a contemporary hand, not a later addition — marks the number as wrong. Changes four to three."

"Which is heresy," Corvel said. It was the first time he'd spoken. His voice was lower than Maren expected, and more measured. A man who weighed words before spending them.

"Which is heresy in a text that predates the concept of heresy by approximately two centuries," Renner said. "The notation also includes a symbol. Small, easily missed." She looked at Maren with the directness of someone who had stopped apologizing for knowing more than everyone else in any given room. "Davan tells me you've seen the same symbol."

"On the disc," Maren said.

"On the disc."

The room held its breath. The collective suspension, the moment where a group that had been arguing in theory encountered the possibility of arguing in practice.

"Describe the symbol," Renner said.

"A curve, broken, with a line through the break. It's on the edge of the disc. It matches the inner orbital path on pre-crash charts."

Renner looked at Edvar. Something passed between them — a glance with years of shorthand in it. Edvar nodded, a small motion, almost invisible. Whatever the symbol was, Maren had described it correctly.

Somewhere in the cold floor beneath her boots the stone had started to pull the warmth out of her feet. The tallow smell thickened as the lamps burned down; one wick had begun to gutter.

---

"Can I ask you something directly?" Asha said.

"You've been asking indirectly for the last five minutes," Maren said. "Go ahead."

Craftsperson's directness met something it recognized. "Has the disc ever responded to you? Warmth, vibration, light, anything that suggests active function rather than inert material?"

The room went very still.

Maren considered the question. She considered what answering it honestly would mean — not for her, because she'd already walked down the stairs and sat in the chair and answered *yes* to *are you hollow*, which meant she'd already placed her bet. But for the argument. For the balance of power between Renner's insistence on evidence-before-testing and Asha's insistence that the absence of evidence was itself the evidence.

"It's warm," she said. "It pulses. The frequency has increased since I arrived in New Plymouth."

Nobody spoke.

"It started as once every few days. Now it's several times a day. The symbol on the edge — when it pulses, the symbol is warmer than the rest of the surface."

Corvel looked at his container. The gray surface sat there absorbing light, inert, silent, untouched by fourteen pairs of Chrysalis-touched hands over the eight years he'd had it. He looked at Maren.

Renner's expression had gone very flat. The flatness of someone whose framework was flexing further than she'd anticipated and who was deciding in real time whether to resist or accommodate. "That's —" she started.

"Reported experience," Asha said. "Not evidence. I know. But it's reported experience from an unchanged person handling Founding-era material, which is exactly the test case you said didn't exist."

"I said *hasn't* existed."

"Past tense. Yes. That's rather the point."

---

Outside, somewhere on the workshop floor above, a stave rack was being loaded; the distant knock of wood on wood came down through the floorboards. The lamps guttered again. Pip got up to trim the wicks, and for a moment the light contracted, pulling the room smaller, pushing the corners further into dark. Maren watched him work. His hands were steady. Dock worker's hands, broad across the palm, but he handled the wick-trimmer with the delicacy of someone who found things through sensitivity rather than force. The light steadied, expanded, reached the table again.

The disc was in her coat, in the inner pocket, where it had been since she'd fled north. She could feel it against her ribs — the warmth, the faint rhythm, the pulse that matched nothing she could identify. Not her heartbeat. Not her breathing. Something else. Something that had its own schedule and was keeping to it with increasing urgency.

Her father: he had carried this disc. He had gone east. He had not come back.

Vane at the gate, leaning against it in the dark with her arms crossed and her voice stripped to its operational register. *They need more information before heading east. We need to know what this group knows. But we're running out of room, Maren. Horst is in the city.*

Ulla's letter, her father's words filtered through Ulla's handwriting: the hill in the eastern highlands. Something there. Something the disc was for.

The recess on the disc's underside — the shaped depression that was clearly designed to receive something specific, something that fit, something that would complete a mechanism she didn't understand.

And Corvel's container on the table: the light-swallowing dark of it, the fourteen pairs of changed hands that had tried, the one pair of unchanged hands that hadn't.

The argument was still going. Renner and Asha, orbiting each other with the practiced gravity of people who had done this many times — Renner pulling inward toward rigor, Asha pushing outward toward action, neither of them wrong, both of them incomplete, and the incompleteness was the thing that kept them circling. They needed something to land on.

Edvar watched. He had been watching the entire time with the patience of a man who made maps and understood that the territory did not care what you drew on the paper. The young cartographer beside him had stopped pretending to take notes and was looking at Maren with open curiosity.

Davan sat near the door. He had vouched for her and then gone quiet, which was the right thing to do. He understood that the vouching was his job and the rest was hers. She noted it. Filed it under *competent*.

She looked down once at her own hands, resting on her knees. Unchanged, as they had always been. A little cold.

Maren reached into her coat.

---

*Vane, at the gate, had turned slightly toward the mouth of the lane. The light off the cooperage yard caught the angle of her cheek, and her right hand had moved without seeming to move, from her side to inside her coat. A figure at the far end of the street — man, heavy build, not moving the way a passing tradesman moved. He was watching the cooperage. Not the door. The approach.*

*Vane did not lift her head. She only shifted her weight so that the gatepost covered her outline from that angle. The figure turned, eventually, and walked on.*

---

She set the disc on the table.

No preamble. No announcement. Her hand went in, came out, set it down. A motion as plain as putting a cup beside a plate.

The disc sat on the wood between the two lamps. It was smaller than the container — palm-sized, dark, the same light-swallowing material. The symbol on its edge caught the lamplight and held it for a moment before the surface drank it down. Beside Corvel's container, the two objects looked like they belonged to the same sentence — different words, same language, same voice that had stopped speaking a very long time ago.

The room went silent.

Not the evaluative silence of her arrival. Not the recalibrating silence after she'd said *unchanged*. This was the silence of a room full of people who had been having an argument for years and had just watched the argument become unnecessary. The theoretical had become material. The gap in the evidence was sitting in a chair. The untested assumption was on the table.

Renner looked at the disc. She looked at the container. She looked at Maren.

Asha's hands came out of her lap and rested on the table, palms down, the first unguarded gesture Maren had seen from her. Corvel had gone very still, trying not to disturb something fragile. Edvar leaned forward — just slightly, just enough for his lamp-shadow to shift on the wall behind him.

Pip, standing by the lamps with the wick-trimmer still in his hand, did not move at all.

The disc pulsed. Once. Faintly. A warmth that Maren felt even with her hand no longer on it, the way you feel heat from a stone that has been sitting in the sun — residual, patient, indifferent to whether anyone was paying attention.

Every person in the room saw it. Not a glow — the material didn't glow. But something shifted in the surface, a deepening, as though the dark became briefly more itself. The container didn't respond. It sat beside the disc the same way it had sat for eight years: inert, sealed, silent under fourteen pairs of hands that were, perhaps, the wrong hands.

Renner went still — not because it had pulsed, but because the pulse was *specific*, and her framework did not account for specificity. Four years of argument had been built on a trait: that any unchanged hands would answer, that the key was the absence itself, distributed wherever the absence fell. The disc had not answered the room. It had answered the person. Her face did not fall — it rearranged, slowly, in the small public way of someone who would do her grieving later.

Renner opened her mouth. Closed it. Opened it again.

"Well," she said. The word came out stripped of everything she usually armored her words with — precision, sourcing, the faint impatience. Just the word. Bare.

The disc on the table. The container beside it. The faces around the room, all of them turned toward her.

The disc was not a keepsake. It had never been a keepsake. Her father had not left it for her because it was precious to him, a sentimental object to remember him by. He had left it for her because she was the only person in his life whose hands had not been changed. Because the disc was a key, or part of a key, or the first word of a question that required unchanged hands to ask.

She had carried it for years thinking she was carrying grief.

She was carrying a test.

And these people — Renner with her four years of argument and her thirty years inside the institution that had managed the forgetting, Asha with her circular evidence, Corvel with his sealed container and his silence, Edvar with his maps and his face that Davan had seen and not spoken of, Pip with his intuition, the young cartographer with her ink-stained hands, Davan by the door who had brought her here — these people had been asking the question she was the answer to. Not the disc. Her. The hollow woman who had walked through three years of fraud and come out the other side holding an object that responded to unchanged hands in a world that had decided unchanged hands were empty.

The disc sat on the table. The container sat beside it. The lamplight failed to catch either surface. The room waited.

She looked at her own hands, resting on her knees. Unchanged. Untouched. The hands the moons had passed over, or missed — hands that a world ten thousand years old had not written its story across. She did not know yet whether that was mercy or accident or something with a name she had not been given. She let the not-knowing stand.

She did not say anything. There was nothing to say. The disc was on the table. She was in the room. The argument was over.

The test had not yet begun.


## Chapter Twenty-One — *[Aldric]*
### Convergence

---

New Plymouth announced itself a half-day out by the thickening of traffic on the highland road — carters first, then droves of penned sheep, then the long slow wagons of the inland merchants with their tarpaulins drawn tight against the coastal damp. By the time the road rose to the last low ridge and gave him the city, Aldric was riding in company without having chosen it: a Keeper on a tired horse between a wool-factor from Stillwater and a family with three children asleep in a handcart. Four days of solitary travel had thinned something in him. The human noise was a warmth he let himself take.

The city lay in the wide bowl of its river mouth, harbor opening west to the sea under a sky gone the color of pewter. Slate roofs, smoke haze, a gridwork of streets older at the center and newer toward the walls. The river cut through from the northeast and emptied into the harbor with the slow argumentative authority of a big slow water. Ships at anchor along the eastern quays, their masts crossed like a small leafless forest.

Above the ridge, in the pale sky, the moons.

He counted them without meaning to. The Steady, low and westerly, pale as watered milk. The rust-tinted one, higher and smaller. Third, near the zenith, the fastest. Three.

Three where the oldest maps counted four. Three where the face in the undercroft had shown him four. He had ridden west for four days with this count as his companion, and the count had not settled. It sat in his chest the way a lintel sits — load-bearing, unobtrusive, the thing that held the rest up — and every time he looked at the sky the lintel was there.

He pressed his heels and his horse began the descent.

---

The temple district ran along the southern ridge above the river, its buildings older and less crowded than the merchant streets below, with the deliberate spaciousness of institutions that had claimed their ground early and never been asked to yield it. The Keeper's House was a narrow stone hall attached to the secondary chapel of Saint Imret the Patient — a minor saint whose chief virtue had been the willingness to remain at a post that no one visited for seventy-two years. Aldric had always found the dedication faintly pointed.

The warden at the gate was a lay brother named Yelin whom Aldric had last seen nineteen years ago at a conference in the Old Capital. Yelin remembered him with the sharp accurate pleasure of a man who had cultivated his memory the way other men cultivated a garden. They stood a quarter-hour in the yard trading the small currencies of seminary conversation — who had died, who had taken orders in the western provinces, who had disappeared into a scholarly obsession in some fishing village.

Aldric waited until the talk had thinned before he asked what he had come to ask.

"Yelin. Has the office had a Keeper through lately? Scholarly consultation?"

Yelin's attention oriented. "Yours?"

"I'm asking generally. The last month or two."

"Sister Ilona came through. Brother Tomas overnight on his way to the island. There was a Keeper from Confluence — I don't remember the name. He didn't stay at the House. Kept at a private lodging. Came to the office only to file his entry."

Aldric had filed no entry. He had not come to the office.

"When was this?"

"A week past. Perhaps ten."

"What was his business?"

"Scholarly consultation. Eastern provinces. Father Thane handled him. I could ask."

"No," Aldric said. He said it more quickly than he meant to and had to do the small repair-work of seeming unconcerned. "No, don't ask. I thought I might have missed a colleague."

Yelin accepted this with the surface courtesy of a man who had made a professional practice of not probing what was offered. But something had registered — the small filing-motion in Yelin's eyes, the entry of the question into an interior ledger for later review. The mistake had already done its work and could not be undone.

Scholarly consultation. Eastern provinces. A Keeper from Confluence a week past, traveling under a description that matched his own almost exactly. Crane had not bothered to invent a different cover.

"Yelin," he said. "I find I may not need lodging at the House after all. I have an old friend in the docks district. I'll come up another day for the office."

"As you wish, Keeper."

He walked his horse out of the gate and down the ridge road with his face composed. When he reached the first switchback on the descent he let his breath out fully, and it came out shaky, and he steadied it.

*Providence,* he thought, and the word arrived in his mouth with its old liturgical weight — and beneath the weight, the word that belonged here instead: *procedure.* Crane had not extended her reach by providence. She had extended it by procedure. A Keeper had been sent ahead. A description had been filed. A desk clerk named Father Thane had accepted the filing without looking at it closely, because filings did not require looking closely, because the procedure assumed good faith, because the procedure had been written two hundred years ago by people who could not have imagined a use for it like this one.

He had been counting the wrong kind of reach. Crane's had not needed the length his fear had granted it. It had needed only the infrastructure that was already there, and the infrastructure was everywhere, and the infrastructure was patient.

The lamp tic moved in his hand. He had reached — at nothing, at air — for a flame that did not exist, and he closed his fingers on the empty space and let them fall.

---

The docks district met him at the river's edge the way a different country met you at a border — not gradually, but all at once. The temple district had been bodies at rest or in ceremony. The docks district was bodies at work, and the work was loud, and the work did not pause for an uncertain stranger with a tired horse and a traveling coat.

A stevedore shouldered past him at the head of a line of men moving sacks from a cart to a warehouse door; the sacks smelled of grain and coarse linen, the cart horses of sweat and piss and the corn they had been eating. A gull argued with another gull above a fish-cart three stalls down. A woman at a flower-stall told a boy to stop running into her pots, and the boy ran into a pot, and the pot went over, and the noise continued past the event without registering it.

The smell of fish was underneath everything — not the clean cold brine he had taken the harbor to be when he first saw it from the ridge, but the worked smell, cut and gutted and salted and smoked and newly-caught all braided together in the warm damp of the waterfront.

A Keeper in a docks district was already a small heresy of bearing. The order's vestments were designed for chapels, not quays. He adjusted. He slowed. He let the crowd flow around him rather than through him, and he let his expression soften into the amiable vagueness of a traveler who had not quite found what he was looking for — an expression so common on the waterfront that it was effectively invisible.

*Wreckage,* he thought, because the word arrived and he let it. The church would call the district a *congregation.* The scholar in him would call it a *commercial community.* Between the two, the word he was actually using was *wreckage.* Small working salvages of a population making its living from the edge of a sea. The boy with his pot. The stevedore with his sack. The flower-woman with her vendor's voice. Each a bit of what the crash had scattered, still functioning, still making the small exchanges by which a life was assembled from the wreck of the larger thing.

He found a public house two blocks up from the waterfront with a yard that would take his horse, paid for a bed and a stall, and came back down into the common room with his cassock replaced by a plain dark overtunic. The landlord — heavy-built, scarred at the lip, the quick uninterested kindness of someone who had seen every kind of guest pass through — asked no questions. He paid her in advance for three nights. She brought him a cup of warm cider without his having ordered one.

He drank it slowly and let his body understand that he was no longer on a road.

---

He needed a way in.

He knew what he was looking for. Reth's directions in memory. Brennan's maps in his saddlebag. Brennan's letter with the phrase *for unchanged hands* — a sentence that had ridden with him for weeks, nearly legible now, not yet.

He did not have, and needed, the people. A city this size, a question this old — there would be people working it. Not institutionally. The institution had decided eight hundred years ago that the question did not exist. But informally. In workshops and the back rooms of scholarly clubs. Brennan had worked it alone in Greenford for twelve years. Someone in New Plymouth was working it too. The question had a gravity, and gravity collected.

He spent the rest of the afternoon walking the Crafting Quarter.

He did not ask for anything. He walked. Smithies together, glassblowers together, mapmakers and scribes along one particular curving lane that the locals called, for reasons no longer clear, the Inkwalk. He walked it twice in the late afternoon, past a chandler's and a bookseller's, and he stopped at a cartographer's window to look at a set of coastal charts drawn by a hand that had spent a lifetime getting the fall of a shoreline exact.

A card in the window: *Consultations by appointment.* A name: *E. Marrick, Cartographer and Chartmaker.* A second address on the door.

He walked the two blocks east and stood across the street from the shop in the dusk and watched it. One man came out carrying a wrapped parcel and did not return while Aldric stayed. He noted the posted hours — *dawn to second bell* — walked back to the public house, ate a plain dinner at the window table, and slept poorly. He dreamed of the face.

---

The shop opened shortly after first bell, which was earlier than the posted hours — the kind of small lie a tradesman told to buy himself a quiet hour at the start of the day. Aldric had been on the street for twenty minutes when the shutter came up. The man who lifted it was in his sixties, spare-built, with ink-stained fingers and the specific attentiveness of a man who had just been at his worktable and had come forward reluctantly to let the day in.

Edvar Marrick looked at him over the half-raised shutter.

"We open at second bell," he said. His voice was level. Not unfriendly. Not inviting.

"I know," Aldric said. "I was hoping for a consultation."

"Consultations are by appointment."

"I understand. May I make one?"

Marrick considered him through the gap in the shutter. Aldric stood still under the consideration and let it happen — he understood that he was being read, and he understood that what Marrick was reading was the fact of a plainclothes traveler at a cartographer's shop before opening, asking in the specific careful cadence of an educated man who was trying to sound ordinary. The cassock was off but the cadence had never come off. Brennan had read him the same way.

"What is the consultation regarding?" Marrick said.

"The eastern highlands," Aldric said. "Specifically a formation east of the highland plateau, approximately eleven miles northeast of the old commission station, roughly four hundred meters in lateral extent. I'm told it's been described in a territorial commission report as a mineral deposit of uncertain composition. I'd like to know if you have mapped it."

Marrick's face did nothing.

He looked at Aldric for a long slow interval that was not silence but a very particular kind of attention — the attention of a man turning a question over in his hand and examining each of its faces in turn, the way he would have turned one of his small instruments. Aldric did not fill the interval. He had delivered the question. The question was doing its work.

Marrick raised the shutter the rest of the way.

"Come in," he said.

---

The shop smelled of linseed and old paper. Aldric stepped through the door and the smell met him the way the smell of a familiar church met him when he had been traveling — a specific density of atmosphere, a quality made of decades of the same materials in the same small space. He was aware, with the stripped clarity that had been following him since Greenford, that he had just crossed a threshold he would not be able to re-cross. The shop was a room he had entered once in the simple way and would never enter the same way again.

Marrick closed the door behind him. He did not turn the sign on the door. The sign still read *Closed.*

"You haven't told me your name," Marrick said.

"Aldric Voss. Keeper at the Temple of Confluence."

"A Keeper."

"Yes."

"And you are asking about a formation in the eastern highlands that does not appear on any public map."

"Yes."

Marrick nodded, small, once. He walked to his worktable and sat down at it — an old wooden chair, scarred at the arms by forty years of the same forearms resting on it — and he did not invite Aldric to sit. He looked at his hands for a moment, arranging them on the table in the way Aldric had come to recognize as the way a person of long patient inquiry arranged the body when the inquiry arrived in the room. Brennan did this. Reth had done it. Aldric did it himself, though he had not been aware he did until he watched other men do it.

"Why are you asking me?" Marrick said.

"Because you make maps, and a formation of that size and nature should appear on your maps if it appears on anyone's."

"And it does not appear on any map you have seen."

"It does not."

"But you have the directions to find it."

"I do."

Marrick looked up. The look was long and measuring and not unkind.

"From whom?" he said.

Aldric considered the question. He had come prepared to offer Brennan and Reth as references, had rehearsed the sequence on the ride from the temple district. But standing in the shop with the linseed smell around him and the old cartographer looking at him with the attention of a man who had clearly been working some version of this question for a long time, he understood that names were not what Marrick was asking for. Marrick was asking for the shape of the approach.

"From a friend in Greenford who has been studying margin notations on pre-crash maps," Aldric said. "Who introduced me to a surveyor who walked the formation forty-odd years ago and could not place what he had found."

The silence that followed was different from the silence at the door. It was the silence of a man who had just heard a sentence he had given up on hearing.

Marrick closed his eyes briefly. When he opened them his hands had not moved on the table.

"The surveyor's name," he said.

"Reth."

"Reth." Marrick said the name as though he had been holding a candidate for it and the candidate had resolved. "He still lives?"

"He does."

"He told you what he saw?"

"He told me. And more than that. He described a recess at the southeastern end of the formation, where the surface curves down into the hillside. A recess shaped like a coin. With a symbol in it."

Marrick's right hand lifted off the table and pressed flat against his chest, over the heart, a gesture so involuntary and so complete that Aldric knew the mapmaker had not known he was going to make it. When he lowered the hand, his eyes were wet, though his face had not otherwise moved.

"The symbol," Marrick said.

Aldric did not describe it. He had come to know, over the last months, the precise moment at which describing a thing replaced the thing with the description, and this was that moment, and he would not replace the thing. He simply looked at Marrick, and Marrick looked back, and whatever communication was required happened between them without the word.

"There are people," Marrick said slowly, "who have been asking some version of your question for years. I have been asking it for twenty. There are others. We meet."

"I would like to meet with them."

"You have not told me everything you know."

"No. I have not."

"Will you?"

Aldric considered. The face in the undercroft. The four shapes around the blue-green world. The one irregular, damaged. The four smooth containers in the sealed chamber, lined against the wall like patient urns. The phrase from Brennan's letter riding in his chest. The Keeper who was not him, filing an entry in the office a week past. Yelin's registering face. The lamp tic on his thumb.

"I will tell them what I have," he said. "Once I see the shape of what they have. Not before."

Something in Marrick settled — not a smile, Aldric had thirty years of reading faces in chapels and knew the difference between expression and arrangement — but the settled quiet of a man who had found a negotiating partner and had stopped expecting to. He stood up from his worktable. The stand was slow, the knees reluctant, the old cartographer's practiced negotiation with his own body.

"There is a cooperage two streets from the river," he said. "I will write you an address. You will present yourself this evening after the second bell. You will buy a set of iron hoops from the apprentice at the counter. You will wait for a nod. And then you will go where you are directed."

"A cooperage."

"The people I meet with meet in its basement. It is as private as any room in this city. The workshop above makes noise enough to cover the talk below."

"And they will receive me?"

"They will receive someone I send."

Marrick took a small square of paper from a drawer and wrote an address on it in a hand that was neither hurried nor decorative — the hand, Aldric recognized with the small shock of a pattern recognized across contexts, of a man who used the same hand to write in the margins of twelve maps over fifteen years. He folded the paper twice and held it out.

"Keeper Voss," Marrick said.

"Yes."

"There is a young woman who will be in the room tonight. She carries an object that you will recognize when you see it. I am telling you this now so that when you see it you do not say the wrong thing. The room has spent the last three days adjusting to it. I would prefer not to adjust again."

"I understand."

"You do not understand yet. But you will."

Aldric took the paper. Marrick turned the sign on the door to *Open.*

---

He went back to the public house and slept three hours. Then he wrote to Brennan — a short institutional letter that said, among other things: *I have arrived. The shape of the question here is larger than I had understood. I am meeting the people who work it tonight. I will write again.* He sealed it and left it on the table to be posted.

He washed his face, dressed plainly, did not wear the cassock. When the second bell rang out over the river from the merchant campanile, he left the public house and walked east along the waterfront and then inland two streets, and he found the cooperage by the smell of oak shavings and the pale lamplight under the shutter of the workshop door.

The apprentice at the counter was sixteen or seventeen with red steam-scalded hands, and did not look up from his ledger when he took Aldric's coins. The nod came after a long enough pause that Aldric had time to doubt it would come at all. A door behind the stave rack. Stone stairs leading down. The air changed three steps down, the way Reth had described the change in the light at dusk on the formation — not subtle, not gradual, but a decisive crossing from one kind of atmosphere to another, and the atmosphere below was stone-cold and tallow-smelling and held the faint chemical bite of a tannery.

At the bottom of the stairs, the basement opened.

Two lamps on a central table. Corners left in shadow. The room held the specific chilly dampness of a cellar that had been dug into living rock and never fully warmed by a summer in six hundred years. Eight or nine people turned to look at him as he came down. He registered them as arrangement first — the old method, the chapel method — and then as individuals.

Marrick at the end of the table, seated. He had come ahead.

A woman Aldric took for Renner by the description Marrick had not given him — the straight-backed, gray-threaded, unsoftened woman who had the bearing of someone who had run rooms. Beside her a younger woman with forearms that told him glassblower without his needing to be told. Across the table a square-built man whose hands had been tanner's hands long enough for the leather smell to live in them. A young woman with ink on the heel of her writing hand who was almost certainly the one Marrick had mentioned in some other context he did not yet have. A lean angular boy in his twenties, all forward-lean, who looked at Aldric with an assessing brightness that was somehow both friendly and cold.

A young man near the far wall, quiet, watchful, with a stillness to him that registered before the rest of him did — twenty, perhaps; long-limbed; carrying himself in the specific way that men carried themselves when they were managing a body that did things without their permission. His skin at the collar of his shirt, where it was bare, had the faintest wash of a color that was not a color — a suggestion of blue under the skin that was not a vein or a shadow or a trick of the lamplight.

And a young woman, small and dark-haired, seated to the left of the glassblower, whose hands rested in her lap and whose eyes met his directly when he reached the bottom step. She was, by Marrick's warning, the one who carried the object. He had been told this so he would not say the wrong thing. Looking at her, he understood the warning better. She had the composure of a person who had made a difficult decision very recently and was still carrying the freshness of the making. Her face did nothing he could read. It did, however, register him completely — the look of a person whose first act in any room was to count the exits and catalogue the inhabitants, and who had done so before his second step off the stair.

The girl who carried the object. He was a Keeper of thirty years. He had read the sealed chamber in the undercroft. He had looked into the face and counted four. He had ridden a thousand miles of road with the word *providence* turning in his chest into the word *wreckage.* He had arrived.

He did not say anything.

Marrick spoke first.

"Friends," he said. "This is Aldric Voss. He is a Keeper from the Temple of Confluence. He has come to us along a thread I will not yet describe in full. He has directions to a formation in the eastern highlands that I believe you will recognize. He has, in addition, something else that I will let him say in his own time."

Renner looked at him for a long interval. Her look was not unkind. It was the look of a woman whose framework had been broken recently, and who was no longer assuming that the next stranger down her stairs belonged in any of her existing categories.

"Keeper," she said. "Sit."

He sat.

The young man near the wall — the one with the wash of blue under his skin — had been watching him since he descended. Now the skin at his wrists had gone, very faintly, from the blue wash to something warmer, a low amber that was not alarm but was not calm either. The young man caught Aldric's eyes on the shift and something in his face registered the catch. He lowered his gaze. The amber at his wrists receded by a degree.

Above the table, the two lamps. Between the lamps, on the wood, an object. Smooth gray. Forearm-length. The surface so uniformly dark it did not quite register as a surface — the lamplight reaching it and stopping there as though it had crossed a boundary into a different kind of light. Beside it, smaller, palm-sized, the same material: a disc.

Aldric's breath caught — one long-practiced careful breath, inhaled and held, the way he had been trained to hold his breath in chapel at the moment of the sanctus. He did not let it out audibly. He let it out slowly through the nose, and his hands did not reach, and his hands did not move, and his hands stayed on his thighs where he had put them when he sat down.

*For unchanged hands.*

The phrase from Brennan's letter arrived in the room the way a piece of cathedral masonry settles into its place and becomes, from that moment, structural — no longer an object carried but a load-bearing part of the standing thing. He had carried the phrase for weeks without knowing what it would hold up. He saw it now. He saw it in the girl's face, and in the disc on the table, and in the young man's amber and blue, and in Renner's broken-and-mended framework, and in the container's inert silence beside its smaller companion. The sentence was coming into focus. He was still outside it — they had a week he did not have, they had arguments he had not heard, they had a specific person he did not yet know by name or history — but the sentence was in the room, and he was in the room, and he was closer to it than he had been at any moment since the sealed chamber.

---

They went around. Names, brief accounts, what each of them held.

Renner. Former temple archivist. Four years on the astronomical question. The argument about the bloodline's capacity. She delivered her account in a voice that had once been aggressive-precise, Marrick had warned him; what Aldric heard was a voice that had been aggressive-precise until recently, and had been carrying its new humility in a way that made the old precision stand out by contrast — the way a hand that used to grip something and has let go still keeps the shape of the grip.

Asha. Glassblower. Circular evidence. The test case. The correction — *hasn't existed* — delivered to him as a phrase already polished by previous use, a stone smoothed by handling.

Corvel. Tanner. A container he had found in the marshland south of the ridge, half-buried in clay, eight years ago, that he had carried to Marrick and through him to this group. The container sat on the table beside the smaller disc. Aldric looked at it for the first time with attention, and under the attention the container's surface did what Reth had said the formation's surface did at dusk: took his looking and did not give it back, the light seeming to go into the material rather than off it.

The young cartographer — Tela, her name was; Aldric did not catch the family name on first hearing and did not press for it — had ink stains fresh on her writing hand and the kind of silence that was not deferential but intent. She was listening rather than speaking. She looked at Aldric the way Marrick had looked at him in the doorway: with the open directness of a person who had decided that courtesies could wait.

Pip — the lean boy — introduced himself with a half-grin that was not practiced. He found things, he said. It was how he had come into the group. He did not elaborate. Marrick, beside him, did not elaborate either, and Aldric took the non-elaboration as data.

The young man near the wall was Davan. Davan, from Amber Valley. New to the city. A carver, among other things. He had come to Marrick through Pip, three months or so ago. He had seen a thing in Marrick's keeping that he did not name in this introduction. He kept his hands on his knees and the amber-blue wash at his collar shifted slowly as he spoke, and Aldric registered the shift without mentioning it — because the young man was clearly aware of it and clearly managing it, and it was not Aldric's place to name a body's tell in a room where the body in question was already performing the management of it.

And Maren. She said her name. She said that she was from Ashwick, and that she had been in New Plymouth for some weeks, and that she was unchanged — she used the word herself, unchanged, evenly, without either defiance or shame — and that she carried an object her father had left her, which was on the table, which had, in the last several days, begun to pulse with increasing frequency, and which had — she said this without drama, setting it down like a stone into the wall of her account — pulsed three days ago, in this room, in a way that the container beside it had not and had never.

She did not look at him when she said it. She said it to the table. She had delivered an account this way before. He understood that.

When she finished, she did look at him. Her eyes were dark and not guarded, not in the usual sense, but had the quality of a room he had once walked into in the old monastery at Vellicar — a chapel whose door had been left open to the weather for an afternoon, the air inside it changed by the outside, and every object in it more itself for the exposure.

"Keeper," she said. "Your turn."

---

He spoke for perhaps a quarter-hour.

He did not say everything. He did not speak of Crane — not yet. He did not describe the Keeper who was not him filing a fabricated entry in the office a week past. He did not say the word *providence* as he had said it to himself on the road, though he thought it once, near the end of his account, and pressed it down.

He said: *A sealed chamber in the undercroft of the Temple of Confluence. Found two months ago. Five objects. One of them is a flat dark surface that responds to touch. It shows an image. The image is an arrangement of orbital bodies. Four of them. One of them is not like the others. It is irregular. It reads as damaged.*

He said: *I have a friend in Greenford — a scholar named Brennan. He has been working the margin notation question for twelve years. Twelve maps in his possession. Four in the oldest, three in the youngest. A clean transition.*

He said: *He introduced me to a surveyor named Reth who walked the eastern highlands forty years ago and found a formation four hundred meters long, metallic, no grain, no seam, manufactured, partially buried under centuries of sediment. At the southeastern end, where it curves into the hillside, there is a recess. The size of a coin. With a symbol in it.*

He said the things he could say, and he did not describe the symbol, because to describe it he would have had to take Maren's disc in his hand, and he was not yet certain it was his to take.

The room received it. Not in silence, exactly — Renner moved a pen fractionally on the table; Corvel's breath altered; Asha's hands came out of her lap and lay flat; Tela stopped pretending to take notes — but in the quality of silence that was not an absence of speech but a presence of attention, a silence that had shape to it and texture, and pressed against him from all sides.

Davan's skin at the collarbones had gone, during his account, to a color Aldric did not have a name for. Not amber. Not blue. Something between them and also beside them, a quiet warm green-gold that held steady rather than pulsing. Aldric looked at it and did not understand it and understood it entirely. The young man was showing his recognition of what Aldric was describing in the only way his body had for recognition. A second narrator. A skin that said what the body underneath it had already known.

"The symbol," Renner said, finally.

"I would like to see the disc first."

Renner did not look at Maren. She waited for Maren to answer for herself.

Maren lifted the disc off the table with the same quiet economy with which she had set it down three days ago and slid it across the wood. It came to rest two hand-widths from Aldric's knee, close enough that he could see the inner orbit symbol on its edge clearly in the lamplight — the four nested arcs, the raised points along their paths, the fourth arc drifting tangentially off the pattern. Exactly as on the door above the Temple of Confluence. Exactly as on the margin of a map twelve of which sat in Brennan's workroom in Greenford. Exactly as, by Reth's account, in a recess cleared of sediment in a hillside eleven miles northeast of an old commission station.

He looked at it.

He did not reach for it.

His hand lifted from his thigh and crossed the short space between his knee and the disc and stopped — stopped of its own accord, without his deciding that it would stop, his trained fingers arrested a span from the surface of a thing his body had been trying to name for months and his mind had been counting toward for longer than that. The hand did not tremble. It simply stood at that distance, a hand's breadth from an object it had crossed a thousand miles of road to come into proximity with, and it did not close.

He had reached for a flame that was not there a dozen times in the last week. He had trained the reach to stop. And now the hand he had trained to stop was stopping at the one thing he had come to touch, and the training had held too well, and he understood, with the specific awkward clarity of a man watching a habit misfire into an unintended grace, that this was the hand's refusal and not his own.

He let the hand stay. He did not force it forward.

He did not force it back.

Maren watched him. Her face was very still. She did not offer the disc; she did not withdraw it; she did not, in the manner some people had, attempt to reassure him. She let him stand in his own hesitation at the width of a hand's breadth from the answer, and her letting him stand there was the largest courtesy anyone had extended to him since the sealed chamber.

"Keeper," Marrick said, quietly.

"Yes."

"Take your time."

He nodded. He lowered the hand. He laid it flat on his own thigh, palm down, the way Reth had laid his hands on the table forty years after filing his report. The disc sat where Maren had slid it. Beside it, the container, inert. Above both, the two lamps on the table, one of them down to the last quarter-inch of its wick.

The room held. Nobody moved to fill the space.

Outside and above, beyond the cooperage roof, the three moons were where they had been for ten thousand years, at their posts, at their stations, keeping a count he had finally, in the cold basement of a New Plymouth workshop, come into the room with people who were keeping it with him.

He would touch the disc. He would touch it this week. He would touch it tomorrow, or the day after, when his hand decided it belonged to him again. Tonight he had come this far, and his hand had stopped at the span of a closed flower from the answer, and the stopping was what his body had given him instead of a speech, and he let it be the speech.

Maren's eyes crossed the table and met his, and the look was not expectant and was not patient — it was, with the economy she seemed to bring to every gesture, only the look of one person acknowledging another across a room that had finally assembled everyone it had been trying to assemble.

The disc pulsed, faintly, once — no warmer than the lamp, no brighter than the dark, just the small deepening of the surface that he had not seen before but that he recognized with his whole body as the thing she had described, as the thing Brennan had inferred, as the thing Reth had half-seen and refused. Maren did not look at it. She looked at him.

His hand stayed on his thigh.

Somewhere above them, in the workshop, a stave knocked against a stave, and a barrel hoop settled into place with the faint iron ring of work being done, and the ring carried down through the floorboards and died in the cold air of the cellar, and the room waited, and he waited with it, and the span between his hand and the disc was the exact width of the sentence he had come a thousand miles to begin.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Two — *Vane*
### What the Contract Was For

---

The patterns told her in the morning.

She had been reading Horst's search for six days, and on the sixth day the shape of it closed by half. Not all at once. In increments. A lodging visited twice on Sellcross Road, which was north of the cooperage by four streets. A boy sent down Marlin's Walk asking after strangers, which was south of it by three. A tailing man she had clocked on Tuesday along the river quays, retracing the same ground on Wednesday in a coat he had not worn on Tuesday.

She sat on the edge of the bed with her boots unlaced and the inventory in her head.

*Six days. Three narrowing arcs. One-shift coat. Two-handler lodging sweep. One outer cordon.*

Horst was not a sloppy man. He was a thorough one. Thoroughness had a signature — it showed as symmetry when the net was wide, and it showed as overlap when the net was closing. The overlap had started Monday.

She gave them two days. Possibly three. Not four.

---

The tin was still in her pack.

She had not taken it out in four days. The last wafer was in it. She had not eaten the last wafer. She had been deciding, every morning, not to eat it — a small sustained decision she made by not opening the tin and not reaching for it, which was the only form of deciding she now trusted. Promises to herself were cheap. Motions not-made were not.

Her hands shook at the fingertips when she laced the boots. Fine tremor. She worked around it. She had laced boots with worse tremors.

---

Maren was asleep in the other bed. Dark hair across the pillow. Face slack in the way a face went slack only after three years of vigilance — the vigilance dropped, briefly, because she had judged the room safe enough to drop it in. Vane registered the trust and did not let it move her.

She crossed to the door and picked up her coat from the hook. She did not wake Maren. Waking Maren would have required explanation, and the explanation was not speakable yet.

*One hour,* she had written on the scrap of paper by the basin. *Back by second bell.*

She put the note where Maren would see it and went out.

---

Lintel Street was different in the morning.

At dusk four days ago the street had been a transaction corridor — grey, underlit, end-of-day traffic thinning toward the bridge, the rooming-house smells dying out of the air and the canal smell coming up. Now it was a working street in daylight. Carts passed both ways. A scullery girl threw a bucket of water into the gutter from a doorway. A barge captain stood outside the pawnbroker's at the corner, arguing price on a coil of rope. Three children ran past her chasing a dog.

She noted the cover and took it.

Third door from the bridge. Iron-banded. No marking. Still there — the city had not swallowed it in four days, which had been a thin private hope she had not let herself formulate.

She did not knock the pattern this time.

She crossed the street and sat at a chandler's stall four doors west and bought a wick-trimmer she did not need. She paid in coin. She waited.

The pattern she was reading now was not a search pattern. It was a production pattern. She wanted to know the schedule.

---

The door opened twice in the first hour. Once: a man in a waxed coat came out carrying a parcel. Went left, toward the bridge. Nine minutes. Returned without the parcel. Went back in. Twice: the same man again, carrying nothing, who went east along the canal path and did not return while she watched.

Not Deren. A runner. The runner was the delivery system — sealed wax pots out, some to a confectioner's or a pharmacist's or a back-room broker's; nothing on the face of them to tell a street-watcher what they were. The confectioner-pharmacist fiction was the kind of cover a Bloom chain used. She had used similar covers herself, on the other side of the transaction, for years.

After the second run, the door stayed closed.

She waited another forty minutes.

It stayed closed.

*Runner out. Deren either upstairs at the desk or out himself. No third body up there I've seen.*

The door-body from four days ago was not on the street. He worked afternoons, probably. The morning was thinly staffed. She had been betting on this and the bet had come in.

She crossed the street.

---

She did not knock the pattern. She knocked like a woman who had come for household service and had been sent to the wrong door.

It opened an inch.

"Who?" A man's voice. Not Deren. Younger. An unfamiliar runner, or a house-boy.

"I was sent from the harbor clerk's office."

"Wrong house."

"Two-one-five Lintel. The clerk's register says Deren at two-one-five."

"Wrong house."

She leaned in, the way a lost stranger leaned in — irritated but harmless, the body-shape of a woman whose morning had already gone badly and who wanted a straight answer before she had to walk back in her good shoes to be told by the clerk she had misread the register.

He opened another inch.

She went in on the inch.

---

He was young — twenty-two, twenty-three. Half a head taller than her. She had the inside of the door before he had his balance back, and she had his arm behind him and her knee in the back of his knee before he had the breath for the sound he would otherwise have made. She put her mouth near his ear.

"Quiet," she said. "This is not about you."

He was not trained. He stiffened and then loosened, which was what untrained people did when a professional told them to be quiet. She guided him to the wall and pressed him flat and held him there with forearm across his shoulders.

"Where's Deren."

"Upstairs."

"How many."

"Just him."

"You're lying."

"Just him." She felt the truth of it in the tension. A liar lied with the neck and the gut; a truth-teller lied, if he lied, with the voice. This one was neck and gut. "He's got the ledger. He's — the morning's slow."

"Door at the end of the hall — the basement. That's the one I want."

"Yes."

"How many down there."

"Just her." He hesitated. She pressed. "Just her. I swear. I don't — I don't go down."

"You've been told you don't go down."
