"Yes."

"Key."

"On him. Deren. Upstairs."

*Of course it is,* she thought, and did not say. Deren's kind of man kept the key on his body because keys were trust, and trust was paid out, not given.

She took a length of soft rope from her coat pocket — she had packed it this morning without allowing herself to review what the packing implied — and she bound the boy's wrists behind him and lowered him to sit against the wall, and she stuffed a wadded kerchief into his mouth and tied the ends behind his head. She did it efficiently. She did not hurt him. He watched her the whole time with the flat dumb fear of a dog that had been beaten without being told why.

She put her face in front of his.

"Nothing happens to you today," she said. "Not by me. Sit here."

He nodded.

She went up the stairs.

---

The stairs to the upper floor creaked on the fourth and seventh steps. She had been counting them in her head from the street, and the count matched her estimate.

The door at the top was ajar.

She listened for the space of three breaths.

A pen on paper. A man's cough. The small dry cough of a man who had drunk nothing yet this morning and was clearing a night's stale throat. A cup being set down on wood.

She pushed the door open.

Deren looked up from his ledger.

He was older than she had pictured from the one short meeting four days ago. Early fifties, possibly. His eyes read her quickly and were still.

She saw him decide not to reach for whatever was at his right hand under the desk. She saw the decision happen and approved of it. He was a merchant, not a fighter. He had made his professional peace with the fact long ago.

"I remember you," he said.

"I remember the house."

She did not move into the room. She stood in the doorway with her weight on her back foot and her right hand at her side, and she let him finish what his face was doing. His face finished. It arrived at the small slack settling of a man who understood that the next hour was not going to be his hour.

"The key," Vane said.

"Which."

"The one to the basement door."

He looked at her. A long look. Older than fear. Not pleading. Not bargaining. The look of a man taking the measure of a turn in his day.

"If I give it to you," he said, "what then."

"You sit at that desk. You do not stand up for a full count of two hundred. I leave."

"And if I do not give it to you."

"Then I take it. And you sit at that desk anyway."

She said it without heat. The flatness of it was the argument.

He reached into his collar — slowly, his hand open to show her he was not reaching for anything else — and he drew up a cord from which hung a single iron key with a square bow. He lifted the cord over his head. He laid it on the ledger.

"The girl is worth something to you," he said.

"No," Vane said. "She isn't."

She took the key. She did not put the cord over her head. She put the cord in her pocket with her thumb on the key so she would know by touch it was still there. She backed out of the room the way she had come in, watching him, and she went down the stairs without her weight registering on the fourth step or the seventh.

---

The boy was where she had left him. His eyes tracked her as she crossed the hall.

The door at the end of the hall was stone-framed, iron-banded, locked. The lock was old — an old wardlock whose key was the one Deren had handed her. She tried it. It turned.

She opened the door and did not go down immediately. She stood at the top of the stairs and listened.

Copper-flower.

It came up the stairwell at volume, the same smell from four days ago, biological, thick, the scent Bloom made when it was not yet the wafer. Her body — her professional instruments, her fieldwork body, the body she had spent ten years and four years of Bloom refining into the tool she had used for everything — reacted to the smell with the small pulse of *yes.* The tremor in her fingertips stilled for the length of a breath. Her shoulders went loose.

She marked the reaction. She did not act on it.

She went down.

---

The corridor was the corridor she had been seeing for months. She did not have to orient to it. Her body knew where the lamps were.

The room at the end. The door, ajar. The same pallet. The same low ceiling. The same condensation trays on the same table.

The woman was awake.

That was new. Four days ago she had been in the induced sleep, her breathing thick and slow, her fingers twitching in her dreams. Today her eyes were open. They tracked Vane as she entered the room with the same slow deliberate movement that a small animal used when it was deciding whether you were a danger it could outrun.

The marks were the same. The puncture sites at the insides of both elbows and at the hollow of her throat, still flushed and faintly raised — worked more recently, too, the inflammation fresher at the right elbow than it had been four days ago. The small white scar at the corner of her mouth was the same. She was thinner than she had been four days ago. Four days was not long enough for hunger to have caused that; it was the slow sustained thinness of someone whose body was being tapped at a rate that exceeded her ability to replace what was taken.

Vane crossed the room. She did not speak yet. She knelt beside the pallet.

The woman's breathing altered at her approach — not into fear, not into hope, but into a held stillness, the held stillness of a person who had learned that hope and fear were both expensive and had stopped spending.

*Junie.* The private word from four days ago, still there in Vane's head, still doing no work but the one work of making it impossible to pretend the person on the pallet was not a person.

"Can you walk," Vane said.

The woman's throat worked. "A little."

Her voice was dry and cracked and small. She had not used it often.

"I'm getting you out of this room. We are going to move slowly. If you have to stop, tell me. If anyone is in the hall, I will handle it. Do you understand."

"Why."

The word was not accusation. It was exhaustion putting forward the last honest question it had.

Vane took the restraint at the woman's right wrist between her fingers and began to unbuckle it. Her fingers were steady enough for the work. The buckle was padded in clean cloth. Whoever changed the cloth kept things clean.

"Because the supply chain I was in ends with you on that pallet," Vane said. She did not look up from the buckle. "I'm not asking you to come with me. I'm asking you to come up the stairs and into the street. Once you're in the street, what happens next is yours. If you want to go, go. If you want to come with me to a place where you can sit down and be given water and not be chained to a pallet for some hours, I can give you that too. I have one friend in this city. She will not touch you without your leaving. Her room is two streets from here."

The woman watched her. Her eyes were grey-green and very tired.

"Who are you."

"I've been using you," Vane said. "For four years. Not you. Someone like you. Now I'm stopping."

"And this is stopping."

"This is stopping."

The woman closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them, her pupils had contracted, and some small coiled thing in her had uncoiled, and she lifted her free wrist for Vane to unbuckle the second restraint herself.

---

They went up the stairs slowly. The woman leaned on Vane's right arm. Vane held her up with the bark-skin side of her body, the side that felt least like skin, which was the side she used for loads she did not want to feel through. The woman weighed almost nothing.

The boy in the hall watched them pass. His eyes had the wet shine of a person who had not known, exactly, what lived behind the door at the end of the hall, and who was now putting it together and would not sleep tonight.

At the street door Vane paused. She listened. Cart traffic. A boy shouting about eels. A woman laughing. No rhythm that did not belong.

She opened the door.

The light was hard after the cellar. The woman flinched and then did not flinch, the flinch overridden by a deeper stillness, the way a body held a pain it did not yet have energy to acknowledge.

"This way," Vane said.

They crossed the street at the pace of a sister supporting a sister who had had too much at breakfast. Vane had practiced the pace. She did not know whether the woman had practiced anything in a long time, but the woman matched her.

They turned off Lintel at the first alley and kept going.

---

Three blocks in, at a small public square with a dry fountain, the woman stopped.

"I'm not coming with you," she said. "To your friend."

"All right."

"I have a cousin in the fishing villages south. Two days on the coast road. I have a coin in my shoe that my mother sewed in before they took me. I have been saving it for this."

Vane looked at her. The woman looked back, and her face had in it the thin hard clarity of a person who had been composing this moment for longer than she had any right to.

"I do not know you," the woman said. "I do not want to know you. I thank you and I am going."

"Can I give you anything."

The woman considered.

"A coat."

Vane took her own coat off her shoulders and put it around the woman's. It was too large. The bark-skin on her right arm met the air and the air met the bark-skin and the shiver she had not let herself have caught her once and passed.

"A name," the woman said. "For the road. In case someone asks. Not yours. Mine. I need one."

"Junie," Vane said. It came out before she thought about it.

The woman looked at her.

"That's not mine."

"I know."

"But I'll use it."

She adjusted the coat across her shoulders. She turned. She walked across the square without hurry and disappeared into the mouth of the alley on the far side.

Vane watched her go.

She stayed until she could not hear the woman's steps.

---

She walked.

Not the long way this time. A direct line through the Crafting Quarter, past the Inkwalk, east along a warehouse row, three turns to clear the residual tailing she had not seen but was not going to trust the absence of, and up to the rooming house.

Halfway there the shiver came back. The air was cool; the morning had warmed but not enough. Without the coat her right arm was exposed from shoulder to wrist — the bark-skin a continuous grain from nearly the shoulder-joint down almost to the bend of her wrist, the newest edge thickest just below the ridge of her shoulder where this season's advance had run the grain toward the collarbone. Visible for anyone to see. She pulled her sleeve down as far as it would go over the bark and held it there with her thumb.

The patch at the back of her right wrist felt different under her thumb.

Not the raised dark grain she had been closing on with a thumb for months. A drier patch. Slightly flaking at one edge. The whorls less tight. She pressed it, and the texture gave a little under the press — a sand-grain tactility, minor, minor — and then she released it and kept walking, because she was not ready to ask her body what it was telling her.

*Data,* she thought. *Just data. Put it in the log and keep moving.*

It was not moving her the way observations usually moved her. It was resisting filing. She wanted to feel it as absolution, and the wanting was what made her suspect it; she wanted to feel it as cost, and the cost was not yet legible.

She kept walking.

---

By the time she reached the rooming house her stomach had turned twice. A mild nausea. The light was too bright at the angle the street lay at. Her hearing was not right — not loud, not muffled, but misallocated, some frequencies standing out and others missing, as if someone had lifted a few of the middle strings off a harp and the chords she knew were coming back to her with the holes where those strings had been.

The shakes were in both hands.

*Hour four,* she thought. *Withdrawal begins with a forward edge. Nausea. Tremor. Sensory misallocation. Standard presentation.*

She catalogued it. The cataloguing was itself a reduced version of the thing she used to do with her cataloguing — not the clean analytical filing that had once served her, but the effortful pretense of it, the form of filing she had learned so well it continued to function even as the function underneath it failed.

That was withdrawal too. It came for the tools first.

She climbed the stairs.

---

Maren was at the table. The note was in her hand. She did not stand when Vane came in. She looked at her.

"I went back," Vane said. Her voice came out steady. That part still worked. "She's out. She's gone south. She doesn't want to come with us and I didn't try to change her mind."

Maren set the note down.

"How bad."

"Starting."

"Tell me."

Vane went to her bed and sat. The weight of sitting was a relief that registered in her knees before it registered anywhere else. She laid her hands flat on her thighs the way she had seen Aldric do last night in the cooperage basement — a borrowed gesture, she noted, because her body had not been making gestures of its own today and had been borrowing from what was in the room.

"Nausea. Tremor. Hearing's off. I'll be less functional for —" she stopped, because she did not know for how long, and saying an estimate she did not have would have cost her something she could not afford to lose. "I'll be less functional."

"How long before you're flat."

"Three days. Four."

"Before you're back."

"Weeks. Maybe a month. The literature is —" She almost said *thin.* She said: "inconclusive."

Maren nodded. Her face did the small thing it did when she was composing herself around information she did not like. It did not hide the composing. That was one of the things about her that kept Vane off-balance: Maren did not hide her adjustments, and the not-hiding was somehow the toughest part of her.

"Horst," Maren said.

"Closing. Two days. Three at the outside."

"Then we leave tomorrow."

It was not a question. Maren had read the shape of the situation before Vane arrived and had already done the arithmetic, and the arithmetic had landed where Vane's had landed, and the fact of their both landing in the same place was the most useful thing that had happened in this room in a week.

"Tomorrow," Vane said. "The hill is five days east."

Maren's eyes held hers.

"The Keeper?" Maren said.

"He'll come."

"You're certain."

"His hand stopped a span from the disc last night. He isn't going home."

Maren absorbed this. She did not ask how Vane had read it through a basement window from the gate. It was sufficient that Vane had read it.

"Davan?"

"Davan will come."

"All right."

The two words. The two words Maren had used with Vane in this room four nights ago when Vane had said *I'm stopping.* They arrived with the same weight now. Something had settled.

Vane let her head down against the headboard.

"Water," she said. "If there's water."

Maren brought water. Vane drank it. The shake of her hand made the cup ring against her teeth, and she set the cup down with the care of a woman setting down an object she no longer trusted herself to hold.

---

The afternoon went. She did not track it well.

At some point Maren went out and came back with a packet of dried ginger root and a small sack of something from the herbalist at the end of the street. At some point a message went — Vane saw Maren write it and saw a boy come for it at the door — to the cooperage, for Davan, or for Marrick, or for both. At some point the message came back: one word on a folded paper, which Maren read and put into her pocket without showing Vane.

Vane dozed and did not dream, which was a small mercy. When she woke the light had changed. Evening. Maren was at the table repacking a traveling bag with the economy of someone who had packed it several times in her life already.

Vane watched her from the bed. Her right arm, uncovered by sleep, lay across the blanket. She looked at the bark-skin in the dusk light.

The patch at the back of her wrist was the same as it had been on the walk home. Drier. A little looser at one edge.

She did not look closer. She looked for the length of a breath and looked away.

It was not for her to examine yet. Looking at it too hard would be an asking, and what she was asking for she was not entitled to ask for, and the bark-skin — she had learned — responded to expectation the way some things did. She would not offer it an expectation. She would let it do what it was doing and not name it until the thing had a shape she could name without flattering herself.

She turned the arm under the blanket.

---

Davan came at dusk.

He came up the stairs quietly and knocked quietly and Maren let him in. He looked at Vane and he did not say anything about how she looked. The wash of color at his collar was blue, calm, and held without fluctuation. She appreciated that. Calm from him was worth more than reassurance from most people.

"Edvar's bringing his trunk," he said. "The face goes with us. He won't leave it behind."

"Horse?" Maren said.

"He has a pair. And a cart. He says the cart doesn't go past the ford but we'll figure what to do then."

"Marrick is coming."

"He's coming. Tela's riding decoy through the east gate. One of the Inkwalk boys with her."

Vane turned her face toward the wall. Her eyes had started to water for no reason she could name. She closed them.

"Aldric?" Maren said.

"Aldric's at the cooperage tonight. He's going to lodge there until we move. He — he asked Marrick if the iron-banded trunk was a burden he should take on his horse because he has a stronger horse than Marrick's."

"Did Marrick agree."

"He agreed."

Vane heard them talking. Their voices were a distance away that was not spatial. She kept her eyes closed and let the talk go on over her, and she catalogued the textures of the voices — Maren's flat-edge steadiness, Davan's open-grained circling that returned to points without forcing them, the silence between sentences where each of them was thinking — and the cataloguing was a comfort and a loss in the same gesture.

She had used to be able to pick up this much from a smell across a room. Tonight she was working from what was being said. The tool she had been for ten years, the instrument she had sharpened and overclocked with the Bloom, was going.

*Not going,* she corrected. *Retrograding. Back to what it was before. My nose. Which still works. Just not for what I've been asking it to do.*

The correction was a kindness she allowed herself. She did not fully believe it. It would become truer over weeks. She would have to wait.

---

They went on talking. At some point Davan laid a hand briefly on the blanket near her knee — not on her knee, on the blanket, the gesture of a man placing a warmth where a warmth could be taken or not taken — and she did not reach for it, and he did not move it. He left it there for the length of a breath and then took it away, and the fact that he had not required anything of her for putting it there was one of the things she filed without commentary, because he had now done several things she would have filed without commentary if she had been keeping a file on him, and it was becoming clear that she was.

He went at second bell. The door closed behind him. Maren turned the lamp down.

---

She lay in the dark a long time.

The plan was in her head, exactly, the way plans had always been in her head: five days east along the highland road; the ford crossing, not the east gate; morning departure; four riders, one cart, a trunk with a face in it, a disc in a small dark-haired woman's coat pocket, a Keeper whose hand had stopped at a span from the answer and was now traveling with them toward the longer answer; and somewhere behind them, at a distance she had estimated and would re-estimate every morning, a professional she had trained with for three seasons in a previous life, closing on a city that would empty of their scent by noon tomorrow and would start to empty of her scent the moment they crossed the ford.

She would not smell him coming. That was the cost. She had been the one who smelled, and now she would not.

She thought about that for a while, and then she thought about the woman in the square, the too-large coat, the cousin south along the coast road, the coin a mother had sewn into a shoe against an unreachable future and the daughter who had carried that shoe and that coin through years of captivity and had walked out today with them and with a coat and with a name that was not hers.

Then she thought about her arm. Just once. Under the blanket.

The drier patch was still drier. The edge was still loose. The body underneath the grain was doing something she had not asked it to do, and the something was not nothing, and it was not for her tonight to decide what kind of something it was.

She put her good hand over the bark-skin through the blanket, not pressing, only covering, and she lay there with her hand on her arm and listened to Maren's breath in the other bed even out and deepen into sleep. She listened to the street sounds through the shutter. A late carter. A dog. A drunk somewhere singing a song she had last heard on a road eleven years ago.

*Tomorrow,* she thought. *Four of us. Five days east. A cart, a trunk, a disc, a plan.*

The patch on the back of her right wrist, unexamined under her palm, flaked a little more overnight. She knew this without looking. She would know it when she washed in the morning. She would not mention it.

She slept after a while, shallow, the body insisting on its minimum.

The three moons were over the harbor outside, at their stations, doing what they had always done.

She did not see them. She was under a roof. She slept, and the roof held, and the plan was a quiet shape in her chest, and the flake at the edge of the bark-patch was a smaller quieter shape under her palm, and between the two the night passed and the city breathed and tomorrow came on.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Three — *Maren*
### East

---

Morning came grey and cold, the kind of cold a harbor city put down
before dawn — salt in it, no friendliness. Maren was awake before the
second bell. She had been awake in small pieces for most of the night,
surfacing and settling and surfacing again, and when the bell went she
opened her eyes and stopped pretending.

Vane was already up.

She stood at the window with her back to the room, dressed, hair tied
back, coat over her shoulders. She did not turn when Maren sat up. She
was doing something with her hands at the sill — the economy of the
motion told Maren what she had already guessed. Vane was repacking a
small thing for the third or fourth time. She would do it again.

"Water's cold," Vane said, without turning. "I didn't build a fire. The
room won't smell of us by noon."

"All right."

---

Maren dressed in layers. The coat Ulla had given her went on last. The
disc was in its inner pocket, where it had been for three months now, and
she checked the pocket with her knuckle the way she checked a knife: not
to see, only to confirm. The disc was warm against her knuckle through
the wool. Faintly, more than it had been a week ago. She noted it and
went on.

She catalogued the room before she left it. Two beds, one stripped.
Basin empty. A coin for the landlady flat on the table. Nothing in the
crack between the boards. Nothing under the bed.

She had slept here for three weeks. She had not become used to it. She
had become less tense in it, which was not the same thing.

"Pack," Vane said.

Maren picked up her pack.

---

They went down to the street by the back stairs.

The back stairs gave onto an alley that gave onto a lane that gave onto
a bridge across one of the lesser canals, and the canal ran south-east
toward the ford. Vane had chosen the way. She had not described it
before. She described it now in a half-sentence as they turned the first
corner.

"Three crossings. Alley. Bridge. Boatyard. If I stop, you stop. If I keep
walking, you keep walking."

"All right."

They walked.

The city at this hour was carters and bakers and a thin scatter of
people on morning shifts. The light was still in the west behind the
ridge. The canal lamps had not been put out yet — tired yellow, wicks
kept up out of habit. The canal smell was up.

Maren walked two paces behind Vane. She had put herself there because
Vane was scouting.

Vane was scouting differently this morning. Her head was not turning at
the rate it usually turned. Her stride was even, but it was a stride
chosen to be even — a slightly-too-deliberate pace, a body not trusting
itself to move on instinct and putting a measure on the movement
instead. Her right hand was at her coat pocket, not inside it. Her
thumb rode the seam and used the seam to know where the hand was without
having to look.

Maren did not comment.

---

At the boatyard they met the others.

Edvar's cart stood at the inland end of the yard, hitched to a pair of
shaggy short-legged horses. Small cart — two-wheeled, high-sided, a
board-and-canvas job built for a mapmaker who had not meant to travel.
The iron-banded trunk was in the bed, roped down against the front
board, a tarp over it. Two water-skins and what was probably blankets
beside it.

Edvar was on the bench. Tela was not with him. Edvar's face had the
settled look of a man who had argued the question and closed it, and
a look like that did not invite re-opening.

Aldric was on foot beside a deep-chested bay, older than she had first
taken it for. Saddlebags packed flat against the leather. A bedroll. A
small sealed case she would have bet was a letter-kit. He wore a plain
dark travel coat without a cassock under it. He looked cold and alert
and a little awkward in his body — a man who had not chosen plain clothes
for thirty years, moving in them with the small stiffness of a body
learning to occupy itself without its professional scaffolding.

He inclined his head to Maren. More than a nod, less than a bow — a
pastoral gesture flattened into a traveller's greeting. She returned it
as a nod. He registered the return and added nothing.

Davan was at the back of the cart adjusting a strap. His skin at the
collar did the small thing it did when he looked up and found her — a
wash of blue-through-green that held and then settled to the low steady
blue he had been holding since the cooperage. He lifted the hand with
the strap in it. She lifted her chin in return.

Vane had already gone past all of them to the far end of the yard. She
was looking at the lane that ran from the yard to the river road. She
was standing very still.

---

Edvar clucked to the horses. The cart began to roll.

They went out of the boatyard in a loose line. Vane first, on foot, ahead
by ten paces. Aldric leading his bay behind Vane, not mounted yet. Then
the cart. Then Davan walking beside the near wheel with his hand on the
sideboard, not carrying anything, his pack already stowed under the tarp
with the trunk.

Maren walked in the middle, next to the cart on the off-wheel side. She
had not been told to. She had walked to that position because the cart
was on her left and the road-side was on her right, and the disc was on
her inside, and anyone wanting to reach her from either side would have
to cross something first — a wheel, an elbow, a horse's haunch. The
arithmetic was so automatic that she registered it only after she had
already arranged herself to it.

The ford was an hour's walk east.

---

The ford crossing was not the east gate.

The east gate ran a toll house and a ledger. The ledger got read. The
ford was gravel and a plank walkway on pilings, and a road on the far
side that climbed through cow pasture to the highland road. No
toll-house. No ledger.

Two riders had gone through the east gate two hours earlier under papers
Marrick had drawn up — decoys, Tela among them, Maren understood without
having asked. They would ride north at the first side-road and come back
by the river without actually leaving.

"If anyone watches the gate," Vane had said last night, "they'll see
what they came to see."

Maren was inside the lie, and the lie was a lie in her favor. She walked.

---

They crossed the plank walkway in single file.

The river was low. The gravel showed in long pale tongues on either side
of the pilings. A woman was in the river to her knees to the north,
washing something in a wooden tub. She did not look up. A cart ahead of
theirs was crossing in the other direction, a hay-wagon with its load
tied slack, the driver sitting high on the bench with his hat pulled
down against a cold that the morning was only now arranging into itself.
The driver nodded at Edvar as the two carts passed. Edvar nodded back
with the professional courtesy of carters who did not know each other
and did not particularly want to and who were not going to add to either
of their days by pretending otherwise.

Vane crossed last. She stopped in the middle of the planks and looked
south toward the harbor mouth and the ships at anchor along the eastern
quays. Two breaths' worth of stillness. Her right hand went inside the
coat pocket for the first time all morning and came out again with
nothing in it.

Then she walked the rest of the crossing.

---

On the far side of the ford the road climbed.

It climbed gently. A pasture road, rutted by cart wheels and the hooves
of the sheep that had been driven down this way to market for centuries.
The pasture fences were stone, not wood. The stones were old — lichen-grey
at their tops, their mortarless joints fitted by the slow patient work of
hands that had gone back into the ground. A rook called once from the far
side of a field and nothing answered it.

When the road came out of the last pasture gate and on to the highland
road proper, the air changed. The canal smell dropped off. The harbor
smell dropped off. What was left was grass, and the stone of the walls,
and the faint cold top-note of a winter that had not arrived but had
begun its arriving — a dryness at the back of the nose, an emptiness
the air took on before frost.

Maren had grown up with that air.

She let it into her lungs and did not say anything about it.

---

By mid-morning they were walking through upland pasture, and the city
was a grey smear behind the last ridge, and Vane had dropped back to
walk alongside the cart.

Not alongside Maren. Alongside the back wheel, at Davan's shoulder. She
walked at his pace for the length of a field, then dropped to the back
of the cart, then moved forward again, then to the side Maren was on.

She was reading the road. Vane's way of reading a road was not by
looking at it but by moving along it at different positions and letting
the road give her what it was going to give her from each angle. What
she was reading for would not be a footprint. It would be a shape.
Something moving at her pace at a distance. Something that kept its pace
when she changed hers.

Vane fell in at Maren's elbow.

"Left shoulder aches," Vane said. Quietly. Conversationally.

"Do you need —"

"No. Just telling you."

The telling-you was a kindness. Vane did not talk about her body
unprompted, ever. That she had said it at all meant she was saying it
for a reason — that the shoulder ache was something Maren should log so
that later, when other things showed up alongside it, Maren would not be
starting from zero.

"All right," Maren said.

Vane's eyes stayed on the road ahead. Her right hand was, again, not
inside the pocket. The seam was where she was keeping it.

"Nothing on our back yet," Vane said. "Long sightline across that last
pasture. I looked. Nothing."

"All right."

She dropped back to the rear of the cart.

---

They stopped at midday at a wayside trough — a stone trough sunk half
into the hill at a bend, fed by a pipe that went up into the slope
somewhere and delivered water cold enough to make the horses stamp. A
pair of sheep in the next field watched the humans at the trough with
the distant insulted attention of sheep watching people.

Edvar got down stiffly from the bench and rolled his shoulders. Aldric
held the horses' heads while they drank. Davan sat on a stone at the
edge of the road and took a piece of bread out of his coat pocket and
broke it in half and held out a half.

Maren sat beside him and took the bread.

She bit into it. It tasted of the oven and of an hour that had passed
without her in it.

Her father had carried bread east ten years ago.

She caught the thought and set it aside. Not away. Aside. She had been
doing this with the thought of her father all morning — let the thought
come, set it aside, come back to what was in front of her: the bread,
the shape of Vane ten paces up the road keeping a sightline east,
Aldric murmuring to a horse, Davan eating beside her with his
skin-collar at the steady low blue he had been at since the boatyard.

Davan did not ask how she was.

She appreciated that.

She ate her bread. When she had swallowed the last bite she wiped her
fingers on the edge of her coat and stood up, because the cart had
finished at the trough and the afternoon's walk had started.

---

The disc pulsed.

An hour into the afternoon, on a stretch of road where pasture had given
over to upland heath and the wind had come up steady out of the north.
She was walking with her hand inside her coat, a habit she had started
in the city and had not unmade, her knuckle against the inside pocket
where the disc sat.

The pulse came.

Not a jump in temperature. A kind of *arriving* of the warmth — the
disc remembering, briefly, that it was a warm object, then forgetting
again. Through her knuckle, up her wrist, gone before she would have
been able to say aloud that it had happened.

She did not take her hand out. She looked at the road.

*Two pulses today,* she thought. *First was in the dark before the bell.
This is the second.*

It was more than yesterday. Not by much. Yesterday had been one pulse at
sunset and one just after sleep. The day before that had been one. The
day before that had been the cooperage.

She catalogued the frequency and did not allow herself to draw a
conclusion from it. Drawing a conclusion from it was work for a night
with a fire in front of her and nothing to decide in the next hour. Right
now she was walking east. The disc was walking with her. Both of them
were warmer than they had been yesterday. That was the fact.

She walked.

---

Vane came up beside her twenty minutes later.

Not on the cart side. On the road side, at her right elbow, the position
Vane took when she wanted to say a thing without the cart hearing. The
two of them walked in step for a moment.

"How often," Vane said.

Maren did not pretend.

"Twice today. Both times it's been warmer than before."

"Inside."

"Under my knuckle."

Vane nodded once. "Through the coat?"

"Through the coat. Not through the coat to anyone else. Me only. Knuckle
only."

"Good."

Vane did not ask anything else. She walked beside Maren for a few more
paces and then let her pace drop and let the cart catch her and passed
around the back of it and came up on the other side by Davan. Davan, for
his part, had gone still on the road side — which was, Maren saw after
the fact, a stillness in response to something his body had read and
hers had not, because Davan's empathic sense was closer to the ground
than any measurement Maren had, and the disc had pulsed, and Davan had
felt the pulse as a weather he did not have a name for.

He did not look at her. He did not speak.

His skin at the collar had gone from the low steady blue to a low steady
green.

She was not meant to see it. She had seen it anyway. The seeing went
aside, with the thought of her father, for later.

---

Aldric fell in beside her in the last hour before dusk.

He had been walking ahead, beside his horse, not mounted. He had not
mounted the bay all day. Deference, she had assumed — a Keeper of thirty
years did not enter a group of four people who were walking by mounting
his horse to the head of the line.
He had been walking his horse the way Edvar was driving his cart: at the
pace the group set.

Now he slowed until the cart caught him, and he let the cart pass, and
he matched her stride.

For the space of two hundred yards he said nothing.

Maren did not look at him. She had learned in the cooperage basement
that Aldric's silences were working silences, and the working was not
hers to interrupt. She walked.

At the two-hundredth yard he spoke.

"I have been counting," he said.

The sentence arrived the way his sentences did — formally, with the
first word doing more work than most people asked of a first word. She
had heard him speak two nights ago at the cooperage for the first time.
She had catalogued his cadence then. It was with her now. She recognized
the opening move.

"Counting what," she said.

"Moons."

"Three."

"Yes."

He walked. He was not looking at the sky. The sky above the heath had
begun to do the thing it did at this hour at this season — a high clear
winter-blue flattening toward the horizon, and in the east the clear
pale edge of a moon not yet risen but gathering.

"My counting has been a professional occupation of mine for two months,"
Aldric said. "I have been counting what my office taught me was not
countable. I have not found the counting useful. I mention this only
because I am not going to count aloud while we walk, and I wanted you to
know it is not because I am not counting."

"All right."

The two words. She said them the way she said them in rooms where
something had settled at depth. Whether something had settled at depth
in this exchange she would find out later.

Aldric walked with her for another hundred yards, not counting aloud.
Then he fell back to the cart and took a word with Edvar about the
bay's shoe, and the cart's pace adjusted a degree, and Maren understood
without being told that he had come up to do a piece of work and had
done it and had left. The work was arrival. He had needed one sentence
with her on a road in daylight that did not involve an object on a
table.

She filed it. She walked.

---

They camped at dusk in a sheep-fold Edvar had known about — a
dry-stone enclosure in the lee of a rise, three walls standing and one
fallen, a dry trough at the open end. A stand of stunted hawthorns
inside the ring, branches bare and north-bent. No sheep in it for a
season.

Edvar drew the cart up to the fallen wall. Aldric tethered the horses
at the windward side of the hawthorns. Davan came back from the stream
with two water-skins. Vane walked the perimeter once, stood a long
moment on a rise a hundred paces beyond the fold, and came back in
without reporting what she had seen.

Maren laid the fire.

She had done this often enough on the road north that her hands did it
without her supervision. Tinder. Sticks. A lean of larger ones. Steel
against flint. She built it small. The fold walls would hold the light
in, but the light of a fire carried at night in country like this, and
Vane had not spoken but Maren had understood.

The five of them gathered around it.

Edvar on the stone trough with his knees up like a man half his age.
Aldric on a folded blanket with his back against the dry-stone wall.
Davan cross-legged on the ground, his back to the open side of the fold,
keeping the lookout position without being asked. Maren opposite him
with the fire between them, her shoulders against the stand of
hawthorns. Vane at the end of the fire on the windward side, closer to
the flame than the others, her coat wrapped around her shoulders and her
hands inside the cuffs.

Vane kept moving closer to the fire. Maren did not comment.

---

They did not talk much.

Edvar sighed once, the sigh of a man at the end of a day he had agreed
to take on and had not regretted yet but might. Aldric took out a small
book from his coat — not a journal; an old traveler's almanac — and
opened it on his knee and did not read it. The book was a prop that
allowed him to keep his hands occupied while his thoughts did the work
his hands were not required for.

Davan carved.

He had a piece of pale wood between his hands that he had picked up at
the trough stop and had not shown to her at the time. It was hawthorn,
cut clean. His knife was small and steady. He carved without watching
what he carved. He had done this on the road north, she knew — had
carved something of her before he had ever seen her, a pine figure with
one arm held close to the body. The carving she had held in the
cooperage yard and had said nothing about for a long time.

She did not know what he was carving now. She did not ask. The steady
small sound of the blade on wood was, in the fold, a warmer sound than
the fire.

Vane was quiet.

She was closer to the fire than was useful, and her hands inside her
cuffs were shaking finely at the fingertips — Maren could see it in the
motion of the cuff-fabric. Vane's eyes were open, on the fire. Her jaw
was set the way a jaw set when a body was running a small internal
current and the jaw was one of the places the current surfaced.

Maren did not go over. Going over would have been a gesture, and Vane's
body was not asking for a gesture. Maren had watched her mother work
through things like this — not withdrawal, her mother had had other
demons, but the same shape. A body working through something alone in
company. The company mattered. To her mother, the alone had mattered
more.

Maren laid another stick on the fire. She set it carefully on the
leeward side so the fire wouldn't flare. The flame took it.

---

After a while Aldric closed the almanac.

"I will take the first watch," he said. "If the group will have it."

He did not look at Vane when he said it. He did not look at Maren. He
had pitched the sentence to the fire and let the sentence do its own
choosing of whom it belonged to. A Keeper's move — and also, the shape
of it arriving a beat late, the move of a man in a group in which
nobody would delegate to him, who was finding his footing by the oldest
move he had: volunteer for a duty before anyone asked him to take it.

He was not trying to lead. He was trying not to be waited on. The
effort of it was audible in the sentence.

Vane did not look up from the fire.

"Wake me at second watch," she said. Her voice came out even. The thing
in her hands did not show in the voice. "I'll take the dead hour before
dawn."

"The dead hour is hard," Aldric said.

"I know."

"I will wake you."

Davan did not volunteer. Maren did not volunteer. Edvar, already,
through the silence that followed, had begun to breathe the slow
evening breath of a man asleep against a wall.

That was the first watch sorted.

The second watch, Maren understood, was Vane's claim on doing work she
could still do. Maren had been making claims like that on the road
north, in the days after the ditch, when her ankle had been bad and she
had wanted to walk the second shift to prove to herself she could. The
claim was not about the watch. It was about not becoming, yet, the
person who was being worked around.

Maren filed the parallel. She did not extend it aloud.

---

Before she slept she looked at the sky once.

The three moons were up.

The Steady was low over the northern ridge, pale as milk-water. The
rust-tinted one was higher, east and smaller. The fastest had come
over the shoulder of the rise in the last hour and stood near the
zenith, small and hard and bright. Three. At their stations. Doing
whatever it was moons did, above a country that had stopped asking.

She looked at them without counting.

She had been avoiding the moons for three years, since the first night
on the road — eyes low, hood up, attention on the ground. A habit.
Avoidance was always a habit eventually. Whether tonight she was
breaking it or only putting it down for a minute to rest her neck she
did not know.

The disc, under her hand through the coat, pulsed once.

Three pulses today. More than yesterday. More than the day before.

Her father had walked under these moons ten years ago and had not come
back. He had carried this disc and left it behind for her to carry
after him, and she had carried it three years thinking she was carrying
grief, and then she had sat in a basement and understood she was
carrying a test, and now she was on a road east, first day of five,
with a cart and a trunk and a Keeper and a hunter in withdrawal and a
woodcarver who had read her before she knew she had been read, and the
disc under her hand was warmer than yesterday, and the
warmer-than-yesterday was not a sentence she had finished yet.

She was not walking toward a grave. She had stopped walking toward a
grave at the cooperage. What she was walking toward she did not yet
know. She had thought in the basement that she would arrive at a shape
for it before she left the city. She had not. The reframing was still
happening, the way some changes went on happening in the body for weeks
after the event that caused them, and the body was hers, and she did
not get to hurry it.

*All right,* she thought. Not said. The two words to herself, the
private register.

Not all right. Arranged. Put down.

She turned her face from the moons and put her head against the pack
she had rolled up for a pillow. The fire was low. Aldric was at the
wall on first watch, almanac closed on his knee, eyes on the open side
of the fold. Vane was on the far side of the fire with her hands in
her cuffs and her jaw set. Davan was already asleep, the hawthorn
piece finished in his palm.

Edvar snored once and settled.

Four pulses.

She had counted the fourth without meaning to. It had come in the last
breath before sleep, under her hand, against her ribs. Not loud. Not
urgent. Only, every few hours, the disc remembering itself a degree
further than it had remembered itself yesterday, and she the one
carrying the rememberer, and the road five days long, and she had come
one day.

She slept.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Four — *Davan*
### The Country Changes

---

The fold was colder at first light than it had been in the night — the
way stone cold went, getting further into itself in the small hours and
taking until the sun cleared the rise to admit it was morning. Davan
woke before anyone but Vane. Vane was up already, a shape against the
dry-stone wall where she had been on second watch, her hands inside her
cuffs the way she had held them at the fire.

She did not look at him when his eyes opened. She was making a short
efficient motion at her wrist with her other hand — tucking a sleeve up
over a bit of something that was nobody's business but hers — and she
finished the motion before she registered him. The sleeve came down.
Her hands went back inside the cuffs.

Something had come off her skin and settled on the cuff. Pale. A flake.
Smaller than a thumbnail. He saw it and looked past it and did not know
he had seen it until later, when the shape of it was already in his
carving hand.

He rolled his blanket. He stood up. His shoulders knew the shape of
sleep on cold ground and complained of it without making much of the
complaint.

Across the fold, Aldric was crouched by the ash of the fire, testing
with a fingertip whether the coals would come back under a handful of
dry grass. They would. He had put grass ready the night before. Davan
watched him work and thought, not for the first time since the basement,
that there was a man who knew how to be useful without being in the way.

---

They broke camp in the old-motion manner of a party that had not been
one long enough to have bad habits yet.

Edvar harnessed the horses, one shoulder into the off-side leather
because his left was stiff from the bench the day before. Aldric rolled
Maren's tarp with her while she stowed the fire stones. Vane walked the
perimeter of the fold once, then once again on a line a hundred paces
further out. Davan stowed the water-skins. He put his hand against the
wheel-hub as he passed the cart, because the touch told him whether the
iron was warm or cold, and cold was the right answer at this hour.

He caught himself at it. He did not name what the catching felt like.
It was too early in the day to be naming anything.

The country east of the fold began as upland pasture and did not stay
upland pasture for long. By the time the cart was moving it was
already a different country.

---

He walked at Edvar's off-wheel for the first hour.

They had fallen into the same loose line as the day before. Vane
ahead, on foot. Aldric and the bay just behind her, the horse bearing
nothing but tack and the small sealed letter-case. The cart in the
middle, Edvar on the bench. Maren on the off-side; Davan on the near
side at the wheel.

The road climbed gently. Stone broke through the grass in bigger
patches now. The walls along the field margins went from field-clearing
walls, wide and rubble-filled, to older walls — narrower, tighter-fitted,
built by hands that had cared about the fit and had not been in a hurry.

He ran a finger along one of them at a gate as they passed. The stones
were cold. The cold went into his fingertip and did not travel. Old
stone did not give warmth back. It took warmth away slowly and politely
and handed it down into the earth.

He walked on.

Maybe it was —

He did not finish the thought. It was too early. The shape had been
under his fingertip for the length of a touch, no longer.

---

By mid-morning the pasture was behind them.

The country opened out into the beginning of the eastern highlands.
Not yet heath. An upland of coarse tussock and bracken gone copper with
the season, grass combed in long pale sheets by the wind, rock breaking
the surface in slow irregular swells — bedrock, not scattered boulders,
the land's own bones coming up through the thin soil that covered them.

And the contour lines thickening.

Not in any map-sense. He had not looked at a map this morning. He meant
it in his body. The rises were closer together, and the ground under
his boots was not field-ground any more — it was the long muscled
shoulder of a country shaped by something larger than weather.

The wind came off the ridge ahead and brought no city in it. No fish.
No coal-smoke. Only dry grass, cold rock, and far underneath all of it
a note he did not know how to place — not a smell, exactly. A register.

His skin at the wrists did the slow thing it did when he had not named
what his skin was doing. The collar of his shirt had opened with the
stride, and the skin between the collar and his jaw had a greenish
cast. Not the green-gold of the night in Edvar's back room. A quieter
shade. Close to the skin. Not reaching for anything.

He did not cover it. Covering made it worse. He walked on at the pace
of the cart.

---

At the first short stop — the road bending around the foot of a tor
and opening a long sightline east for Vane to stand in — Davan went to
Maren.

The cart had been between them most of the morning. He had not minded.
There was a working rhythm to their not being in each other's pockets
that had been established in New Plymouth, and he had no interest in
breaking it. He came over now because she was holding her hand inside
her coat again, and he had been watching, without meaning to, the way
the hand went in and out of the coat across the morning.

She turned her face toward him as he stopped at her elbow. Her eyes
went to his collar, then back to the road.

"Warmer?" he said.

"Twice. Once before dawn. Once an hour ago."

"Same as yesterday, then. At the pulsing."

"A bit more. Warmer when it pulses."

He nodded. They stood a moment.

"There's something —" Davan said.

He let the word sit. Maren did not ask him to finish it. She was
reading his collar for him, because he was evidently not able to read
it for himself, and she nodded once, acknowledging what she saw, and
did not comment.

"Later," he said.

"All right."

He went back to the off-wheel. Her hand went back into the coat. The
cart began to roll. Vane came down off the tor and took her place at
the head of the line, and the country opened east, and he walked into
it with his skin doing its slow held green and the note underneath.

---

It was not a human note.

He had, for nine months now, been reading emotional residue in rooms —
the leavings of bodies that had felt something, the way a room could
smell of anger after the angry person had gone, the way the back bench
of a chapel held grief soaked into the wood. This was not that. This
was older. This was not human at all.

The road was old. The wall-stones along the road were older. The
bedrock under the wall-stones was older than either. What he was
reading was older than the bedrock — not in a geological sense, because
geological sense was not something his body had any instrument for, but
in a register sense. The way his proximity-sense had worked in the
cooperage with Corvel's container, the way it had worked in Edvar's
back room with the face — only bigger now, diffused across the ground,
and *under his feet.*

Near noon, he stopped at the verge and put his palm flat on an exposed
slab of rock. The rock was cold. The cold went into his palm the way
the stone at the wall had. But beneath the cold of the surface, not
quite reaching him and not quite failing to, was — the faintest —
something the stone did not have on its own account. A carrying. The
rock was carrying a thing that was not the rock.

He took his hand away. He walked on.

He did not tell Maren. He would tell her. He was circling. There was a
kind of saying that came from the circling, when the circling had gone
around enough times that the sentence came out clean, and he was not
there yet.

The skin at his wrists stayed green. Low. Steady.

---

They stopped at midday at a place Edvar had picked — a stone outcrop
by the verge that served as a table for travelers, a worn groove in its
top from generations of packs set down on it. A stream went past below.
Vane walked down, drank from her cupped hand, and walked back up
without turning her back to the road.

Edvar got down. Aldric unbuckled the bay's brow-band and let her ease
her head. Maren set her pack at the foot of the stone. She looked at
Davan across it, and she looked at Aldric, and something in her
decision-making went quiet in the way he had learned to recognize from
the cooperage basement.

She reached into the inside pocket of her coat. She took out the disc.
She set it on the stone.

The stone was light gray, pocked from weather, and the disc against it
was the dark it was — the dark that had taken the lamplight in Edvar's
back room and kept it, the dark that had stopped the lamplight at the
cooperage table. In daylight the effect was different. Under lamps the
disc had looked like an absence inside a ring of light. Out here it
looked like a window cut into the stone into a place the daylight did
not reach.

Aldric went still.

Davan had seen him go still in the basement — the hand that had not
closed the short distance, the hand that had stopped itself a handspan
away. This was the same stillness. It was the stillness of a man whose
body had just been shown the thing his body had travelled a thousand
miles to come near, and whose body was choosing not to move because
moving was not the answer yet.

Maren did not ask. She had asked once, two nights ago. She did not ask
twice. She went down to the stream to fill a skin.

Vane, at the upper edge of the outcrop, saw this arrangement and did
not comment. She moved thirty paces further up the road, took her
stand at a boulder, and watched east. Edvar took a piece of bread
out of the cart and broke it, and handed half to Davan without looking
at him, and sat on the cart step with his own half and ate slowly.

Aldric crossed to the stone.

He did not kneel. He leaned. His hands went flat against the top of
the stone on either side of the disc, not touching it, the way a man's
hands went flat against a lectern, and he looked at the disc at the
exact distance a man looked at the page on a lectern, and he looked at
it for a long time.

Davan had finished his bread before Aldric spoke.

---

"The inner orbit symbol," Aldric said.

His voice was level. It had the precise level of a voice that had held
a sentence for a very long time and had decided, now, to let it out in
the shape it had been kept in. Davan heard the shape of it — long
careful clauses, institutional vocabulary — and underneath the shape he
heard the other thing. A second voice under the first. A stumbling in
the places where the institutional words wanted to give way.

"I have been counting," Aldric said, "what it describes. For months."

He did not take his eyes off the disc. Edvar stopped chewing. Maren
was just coming back up the path from the stream, the skin heavy in
her hand, and she saw the shape of the moment and stopped at the edge
of it and did not step in.

"The moons," Aldric said. "Three in the sky. The fourth on the disc.
Four arcs. Three stations and a — " the word caught. He let the
catching stand. " — and a descent." He said the word like a man who
had known for weeks it was the right word and had only just now said
it aloud. "The fourth arc drifts off. I had taken it for damage in the
engraving. I do not take it for damage now."

"It was meant to drift," Davan said quietly.

"Yes."

"Drawn that way on purpose. The symbol is *before.* Before the —"

"Before the descent."

"Yes."

Aldric closed his eyes briefly.

When he opened them he was looking at Maren, not at the disc, and his
hands had lifted from the stone and folded themselves at his waist in
the small involuntary gesture of a man whose hands had remembered, for
a second, that they had been trained to fold. The folding looked
awkward on him in plain clothes. He saw that it looked awkward, and he
did not unfold, because unfolding would have been another thing.

"I have a thing to tell you," he said. "All of you. That I have been
holding." He glanced at Edvar. "Some of it Marrick knows. Some I have
not said."

Maren set the waterskin on the stone and sat on her pack. Edvar leaned
against the cart wheel with his bread in his hand. Vane did not come
in from her boulder. She did not need to. A voice pitched at the stone
would reach her, and her eyes would stay east.

Aldric spoke.

---

"There is a chamber," he said, "in the undercroft of the Temple of
Confluence. I found it eight weeks before I rode for Greenford."

He said *eight weeks* like a man giving testimony. A scholar gave a
duration that way; a priest gave a confession that way; and Aldric was
giving it both ways at once.

"Sealed for — I do not know how long. Long. The stone around the door
was cut before the temple above it. The door opened on a small room.
And in the room were five objects."

He paused.

"Four containers. Smooth. Dark. The same dark as the disc. The same
dark as —" he inclined his head minimally toward Edvar, and Edvar
nodded minimally back, and the acknowledgment moved between them in
the quiet shorthand of men who had spoken some of this already. "— as
other objects the group has seen. They stood against the wall in a row
like — " he paused again. "Like patient urns."

The phrase had lived in his head for months. He had never said it aloud.
Saying it now was itself a small crossing he did not take back.

"And the fifth object was a face."

Maren's hand, at her knee, did the small involuntary reaching he had
seen her hand do in the cooperage — a half-inch of motion and then
stillness. She brought the hand back onto her thigh.

"Flat," Aldric said. "Dark. The size of a large serving tray. A
surface that answered to a hand laid upon it. I laid my hand upon it
eight weeks ago and it — " the liturgical word came up into his mouth
before he could catch it, and he let it come because it was the word
he had, and because they would hear the other word under it " — it
*showed me.*"

He was speaking to all of them now.

"An arrangement of bodies. Four of them. Around a world — blue, green,
with cloud. A star at the center burning. The three near bodies each
with their mass. And the fourth —"

He stopped. He was looking at Davan now. He had heard something, or
seen something, that had turned him toward Davan at the word *fourth,*
and his pause was the pause of a man who had not expected another voice
to be ready.

Davan's skin at the throat was green-gold.

Not pulsing. Sustained. The color it had gone to in Edvar's back room
over the face with the lamp burning down by half. He had not felt it
arrive. It had arrived while Aldric spoke, at the count of the three,
as if his body had been running the count with Aldric and had reached
the fourth at the same beat.

He did not cover it.

"Edvar has one too," he heard himself say.

It came out flat. It came out plain. He had been circling it all
morning under his ribs, and the circling had come around.

Aldric's expression did not change very much. It adjusted slowly, the
way a man's expression adjusted when the new information was the
information he had half-expected without letting himself know he was
expecting it.

"A face."

"A face."

"The same kind."

"The same kind."

Aldric breathed out, once, very carefully.

"Show me what yours showed," he said to Davan, and then with a small
correcting turn of the head to Edvar. "If you have a way. I know the
faces do not travel easily."

"Mine is in the trunk," Edvar said. "The trunk is in the cart. I
brought the trunk."

---

They did not open the trunk at the outcrop.

Vane came in from her boulder and said, quietly, without heat, "We are
on a road. It is midday. People use this road."

Aldric nodded. Edvar nodded. Maren nodded. The trunk would be opened
at camp. The business of the road came back. The horses were watered.
The cart moved off. Aldric mounted the bay for the first time all
morning — not as a claim on the head of the line, but because Edvar
had said, over the cart wheel, that the afternoon was long, and
Aldric's knees were older than his pride. Aldric had laughed once, a
thin surprised laugh, and had mounted.

They rode and walked east into the afternoon.

---

The trunk was opened at camp.

Camp was a cart-stand, a place used by carters through the highlands
for centuries, set into the lee of a stand of ash. The ash trees were
bare. Their branches were north-bent, the way the hawthorns at the
fold had been. The country had a way of telling you which way the wind
lived.

Vane walked the perimeter. Twice. Wider each time. She came in from
the second circuit and did not say anything, and her not saying
anything meant the same thing as the morning: no one on the back, long
sightline, clear.

Edvar brought the trunk down from the cart bed with Davan's help.
Aldric held a lamp. Maren sat cross-legged on a blanket against the
cart wheel, her coat over her knees, her hand inside her coat where
the disc had gone back at noon and had stayed.

Edvar turned the key on the cord inside his shirt.

The face came out of the oiled canvas the way it had come out in the
back room — flat, dark, light going in and not coming out. Aldric made
a small sound, the sound a man made when his breath caught on a word
and let it go without the word coming.

The green-gold Davan had carried through the afternoon had faded into
low blue by the time they reached camp, the way a color faded when the
body had held a thing too long. Now, with the face on the canvas in
the lamp-light, it came back. Throat. Collarbones. Wrists.

Edvar set the face on the flat of the blanket and looked at Aldric.

"You want to," Edvar said.

"No," Aldric said. "I want Davan to. This is his."

Davan knelt and put his fingers on the surface. The surface lit, cold
and pale and precise, spreading from his fingertips, and the image
assembled itself in the small circle of lamplight and cold pre-crash
glow — the blue-green world, the three near shapes with their masses,
and the fourth.

Smaller. Further. Distant.

The distance of the fourth was the thing his body had gone to both
times he had seen it — the far-away quality of a point that was almost
a shape, something barely resolved, whole at the limit of what the
face could render.

Aldric looked at it for a long time.

"Mine showed four," Aldric said at last. "And a burning point. And the
world."

"Yes."

"The near three — these three."

"These three."

"And the fourth." He did not finish the sentence. He looked up at
Davan. "Yours shows it —"

"— smaller. Further. Distant." Davan said the words as he had said
them to Edvar. "That is what I saw."

Aldric nodded.

He did not say what his face had shown him of the fourth. Davan did
not ask. The asking was not for tonight. The asking would come —
tomorrow, the day after, at some hour the group could do the asking —
but tonight the two accounts were on the blanket together and the
accounts were enough. Two faces. Two renderings. The fourth shape was
present in both, and the how of it could keep until there was light to
see it by.

What landed in him, instead, was the other thing.

---

"Brennan's letter," Aldric said. "There was a phrase. *For unchanged
hands.*"

Maren did not move. She was listening.

"The surveyor," Aldric said. "Reth. Who walked a formation in the
eastern highlands forty years ago — a metallic formation, four hundred
meters across, no grain or seam, manufactured. With a recess at its
southeastern end. A recess shaped like a coin. With the inner orbit
symbol inside it."

He had delivered that sentence in the cooperage. Davan had heard it
then. It did different work now. On a road east, under a lamp, over
two faces laid on a blanket, the sentence became what it had not been
in the basement: *architecture.*

"And Brennan's survey," Aldric said, "of the ground immediately east
of the Temple of Confluence. There is a quality to the bedrock there
that he — " Aldric reached for a scholar's word and visibly chose
another one. "That he has been afraid of. For years. Metallic
signatures at depth where metallic signatures should not be. Not the
formation Reth walked. A smaller — a scatter. Under the temple. Under
the lands adjacent. As though something —"

He stopped. He looked at Davan. He had heard himself approaching a
sentence a cartographer had been unable to finish for twenty years,
and the hesitation — Davan read it — was not theological. Courtesy,
rather.

Davan finished it quietly.

"As though something came apart in the coming down," he said. "And a
piece of it went where it went. And another piece of it — pieces of it
— went elsewhere."

"Yes."

"The temple is built on a piece of it."

"Above a piece of it. But yes."

The silence after that was the silence at the cooperage table after
the disc pulsed, and also not. At the cooperage the silence had been
six faces catching one new thing. Here there were four. At the edge of
the lamp-circle, where the ring of light failed and the ash-bark
began, something moved in the dark — Vane, circling — and the silence
extended to the dark and the dark extended to the silence and the
country underneath it all carried its carried thing in its cold slow
register and said nothing because it had not been asked a question.

Davan's skin at the collarbones stayed green-gold. He did not try to
make it do anything else. The country carried what it carried, and his
body carried what his body carried, and for a long moment the two of
them were simply the same body.

---

Edvar shut the trunk eventually. Slowly, one knee at a time, the old
negotiation. The face had been out for perhaps twenty minutes. Enough.
The pale glow bled from the canvas seam for a while after, which was
why Edvar wrapped the canvas before the wood — the way a man wrapped
a candle when he carried it through a draft.

Aldric sat back on his heels. Then he stood and pinched something at
his coat that was not a lamp wick and let the fingers fall. He caught
the fingers at the fall, this time. He set them flat against his thigh.
His face did the small work of a man being honest with his hand about
what it had been about to do.

Maren did not comment.

Vane came into the light and sat at the edge of the blanket. She did
not look at the trunk. She looked at her hands inside her cuffs.

"You'll want watches," she said. "I'll take first. Aldric second.
Davan third."

"The dead hour," Aldric said.

"I'll take the dead hour. I am awake in the dead hour."

Nobody argued with her. Maren looked at Davan. Davan looked back. The
look was not an exchange. It was an acknowledgment. There was no
sentence any of them had yet that would do the day's work in summary,
and they did not reach for the sentence. For now the watch was set,
and the face was in the trunk, and the trunk was in the cart, and the
cart was in the lee of the ash.

---

He did not sleep for an hour.

He lay on his back with his coat over him and the hawthorn piece from
the night before in his hand, and he took the knife out of his belt
and carved.

He did not try to see what he was carving. He let his hands work. He
had been doing this on the road north; he had been doing it in the
docks at lunch; he had been doing it in Edvar's front room in
quarter-hours between conversations. His hands would find the shape
they found, and then he would look down.

Tonight the shape the hands found was a piece of ground.

Not a shape he had carved before. Not the symbol — the inner orbit
symbol had come out of his hands so many times in the last year he
could have carved it in the dark and had. This was a different thing.
A low slow rise. A shoulder of earth. The top of a thing you would not
know was a thing unless you had walked on it. A hill that the woodgrain
said was hollow under it — he had gone against the grain with his
knife-cut at the top, so the fibre ran straight down inside the shape,
and the straight-down of the grain made the top of the rise look like
it had something beneath it that was not the rise.

He looked at it in his palm.

He had carved a buried thing.

He had carved it without deciding to, and he had carved it with the
note under the country working in his wrists all day. The piece of
hawthorn was not the hill east of New Plymouth — the hill was five
days and he was on day two — but the shape of what his body had been
reading along the road was in his hand now, small, four-fingers long,
the wood a pale hawthorn-yellow in the lamp-edge light.

He set it on the blanket beside him.

The green-gold at his collarbones dimmed, at last, to the low steady
blue of a body at rest. Across the camp Vane was upright at first
watch, her shape against the ash. Maren was already asleep, one hand
inside her coat. Edvar had his mouth open in the way of men his age
asleep and did not care who saw. Aldric was in his blanket with his
eyes closed and was not yet asleep, his hand palm-down on his thigh,
the way he had laid it after the disc on the stone.

Davan closed his hand around the carved piece. The hawthorn was cold
on one side where the air had reached it and warm on the other side
where his palm had been. He kept his palm against the cold side until
the cold side was not cold any more, and he held it there past the
warming — past the point where holding did anything — because the
country under the camp was cold in a way that a palm could not warm,
and the hawthorn in his hand was a small thing, and the smallness of
it was what he had to give.

He did not finish the thought. He held the hawthorn piece against his
ribs under the coat, and the wood went on taking the warmth out of him
the way old stone took it out of a hand at a gate, slowly and
politely, and after a while his hand was on the carved thing and the
carved thing was against his heart and the country was under all of
it and did not ask anything of him at all.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Five — *Aldric*
### The Shape of Old Things

---

The second watch was not the cruelty people said it was.

At the Confluence Aldric had stood the watch before Lauds for thirty
years — the hour when the chapel was coldest and the sanctuary lamp
needed the longest sight to see to — and he had come to a private
understanding with the small hours that they were simply awake. Other
hours were fuller. Other hours were more pleasant. But the watch did
not pretend to be anything, and a priest could not, after a certain
point, tell the difference between the pretence of other hours and
their substance, and so the watch had become, in its way, honest.

He sat against the north-bent ash with Edvar's lamp hooded at his
feet and watched the open side of the cart-stand through the dark.
Vane had taken the first watch and gone to her blanket without
speaking; her breathing, after half an hour, had quieted into
something that might have been sleep and might only have been the
body finishing a day. Maren slept the watchful sleep of a person
who had done it in barns. Davan, a man whose body had worked all day
at carrying what his wrists had carried, slept without motion. Edvar
slept loudly and without apology.

Aldric's hand had reached once, in the first quarter hour, for a
wick that was not there. The ember at the lamp had wanted tending in
the old way — pinched between thumb and forefinger, the small
pastoral trim — and his fingers had come up off his knee and had
gone the short distance and had closed on nothing.

He had not caught himself.

His fingers had opened before they closed. Not because he had
decided. Because there had been nothing there to close on, and the
hand, finding nothing, had come back to his knee without being told.

He had not marked the motion. He had looked at his hand after, on
his knee, and the hand had done a thing without his supervision and
the thing had been a thing not done.

---

The eastern sky began to think about its morning a long time before
it committed to it. Grey at the lip first, then a paler grey, then
the thinnest suggestion of colour that was not yet colour. A rook
called from some field a mile off and another answered.

The thing the hand had done, which was the not-doing, was a small
thing. He noted it.

---

They broke camp in the pale.

Edvar rose stiffly and poked at the coals and found nothing but ash
under the stick. "Clean," he said, and the word meant the camp would
leave no trace. Vane was out on her perimeter circuit before Aldric
had found his second boot. On her return she gave Maren half a shake
of the head, no sentence under it — nothing on the back — and went
on with her pack.

Aldric put a hand against the bay's warm neck as he cinched. She
leaned her head into his shoulder briefly and he let her. He
tightened the girth a hole. The hole was new. Four days on the road,
three camps, and the weight that had always gone first on an old
horse in autumn was going first on her now.

Davan came past him with the water-skins. The young man's skin at
the open collar was pale, the ghost of last night's green-gold still
in it the way a stain stayed in a cloth after washing. He nodded to
Aldric. Aldric nodded back. Neither of them had spoken since the
lamp had gone down over the blanket.

The thing on the blanket was in the trunk in the cart. The thing in
Aldric's chest was not yet in any container he had words for, and
the morning was not going to put it in one. The cart was harnessed.
The fold was clean. He mounted the bay.

---

The road took them out of the ash-lee in a long climb.

They were at the foot of Irongate country. The name had always
sounded larger than the place. *Irongate* suggested an outpost, a
wall, a border-fort holding a line. The reality, as Brennan had told
him on the ride east to Greenford, was that Irongate referred not to
a construction but to a shape of country — a long narrow gap between
two shoulders of high bedrock, running east-northeast, a gate carved
by glaciers before there were men to call any of it by any name. The
iron in the name was the iron in the rock, a rust-red seam along the
north shoulder that bled down into the scree. No fort. No wall. Only
the country, narrowed by its own geology, insisting on a line of
passage.

They climbed into it through the morning.

The ground changed as they climbed. The coarse tussock of yesterday's
upland gave way to shorter grass with more stone in it — heather-ends
brown as the year turned, bedrock breaking the turf in wide bare
patches, the horizon coming closer on either side and further away
in front. Sheep thinned. A single hawk rode the updraft at the edge
of the north shoulder and did not cry.

He had been trained to note the change in country the way a geographer
noted it, because a geographer was one of the things he had been
trained to be among the others, and he found, after a while, that he
was also noting it in another register — the way stone *carried,*
the way Davan had named it last night at the outcrop without naming
it. He did not have Davan's instrument. His instrument was older and
clumsier and had been trained on a different sort of ground. But it
was not, he thought, a different question.

---

Midmorning they stopped at a bend where a small spring came up
through mossed stone and ran across the verge. Vane drank first,
cupped-hand, back to the road. The bay drank like a horse that had
been thinking about it for an hour. Edvar loosed the cart-harness
and rolled his shoulder.

Aldric dismounted. His knees were stiff. He had spent too many
hours at a lectern and not enough on a horse, and a man found out
what his body had been doing for thirty years when he asked it to
do something else for four days running.

Maren came up to the spring with her water-skin. She filled it
carefully, hand inside the mouth to keep the canvas from taking in
moss. When she stood, the disc under her coat pulsed — he could not
see the pulse, of course, but he saw its consequence: her free hand
went briefly inside the coat at the inner pocket, and her breath went
out in the short practical breath of a person registering a weather
and moving past it. She saw him watching. She did not pretend she
had not.

"Three this morning," she said. Quietly. Not to him, exactly —
pitched at the spring, for anyone at the spring who needed it. "Day
started at one. That was the third."

"More than yesterday."

"More than yesterday."

She capped the skin.

"The warmth?" he said.

"The warmth keeps up."

He nodded. He had no answer to give her — he did not yet know the
question well enough to answer it — but the exchange was its own kind
of work, and he did not want her to think her report had reached a
room without a receiver. He was the receiver the institution had
trained up. He would receive.

Vane went further up the road and stood at a knuckle of rock thirty
paces on and looked east under her hand. Her back was to them. Her
shoulders were set low, the way shoulders set when a body was
carrying a thing and deciding to carry it quietly. He did not look
at her longer than a stranger at a spring would.

Davan, at the cart-step, offered a piece of dried apple. Aldric
took it.

"Thank you."

"The bay work out the stiffness?"

"She does. I don't, at quite the same rate."

Davan's response was a small motion of the head that did not commit
to being anything. The apple was sharp and thin and very good.

The disc, under Maren's coat across the cart, did whatever it did.
Aldric was not close enough for anything to reach him. Davan, beside
him, was close enough; and Davan's skin at the wrist took on a shade
of the green and let it go again, like a word said once under the
breath and not repeated. Aldric did not ask. He had learned to
accept the half-answers of a man working a long question. He gave
the apple-stem to the bay, who took it with the dignity of an old
horse accepting a sweet.

They moved on.

---

By early afternoon the shoulders of rock had closed in.

The road went along the north side of the gap, along a ledge cut by
carters over generations and deepened by rain until it was less a
road than a long trough of stone. The south shoulder stood across
