the gap at half a mile's distance, and the distance did not vary
for an hour — the country had found its shape and committed to it.
A stream ran at the bottom of the gap, small and loud, and the
sound kept up under the cart-wheels the way a low note kept up
under a hymn.

Aldric rode at Edvar's off-wheel. He had spoken perhaps five
sentences since the spring. He had been thinking instead — the way
he had been trained to think at a lectern, long architected clauses,
the scholarly way — and he had been thinking it on top of another
thing the scholarly way did not quite have a handle on.

The second thing was this:

The temple at the Confluence had been built over a piece of the
lander.

He had been carrying this since the lamp-circle last night, since
Davan had finished his sentence for him with the proper courtesy of
a man who had been finishing his own unfinished sentences for a long
time. *The temple is built on a piece of it.* *Above a piece of it.
But yes.* He had said *but yes* because the preposition mattered.
The temple was not *on* it the way a building was *on* a foundation.
The temple was *above* it. Placed to be over.

The distinction had been scholarly when he made it.

Riding east in the iron gap, under a sky that had got larger, it was
not scholarly any more.

He had spent thirty years of careful honesty inside an institution
that had been built, stone on stone, above a fragment of the thing
he was now riding east to find. Every morning of those thirty years
he had gone down to the chapel at Lauds and walked an aisle of
floor-stones laid above undercroft-stones laid above whatever else
lay below the undercroft — and the undercroft, he now knew, was not
the bottom. The chamber he had opened two months ago was not the
*foundation* of the temple. It was a place cut into the side of a
buried thing, and the temple had been put over it. His predecessors,
in some century he had not located yet, had understood well enough
what they were doing to do it deliberately.

They had not hidden the face in the temple.

They had built the temple to hide the face.

He let the sentence arrive. It arrived slowly, with a settling, the
way a vault-stone settled into a vault. He held the reins and let
the bay take her pace and let the sentence stand.

His hands on the reins did not fold.

---

They stopped where the north shoulder came down in a tumble and gave
the road a kind of shelter. A dry-stone fold older than yesterday's,
three walls and most of a fourth. Vane had walked the shelter before
they committed to it. She came back with a stone in her hand and
turned it over once before setting it down on another stone, and the
turning was all the approval anyone was going to get.

Edvar unhitched. The horses cropped the short turf. Maren laid a
fire small enough to hide in the lee of the wall. Davan filled the
kettle. Aldric unsaddled the bay and tethered her at the leeward
wall and rubbed her down with a folded sack.

They were an hour past the eastern edge of Irongate country now,
camped on heath that ran russet and wide around them, broken bones
of other folds scattered at intervals across it. The road behind
climbed back toward the gap they had come through. Ahead it went
on east toward a darker line in the far distance — a further ridge,
or the beginning of the coast weather a day ahead.

Vane stood at the mouth of the fold for a long time looking back.
Not forward. Back. West, the way they had come. He knew what she
was looking at. He did not ask.

---

Davan opened the trunk at dusk.

Edvar produced the key from the cord and passed it to him, and
Davan unlatched the trunk and lifted out the oiled canvas and laid
it on the blanket in the lee of the cart-wheel. Aldric had set a
lamp on the stone of the fold wall at the height that would let the
light come straight down across the canvas without glare.

The face came out of its wrap.

The undercroft had been the first seeing — his own chamber, his own
hand, alone, the candle going down. Last night at the ash-lee had
been the second, Davan's hand, Davan's light. This was the third:
a fold in Irongate country, a lamp in a wall-niche, four people
around a blanket and no stranger at the gate.

Maren sat cross-legged at the head of the blanket. Edvar took the
cart-step. Vane was at the mouth of the fold with her back to them
and her eyes east. Davan knelt at the blanket's edge and did not
put his hand on the face yet.

He looked at Aldric.

"You," Davan said.

Aldric did not answer. He looked at his hand on his knee. The hand
had not folded at the waist when he sat down; he had caught the
training in time; he had put his hands flat on his thighs as he had
learned to put them flat. The hand that Davan was offering the first
touch to — his right, the one that had reached a thousand times for
a wick that was not there — was not trembling. It did not reach. It
sat where he had put it.

"Together," Aldric said, after a moment. "If you will."

Davan nodded.

They laid their palms against the surface at the same time.

The face lit.

Pale. Cold. Familiar to Aldric in a way he had been trying not to
feel, because the familiarity was not nostalgia — it was *return,*
and he had not wanted to begin this work from a register that could
be made sentimental. He breathed through the familiarity. The image
assembled.

The blue-green world, first. Then the burning point at centre — not
the sun, he corrected himself even as the old word arrived, *the
star of this system,* because the old word meant a different set of
things than the accurate word and he was past a point in his life
where the old set could be allowed to do the work of the new — the
star. Then the arrangement.

He counted.

One. Two. Three.

Four.

The same count. The same arrangement in the near three. The same
drift in the outer one. It had been the inner three of his undercroft
count and it was the inner three of this face's count, and the
precision of the matching was one of the small cold mercies Aldric
had been granted in the past two months — that he was not mistaken,
that the data were the data, that somewhere above or between them
there was a system that had been keeping a faithful record and was,
through these small dark surfaces, still keeping it.

The fourth shape rendered in the lamp-light.

It was smaller than his had been.

Not by much. A hand-breadth's worth of smaller, if one were looking
at the two faces side by side. The shape was roughly the same — a
rounded irregular body, not circular, with an extension on one limb
that resolved when Aldric leaned closer as a — a *jag*; an angle of
broken profile; the asymmetry of a thing whose outline was not the
outline a maker would have chosen for it.

"Smaller," Davan said, quietly, his voice very level. "Further.
Distant. As I saw it last night."

"Yes."

Aldric did not lift his hand from the surface. He looked at Davan.
Davan was looking at the display.

"Mine," Aldric said, "showed it larger. Closer. Or — " he stopped,
because the word *closer* had arrived before the accurate word, and
he wanted the accurate word. "Not closer. The same. As if from a
nearer position. The same object. Nearer. And — " the liturgical
word was trying to come up to meet the scientific one, and he let
them come together this time; he was tired of keeping them in
different rooms " — and broken. Damaged. The jag, larger. The
asymmetry legible. From my face it looked as if something had
happened to it recently. From yours it looks like a point at the
limit of what the instrument can still see."

Davan looked up.

"Same object," Davan said.

"Same object."

"Different where the face is."

"Yes."

---

They did not speak for a long moment.

The lamp did its small work in the wall-niche. The kettle, at the
other fire in the lee of the wall, had gone off the boil and was
ticking down. Edvar had not moved on the cart-step. Maren had not
moved on the blanket. Vane, at the fold's mouth, had turned her head
a fraction — not to look but to hear better — and had turned it
back.

Aldric understood the shape of the silence.

It was, he thought, the silence of two scholars who had found the
same volume in two different libraries and had laid the copies side
by side for the first time and had discovered that the annotations
differed in the way annotations differed when the marginal hand had
been writing from different rooms at different times. Not a
contradiction. Not an error. A *position.* The two faces had been in
two positions when they had recorded, and the positions had produced
two accounts, and the two accounts together — this was the piece
that was landing in him with the slow settling of the vault-stone
again — the two accounts together were more information than either
alone, because the difference between them was itself data.

The faces had not been the same face.

They had been two instruments recording the same event from two
vantages. The vantages had been different because the *instruments*
had been in different places when they recorded, and the instruments
had been in different places because the debris field was wider than
either face could, on its own, see.

"The debris field," he said, aloud, because the word had to come out
for the sentence to become the sentence. "The debris field is not
the thing we've been looking at. The debris field is wider than
either of these surfaces alone could tell us."

Davan said: "Yes."

"A face at the Confluence. A face in a collapsed building in your
mapmaker's part of the city. Corvel's container in the marshland
south of the ridge. Reth's formation in the highlands ahead of us.
Brennan's metallic signatures under the Confluence itself. None of
these is the same piece. They are all — "

"Pieces," Davan said.

"Pieces."

Aldric lifted his hand from the face. The image held a beat after his
hand lifted — these surfaces did that, he had learned, they held an
activation the way an afterimage held in an eye — and then it began
to fade, slowly, the blue-green world going first, the star going
last, the four shapes all dimming together. Davan did not lift his
hand. He watched the fade. When the face had gone dark he lifted his
hand too and set it, palm down, on his thigh.

Maren, at the head of the blanket, spoke for the first time since
they had laid the face down.

"How wide," she said.

Aldric looked at her. She was not asking him specifically. She was
asking the air above the blanket, and the air was, at this moment, a
room the four of them were in together. But the question was his to
answer more than the others', and he answered it.

"I don't know."

"Guess."

He considered. He considered Brennan's scatter under the temple —
small signatures, a scatter, the sort of thing that resolved under a
surveyor's instruments as *debris*, not as *formation.* He considered
Reth's formation in the highlands — four hundred meters, a continuous
manufactured surface. He considered the marsh. He considered Edvar's
face in a collapsed building that had once been a house in the old
quarter of a port city that was, on the oldest maps, not there at all.

"At least four separate sites. That is not a guess. That is four
confirmed. The field is the distance between the furthest two sites
we know about. Confluence to the highlands. That is — sixty miles.
A little more."

"Sixty miles," Maren said.

"The places I can confirm. There will be others."

"How many others."

"I don't know."

She nodded, once, and did not push. She was a person, he had come
to recognize, who understood when a question had reached the edge of
its answer and did not pretend by asking again that the edge could
be moved.

The lamp did its work. The canvas, dark, lay on the blanket. The
face, under the canvas, was dark under it, the way a hearth-stone
was dark under a wrapped cloth.

Edvar shifted on the cart-step. Vane, at the mouth of the fold, took
a breath in and let it out and did not turn.

Davan wrapped the face in the canvas, slowly, with the care Aldric
had seen Edvar use last night with a candle in a draught. Edvar
lowered it into the trunk. The trunk closed. The key went back on
the cord into Edvar's shirt.

---

Aldric sat a long time at the fire after the trunk was in the cart.

Maren had gone to the mouth of the fold to speak quietly with Vane,
the wind and the stream for privacy. Edvar was asleep under the
cart-step. Davan was at his blanket with a piece of wood and a knife
and had not begun to carve yet. The lamp in the wall-niche was out.
A smaller lamp sat on a stone at the fire's edge, the size of a
man's fist.

Aldric looked at it.

He had tended lamps for thirty years. Chapel lamps, sanctuary lamps,
tallow dips at wayside-chapels on visitation years. His thumb and
forefinger carried a dozen small scars where a wick had done what
wicks sometimes did. He looked at his hand. It was on his knee. It
was not, at this moment, anywhere near the lamp.

He did not reach.

He sat with the not-reaching. The hand did not do its thing. The
lamp did its small yellow work and burned its small length of wick.

He had spent thirty years as a man who managed other people's fires.
The instinct that had kept his hand at the wick of every lamp in
every chapel of a minor provincial order was the instinct of a man
who had loved small tended things and had thought that the tending
of small things was what his hand had been given him for.

He had been wrong about what his hand had been given him for.

His hand had been given him for the work that was in front of it
now, which was not the tending of small lamps. It was the *opening*
of what the institution he had served for thirty years had been
built expressly to keep closed. The temple had been put over the
face to keep the face under a floor. The next Keeper, if he was
going to be honest, was going to be the one who took the floor up.

He did not yet know how a man took up a floor he had sworn to walk
on. The *how* was tomorrow's, or the week's, or the year's. The
*that* was this hour's, and this hour had given it to him, and he
accepted it.

He looked at the small lamp a last time. The wick did not need
trimming. The wick was doing its work well enough. The wick was
not his wick.

He leaned down and blew.

The flame went. The cold came in at the space where the flame had
been. The hand he had not used to put it out stayed on his knee,
open, palm up, the pads of the thumb and forefinger resting on air.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Six — *Vane*
### Withdrawal

---

The dead hour did not begin at an hour. It began at a change in
the cold.

She had come onto second watch at the ash-lee with her hands
already numb past useful, and she had sat against the north-bent
ash and taken her post because taking her post was what she had
said she would do. Aldric had gone to his blanket without
pressing. She appreciated the not-pressing. She did not want the
Keeper's face looking at her face at the moment her face was
doing what her face had started to do in the evenings.

*Tremor both hands. Gut off. Hearing misallocated — low
frequencies lifted, high ones thin. Temperature off-register,
the kind of wrong-warmth that fools you into taking the coat
off. Do not take the coat off.*

She kept the coat on.

The cold at the ash-lee was the cold of an upland camp at the
third hour — dry cold, a cold whose signature she knew. She had
sat out a hundred of these. Her body knew how to read what was
the weather and what was the body.

Tonight the ratio was not clean.

---

At some point in the dark she checked the arm.

Left hand under right sleeve, thumb to the back of the right
wrist where the patch had been flaking since the night before
they left the city. The grain was drier than it had been at
dusk. She ran her thumb along it once, slowly. The flake lifted
at the edge. It did not come off under her thumb. She took her
thumb off.

*Not looking at it in lamplight. Not tonight.*

She put the sleeve back.

A rook moved in some field a mile off. Nothing nearer. Her nose
told her nothing nearer, which was the information.

Her nose. Still hers.

She let the small fact sit. She did not decorate it.

---

First light came without her deciding to let it.

Aldric rose. She registered him by the long motion of a man his
age sitting up, two pauses in it. He took the watch without
asking and she yielded it without speaking, and she went to her
blanket with her coat still on, and her hands inside the cuffs
kept up their tremor. She had stopped rating it by severity.
Rating by severity required a baseline and the baseline was now
yesterday.

She closed her eyes for the length of two breaths. The body took
what it could and gave the rest back.

---

Maren was at the cart when Vane stood up.

She was repacking the water-skins. She looked up once, briefly,
and her face did a small thing that registered and did not
pretend. The face said: *I have already decided not to ask.*

Vane crossed to her pack and drew out the chapped leather cuffs
she used in cold weather and pulled them over the backs of her
hands. They took the tremor out of the cuff-motion. They did not
take the tremor out of anything else. They were cover.

Maren watched her do this without looking at her.

"There's tea," Maren said. "Short kettle. Davan set it."

"All right."

The cup clinked once against her teeth. She set it down before
it clinked a second time.

Maren's free hand went inside her coat as she reached for the
kettle-handle. Came out. Went back to the kettle. She did not
comment and neither did Vane. Vane catalogued it. *Second of the
day. First was before the bell.*

---

They broke camp in the pale.

She walked her circuit of the cart-stand. She had walked a
hundred of these. The circuit was thirty-four paces and had five
breaks in the ground to be registered and two stumps that
resolved on the return pass differently than on the out pass,
because early light and late light were different sentences.

On the fifth pace she saw it second.

A dew-track across a shelf of stone. Four hours old, probably. A
small animal. Not a person. She should have seen it on the out
pass. The missing was the information.

*Re-reading the same stretch. Day three symptom per Cade's old
chart. Attention fragments. Compensate: extend the circuit, slow
the pace.*

She extended the circuit. She slowed the pace.

She came back in with nothing new to report except the wider
circuit, and she set that against the report she had not made
and went to the cart.

---

The road out of the ash-lee climbed.

She was ahead by ten paces — the position she had been taking
since they left the city. It let her set the pace and put her
face into the wind, which this morning was cold out of the
north-northeast and kept the wrong-sweat from turning on her
under the coat.

Aldric rode behind her with the bay. Then the cart, Edvar on the
bench, Maren at the off-wheel, Davan at the near. She knew their
positions without turning her head.

She would have known their positions yesterday by smell — bay
sweat, Edvar's old oil, Aldric's travel coat, Maren's coat-collar
with the rooming-house soap, Davan's warmth. This morning she
knew them by pace and by the direction of the wind. Her nose gave
her the bay and gave her Davan at distance and the cart-wood
faintly and did not give her Maren and did not give her Aldric.

The baseline had contracted.

*Three feet. Two feet on a wind. Down by an order from what it
used to be. Not gone. Contracted.*

She walked.

---

They were into the beginning of Irongate country by mid-morning.

The shoulders of rock began closing in. In the last country they
had crossed, a follower at the edge of a sightline could be lost
in the weave of heath and rock without having to be skilled. In
this country, with the sightlines lengthened by the gap, he had
to be actively managing distance.

She knew the tactics. She had used them for ten years.

She was working the problem backward before she knew she was
working anything.

*If I were following four people and a cart through this country,
I would drop behind by three quarters of a mile at the last rise,
wait thirty minutes, then close to half a mile and hold. The gap
shape forces me closer than I'd prefer. The rust scree on the
north shoulder gives me cover at eye level. I'd take the lower
line at the stream if I had to.*

She walked another twelve paces before she registered that she
had been, for the length of the thought, inside a man's head
that was not hers, at the distance that thought described.

Information.

She did not act on it. One inferential step was not confirmation.
She needed the wind.

---

The wind shifted at the mid-morning bend.

She had been waiting for it. The gap turned a quarter-compass
off north, and the wind that had been in her face came across at
her right shoulder, and the wind that had been going nowhere
instructive began to take the back of the line and carry it
forward to her.

She turned so her left nostril had the wind's work. The bark
grain at the right shoulder carried scent poorly — the wrong
instrument.

She breathed in and did the old reading.

Dry grass. Sheep-country mud days old. The rust in the north
scree. The horses. Edvar. Maren at the off-wheel, Aldric further
back, Davan at the near. Nothing new in the party.

Back of the party — the long back, the ground they had crossed
this morning.

Nothing.

She waited.

The wind did not hand you a signature on the first breath. It
handed you what was there densest. What was thin and professional
and managed arrived, if it arrived, on the second or third.

Second breath.

Cold-metal.

Not a flood of it. A thread. The thin carried note of a person
who was not panicking — a person holding himself at the flat
register of a body doing its work. Cold-metal without the sour
edge of fear. The cleaner cold. The cold of a contract man
reading his mark.

She had smelled it before, in her own mouth, when she had been
the one doing the reading.

*Professional. Male. Singular. Behind us by a distance the wind
is carrying him at this shift.*

She did not turn her head. She did not change pace.

*How long.*

She ran the wind backward in her memory. The chart she had been
keeping had holes in it now where the entries for the previous
nights should have been. But the ford. At the ford the wind had
been up from the harbor mouth across her right shoulder the way
it was now, and she had stopped on the plank walkway and put her
hand in her coat pocket and taken her hand out with nothing in
it, and she had registered at the time that she had been about
to smell something and had not.

Now the smell was back. The wind had handed her what it had
tried to hand her at the ford, when she had been less broken for
the handing.

He had been with them since the ford.

She knew this with the precision you knew a thing when the wind
delivered it the second time.

---

She did not tell them at the bend.

She walked to the next reasonable stop — a spring coming up
through moss where the road took a half-circle around a knuckle
of rock. When Maren came to the spring with her skin, Vane stood
at her shoulder and took a cup-full and drank first, back to the
road, and said at a pitch meant for the spring and no further:

"One behind us."

Maren's skin-filling hand did not pause.

"How far," Maren said.

"Three-quarter mile. Professional. Singular."

"How long."

"Since the ford."

Maren nodded once.

"Aldric," Maren said, not looking up. "At the cart."

Aldric had come down from the bay. He did not come to the
spring; he understood by the angle of Maren's head that the
conference was not for him to step into. He went to the cart
instead and stood at the off-wheel and said something to Edvar,
and Edvar went quiet.

Davan, on the near wheel, stopped walking entirely. His eyes
came up to Vane's at the spring. His skin at the open collar
took on a thin cool amber for the length of a breath and dropped.
He had read the spring from ten paces by reading her face.

Her face had done a thing without her. She was not entirely in
the driver's seat of her own face today.

---

They regrouped at the cart a minute later.

Vane pitched her voice low. Not private; just short of carrying.

"A man behind us. Professional. One. Cold. Not in a hurry. The
wind just gave him to me. He's been with us since the ford."

Aldric absorbed this without his face doing anything. His hand
at his coat did not reach for any wick or any fold.

"Not Horst," Aldric said. Not a question.

"Not Horst. Horst is in New Plymouth. This is one of his. Horst
stays with a city contract."

"How certain."

"I trained with him three seasons. He works a city."

"Tactics," Maren said.

Vane took a breath. It caught. She let it catch. She took
another.

"Three moves. One: we slow. Quarter-pace. A man dragging a tail
wants to find out whether we've noticed him, and a quarter-pace
is inside the normal error a cart makes over a day. If he holds
his distance at quarter-pace, he's following a contract. If he
closes, he's looking."

"Two," Davan said.

"We change camp. We were going to camp at the east edge of
Irongate. Edvar had a fold picked. We don't camp there. Edvar?"

"There's an older one on the eastern heath. Used to be a
shepherd's. Three walls. I've camped it twice."

"We camp there. If he's working from a route, the empty fold
costs him an hour. If he's working from sightline, he's on us
already and the extra mile is our profit, not his."

"Three," Maren said.

The tremor in Vane's left hand did its small visible work at the
fingertip. She did not hide it.

"I do not walk point today. Davan walks point — not to track;
just to hold the pace. I walk back of the cart. My nose is worse
than yesterday and worse than it will be in a week, but it's
better than any of yours by a margin still worth something. The
back of the cart is where the wind brings what the wind brings."

"Can you walk back of the cart," Maren said. Flat. Not the
softened voice.

"Yes."

"If you can't."

"You'll see it. You'll put me in the cart."

Maren nodded once.

"All right," she said.

---

They moved on.

Vane took her new position at the back of the cart. She walked
with one hand on the tailboard and her weight rolling foot to
foot the way she had rolled it on night watches where the body's
protest had to be managed without reaching the face. The wind
kept up across her right shoulder. The cold-metal thread came and
went at the wind's intervals, not at rhythm but at wind. Once
every fifteen minutes was enough.

He was closing by a few paces an hour. He was doing the sum she
had done an hour ago from inside his head, and he was doing it
correctly, and the fact of his doing it correctly was what she
had needed to know about him.

*Not green. Contract man. Horst's. Not the raw lads. Works
highland routes. Reads rock cover. Knows how carters break the
day.*

The analysis began to stretch and snag. *Male. Right-handed,
probably. Not young.* The next sentence was going to be a
category that did not fit the data. She did not reach for it.

He was a man behind her at three-quarters of a mile. She could
smell him. That was enough.

Twice in the hour after, at the off-wheel side, Maren's hand
went inside her coat and came out again without comment. Vane
did not ask. Davan on the bench had stopped speaking to Edvar at
the second of them, and his collar had stayed the low held green
Vane had seen yesterday at the outcrop. The two of them had
their instrument for this and Vane was not it. That was also
enough.

---

Around the middle of the afternoon Davan dropped back from point.

He did not come all the way to her. He came to the near wheel
for a few minutes, and then drifted toward her without angling
his body — a motion she recognized as one of her own, the kind
of approach that did not ask a room for permission.

He stopped at the tailboard two paces from her hand.

His skin at the wrist had a pale amber wash to it that was not
fear. It was the pale amber of a body carrying a weight whose
name it had not decided on.

"Water?" he said.

"No."

"Shoulder?"

"No. The shoulder's all right."

He let this pass. He was not going to ask the question he had
come to ask. He was going to stand at the tailboard for the time
it took him to stand at the tailboard.

He was reading her.

Not the way Maren was reading her — Maren watched the surface
shapes. Davan was reading the thing under the chart. The register
he worked in was her body doing something without her permission,
and his body took the thing on the far end of the register and
had to manage what it did on the way in.

"Don't," she said. Quietly. Not a command.

"Don't what."

"Don't keep your skin where other people can see what it's doing
on my account. Turn your back to the wind. Turn your wrist in."

A beat. His collar-color dropped a shade. The amber at the wrist
stayed.

"I don't know how to turn it in, exactly," he said. "Yet."

"Then stop standing here."

"All right."

He waited two breaths. He took a step back. He turned, slowly,
so the wrist was on the lee side of his body, and he walked back
up the near wheel to Edvar's bench, and his skin at the wrist —
she watched it for a count of ten — dropped the amber back into
blue over thirty paces.

It had cost him to stand at the tailboard and have his skin do
that at her. It had cost him more than he had been going to say.
He had stood there anyway.

She filed it. Not the way she had filed his warmth a week ago.
Differently. The entry sat in its slot crooked.

She did not try to re-cut the stone.

---

By late afternoon the gap had opened.

The shoulders of Irongate fell away and the country ran out east
in a long heath whose russet the wind had been combing all day.
The fold Edvar had picked two nights ago was at the road-verge
with three walls and most of a fourth. Vane walked off the cart,
stood at the wall's corner for a count of forty, and came back.

"Pass it," she said.

Edvar clucked to the horses. The cart went on.

"He'll find it empty," Aldric said from the saddle.

"He'll find it empty."

They rode on for the better part of another hour. The wind had
dropped. The cold-metal thread had dropped with it. Absence of a
reading from the wind was not a reading; it was the wind not
working. The contract man was still back there. He would not
have turned off a cart he had been following since the ford on
the afternoon of day three because a fold came up empty. He
would take the empty fold as intelligence and press his pace up
a quarter until he had relocated them on sightline.

*Which means he closes by more than a few paces an hour after
dusk. Unless he has a lamp and is willing to risk it.*

He would not have a lamp. Lamps in open country at night were
amateur work. Horst did not hire amateurs.

She kept walking.

---

They camped at the shepherd's fold an hour past dusk.

Edvar put the cart into the lee of the longest wall. Aldric
tethered at the windward. Davan took the water-skins. Maren was
building the fire — small, in a pocket of wall that held the
light in — before Vane had decided whether to sit down.

She sat down eventually, at the leeward wall, with her back
against the stone. Her legs went loose in a way that had not been
a decision. Her hands shook badly now without the cuff cover.

Maren came across the fold with a tin cup.

She did not offer it. She set it on the flat stone beside Vane's
knee.

"Not tea," Maren said. Quietly. "My mother's mother used to boil
this down for women who shook in the winter — sweet-flag root,
and a piece of ginger, and a pinch of the dry mint I packed.
It's not a cure. It's a thing that slows the hands for a half-
hour. Drink it or don't."

Vane looked at the cup.

"Hot?"

"Hot enough. Not scalding. You won't chip the cup."

Vane reached for the cup. She needed two hands. She did not
hide the needing. She drank it in three small swallows. The
sweet-flag hit the back of the throat first, bitter and resinous
the way sweet-flag was, and the ginger came after, and the mint
was the thing that made it drinkable. The heat went down and sat
where the heat sat when it was doing a job.

Her hands stopped shaking about a minute later. Not all at
once. They went from the fine fast tremor to a slower coarser
one to a series of long pauses between the tremors to nothing.
She breathed out a breath she had not been planning to breathe
out.

Maren was already on the other side of the fire turning Davan's
knife in the coals to heat a rasher for Edvar.

She had made no production of any of it. She had brought the
cup, put it on the stone, said what was in it, and gone back to
the fire. The going-back-to-the-fire was what made the bringing
possible. If she had stood over Vane to watch her drink, the
drinking would have been a different thing. She had not stood
over. She had put the cup down and walked away, and the walking
away had given Vane a door.

Vane sat with the empty cup in her lap and the half-hour of
quiet hands ahead of her and the old professional ledger in her
head trying to book the transaction — *gift, received, cost — *
and the ledger refused the entry. The ledger had not been built
for this kind of transaction. This was not a gift. Maren had
named it. *A thing that slows the hands for a half-hour.* It was
a tool in a herbal kit. It was Maren doing for her what Maren
had done for her own ankle at the woodcutter's shelter, Vane now
understood, because Vane had asked Maren about the ankle on the
road north two weeks ago and Maren had said, *I wrapped it. I
went on.* The same register. The body was a body; the body did
what bodies did; you made it a little more bearable and you went
on.

Not care. Craft.

She had been braced for care. Care would have been harder to
receive. The craft was easier because the craft did not require
a gratitude she did not have the coin for tonight.

---

She slept at first watch against her will.

Aldric took the fire. Davan took the wind side. Maren took the
space between her and the open side of the fold, which was, she
registered as she was going under, a position Maren had taken
without its being discussed. She had gone to sleep on the inside
of Maren's shoulder-line by the fold wall, and Maren had placed
herself between the open and the inside, and Maren's position was
the position a professional would have taken.

Maren was not a professional. Maren was a seventeen-year-old
from a hamlet who had learned to read rooms because reading
rooms had been the only way to survive her own village. She had
brought the same instrument to the fold.

*Not a professional,* Vane corrected. *Somebody who has been
forced into the adjacent country without the forced part showing.*

She slept.

---

She dreamed of nothing. The nothing was a mercy.

When she came up out of it the fire had gone down and the wind
had dropped and the stars were very cold overhead. Aldric was
at the wall on watch. Davan was asleep, the hawthorn piece from
the night before out of his coat pocket into his hand. Edvar
snored. Maren, three paces to her left, slept with one hand
inside her coat and her face turned toward Vane a degree more
than the fold would have required.

Vane lay on her back for a while.

Her right arm was out from under the blanket. She had not meant
to let it be out. The bark grain across her shoulder met the cold
air, and the cold went into the grain and did not travel, the way
old stone took cold and kept it. The patch at the back of her
wrist — unexamined — flaked a little more against the cuff of
her coat. She would know it in the morning. She did not look at
it.

The edges were receding.

She had not said this to herself in those words. She thought the
words now. The grain at the shoulder-edge was a degree narrower
than it had been at the city. The grain at the inside of the
elbow was starting to give up the inside of the elbow back to
skin. The body was not healing. The body was un-feeding a thing
it had been feeding. There was a difference. The difference was
the difference between a tree that stopped being watered and a
tree that was being tended back. The tree that was not being
watered did not get its old shape back. It got a smaller shape.
It lived at the smaller shape if the other conditions held.

She was going to get a smaller shape.

The shape would have her nose in it. Her nose would be the nose
she had been born with, sharpened by ten years of use, un-
sharpened by four years of drug. Less than what she had been
last week. More than what she would have been without the ten
years. A working instrument. Not the overclocked one.

She had smelled one professional man at three-quarters of a mile
today on a wind that had taken two breaths to give him up.

The instrument worked.

She lay in the cold with her arm out of the blanket and counted
the working instruments she had, the way she had used to count
kit at the start of a contract.

*Left hand, shaking, usable. Right hand, shaking, usable. Legs,
tired, usable. Eyes, tired, good at distance. Ears, off, coming
back in a week. Nose, working. Arm, losing bark at the edges.
Knife, in pack. Rope, in pack. Lock-tools, in pack. The man at
three-quarters of a mile, catalogued. The party, here, sleeping.
The disc in the dark-haired woman's coat, across the fold.*

She got to the end of the list.

She started again.

She stopped at *nose, working* and did not go further.

*Nose, working,* she thought. And then, because the ledger was
broken in places tonight but was not broken here: *mine.*

She pulled the blanket up over the arm. She closed her eyes.
Across the fold, Aldric made the small motion a man made at a
wall on watch when the hour changed — a shift of his weight from
one hip to the other — and the fold took the sound and kept it
and did not give it to the country outside.

Somewhere out in the heath, at a distance she could no longer
smell past the fold wall, a contract man was going to bed down
cold and wake at first light and press his quarter-pace.

She would be awake before he was.

The tremor in her left hand started up again under the blanket.
She did not catalogue it. The cataloguing, tonight, had done its
work.

She slept.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Seven — *Maren*
### The Hill

---

Day five opened in a light that did not match the day.

The heath behind them had kept the grey overcast of the coast for two
days, a weather that did not break and did not deepen. That morning
broke. Maren came out from under the hedgerow where she had slept and
stood at the cart-pole and looked east and the east had a pale blue
edge above the ridge-line, thin and unconvincing, and the sky behind
her was still the sky of the coast. Two weathers. A seam between them
running north-to-south along a line Maren could not quite place until
the wind gave it to her — the wind had changed at some hour while she
slept, and was coming now off the east country, dry, with no salt in
it.

Dry air off dry land. That was the eastern weather line and they had
crossed it overnight.

She catalogued the camp. Cart in the lee of a long stone wall, Edvar
still asleep under the bench, the horses cropping at a forage she did
not know the name of. Aldric had the last watch and was sitting with
his back to an outcrop at the wall's end, the bay tethered at his
knee. Davan was at the water-skins. Vane was not visible.

Vane was not visible because Vane had been up and moving since the
watch changed, and Vane worked the perimeter by a pattern Maren had
stopped trying to track. Vane would be back when Vane was done.

Her hand was inside her coat at the pocket without her asking it to
be there.

The disc was warm against the pad of her thumb. Not the faint warmth
of the early morning. A steady warmth that had been there when she
woke — that had *woken her*, before the light had. She understood
this on the second breath. It pulsed once under her thumb and she
registered the pulse and took her hand out.

She did not look at the east.

Not yet. The east would be there when she was ready to look at it.
She went to the water-skins first, because the water-skins were what
you did before the day, and today was a day on which she wanted to
have done the things you did before the day.

---

Davan was filling the second skin at the stream.

He looked up when she came. His skin at the open collar carried a
sustained green — not the green-gold of the face, but a held lower
register, the green he had walked in for two days and had not
mentioned. The color at the wrist was deeper this morning. Not
fluttering. Set. As if something he had been holding at bay had given
up being held and had simply begun to be.

"Morning," she said.

"Morning."

He passed her the full skin without her asking. She took it and
shouldered it and stood beside him at the stream. The stream ran
clear over a bed of flat pale stones. Small.

"You slept," she said.

"Some."

"You're lit."

"I know."

He capped the second skin. His hands were steady. She watched them
because watching his hands was a way of watching him that did not
require him to say anything, and this morning he did not look like a
person she wanted to ask questions of. He looked like a person
carrying a weight with both hands.

"Since when," she said.

"Since the wind changed."

"The wind changed before dawn."

"Since then."

She nodded.

He did not tell her the rest. The rest would be what Davan brought to
the cart when Davan had finished circling it, and she had learned to
let him circle. She capped her skin. The water was very cold in the
bend of her arm.

"Maren."

She looked at him.

His eyes were not on her. They were on the east, above the ridge-line
where the pale blue had opened. He did not lift his hand to point.
Pointing was not the register. He said her name and he looked east
and his collar-color deepened by a shade she had not seen before —
not green-gold, not yet; a green with a warmer edge coming into it at
the base, the color of a thing beginning to be ready.

"Yes," she said. "I'll look."

She turned.

---

The ridge was ten miles off. Maybe less. The country east of the
heath rolled away in a long slow fall to a plain she could not see
the bottom of, and beyond the plain the ridge-line went up in the
pale unstained blue of the new weather. Forested at the bottom. Bare
rock toward the top. A long crest with three or four bumps along it,
the kind of ridge-line that resolved at closer distance into separate
hills.

Behind the ridge, further in, higher up, there was a hill that was not
any of the bumps.

She did not see it immediately. The light came first. The
sun was not up yet — the light was the pre-light, the grey-bluing
that filled the east before a dry clear morning — and along the ridge
the pre-light was picking out the usual edges: rock-face, tree-line,
snow in the higher gullies. Beyond the ridge, in what should have
been shadow, something caught a light that wasn't there yet.

A pale dull gleam. Not reflection. Not brightness. A not-quite-shadow
in a place that ought to have been shadow. The eye moved past it
because the eye was looking for something else, and then the eye came
back.

She looked.

The hill was long. She could see the length of it from the heath
because the ridge in front of it was lower than it was. Long and
low-backed and running roughly east-southeast, rising not in a peak
but in a long swell that came up from the land and settled back into
it without a summit. Her first thought was *esker* — the word Edvar
had taught her for the long glacial ridges south of Stillwater — and
her second thought was that eskers did not do that with light. An
esker took a light the way grass took it. Softly. This took it the
way a wet slate did. Dully. Held for a moment. Released.

It was the wrong shape for a hill.

That was the sentence she arrived at on the second look. Not too
large. Not too small. *Wrong.* A hill was a thing that had been
shaped by water and weather and the slow compression of the weight of
the country above it. This thing had been shaped by something else.
The proportions were too long for the height. The back was too even.
The flanks curved at a rate a hillside did not curve — curved at an
even rate, the rate of a thing that had been made at a scale and then
buried.

Centuries of growth. Vines and roots and sediment in every hollow.
The long overgrowth had given it the texture of a hill and almost the
silhouette of one, and from the valley road — Maren understood this
without having been on the valley road — from the valley road at any
hour but this one, at any angle but this one, at any light but the
sideways pre-light of the wrong weather, the thing would read as a
hill.

It was not a hill.

She had known this from Reth's report, carried to her through Aldric
on the third evening east. Four hundred meters. No seam. No grain.
Manufactured surface. She had heard the words and held them in a room
in her head where she held the words for things that were real.
Seeing the thing was not the same as having the words for it. Seeing
the thing was a door opening in the room, and the thing walking
through.

Her hand was inside her coat. The disc was humming.

Not pulsing. *Humming.* A low continuous warmth against her palm that
had not been there ten minutes ago at the stream. She closed her
fingers around it and opened them and closed them again and the hum
did not change, did not fall away at the breath between pulses as it
had always done. The breath between pulses had closed up. The disc
was doing the thing it had been doing more and more often all month,
now without stopping.

"All right," she said. Aloud. To no one.

It meant something had settled.

---

Aldric had come down off the outcrop.

He was on the heath ten paces from her with the bay at his shoulder
and he was looking east with both hands on the reins in front of him,
palms up, the reins laid across the palms the way a man laid a
sentence on a table for reading. She had not heard him come. She had
not needed to. She stepped across the heath to him and stood at the
bay's off-shoulder and did not speak.

He spoke first.

"Four hundred meters lateral extent."

His voice was measured. The voice she had learned from him was the
voice he used to count. A Keeper's voice, he had called it once,
without making it a joke — the voice of a man trained to receive an
account. He was receiving an account now. The account was the shape
on the ridge.

"Running east-southeast," he said. "Low at the southwestern end.
Rising to a crest at about the midpoint. The crest is not a summit.
It holds level for — I can't estimate from here. Then the curve comes
down at the southeastern end. Curves *down*. That is the piece Reth
marked. The surface goes into the ground there."

"Goes in," she said.

"He said it. *The thing goes into the ground, Keeper. It doesn't just
sit on it.* I did not understand at the time what he was describing.
I understand now."

She understood too. The long swell on the ridge was not a ridge-top
feature. It was a piece of a thing that had come to rest at an angle
in the country, the larger part of it buried under the hillside, the
smaller part of it showing. A thing lying down in the earth. A thing
that had *come down* and the earth had taken most of it, and the part
that still showed had gone under grass and vine and tree and had
become, for every eye that had looked at it without the words, a
hill.

"Reth's recess," she said.

"Southeastern end. Where the curve goes down."

"How far in."

"From the angle and the light — I can't be precise. An hour's walk
from the base. Perhaps less if the way is clear."

"It won't be clear."

"No."

He did not lift his hand to the disc in her coat. He did not ask. She
offered it anyway, without taking it out. "It's been humming since
dawn."

"Humming."

"Not pulses. Continuous."

He took this in. He had put his hand in his pocket for nothing twice
in the last hour — the shape of the lamp-tic without the tic, she
thought, not a reach but a ghost of one, fading. His hand on the
reins did not move.

"We should go," he said. "Before the rest of the day happens to us."

---

They broke camp without speaking.

Edvar was awake by the time she got back to the cart and did not ask
why they were packing early; Edvar had learned a great deal in his
sixty years about not asking. Davan folded the canvas. Maren rolled
the blankets and packed the kettle wet because there was no time for
the kettle to dry. Aldric tightened the bay's girth a hole tighter
than yesterday. The bay was losing weight.

Vane came back from her circuit as the last strap went down.

She looked at the east without going still the way Maren had gone
still. Maren understood this later; Vane had seen the hill already,
had seen it on her perimeter circuit in the pre-light and had walked
the rest of the circuit with what she had seen cataloged and put in
its proper slot, which was the slot for the thing they had come here
for. Vane's eyes came back from the east and went to Maren and did
not stay.

"He's closer," Vane said.

"How close."

"Half a mile. Maybe a little more. He pushed pace last night. He
knows we've seen the ground ahead."

"He can see it too."

"He's not looking at it the way you're looking at it."

Maren nodded.

"He won't close before the base of the ridge," Vane said. "Professional.
He'll stay at half a mile until he has cover. I'll take the back
line. Davan point. Aldric the cart. You with the disc."

"Me with the disc."

"That was the job from the ford. It's still the job."

A beat. Vane's left hand at her side was shaking at a rate Maren had
not seen it shake before. She did not hide it. She had stopped hiding
it three days ago.

"Can you work," Maren said.

"Yes."

"If you can't."

"You'll know. You'll put me in the cart and go on. The way we
agreed."

"All right."

Vane turned. She went to the back of the line and took her place
without further sentence. Davan took point. The cart rolled.

Edvar on the bench, the reins in his hands. Maren walked at the
off-wheel with her hand inside her coat. She did not take the disc
out. Taking the disc out was for the place. The place was ahead of
them across five miles of heath and two gullies and a rising tree
line and the curve of an approach she had seen all her life in
dreams her mother had not lived to interpret for her.

The wind was at her back, pushing her into the east.

---

They dropped off the heath an hour before noon.

The country folded. A dry creek bed in a trough of sedge gave the
cart half an hour of difficult going; Edvar worked the reins and
Davan walked at the near wheel to steady the trunk; the trunk, Maren
knew without being told, was the face in its oiled canvas, and the
face in its oiled canvas was part of this going-east in a way the
earlier going-east had not been. The face at the fold had been
evidence. The face in the trunk today was a piece of the ship riding
in a cart toward the ship.

She did not think this for long. She did not want to think it for
long. There was a register at which the trip became too clean — *the
piece returning to the whole* — and the register was not hers. She
was a girl walking east with a thing in her coat. The thing was warm.
The east was an hour away.

The ridge came up.

They took it where Edvar had picked the line off an older map —
Reth's directions through Aldric, layered with Edvar's road-sense,
laid onto a track that hunters had used and farmers before them. The
track climbed through the tree-line and out the other side onto a
shoulder of bare stone. They left the cart at the shoulder. Edvar
stayed with it. He had said, half a mile back, *I'll stand the cart*,
and nobody had said otherwise, and Maren understood why he had said
it — Edvar was not going the rest of the way; Edvar had gone far
enough.

She hugged him, briefly, before she went on.

He put his hand on her hair and took it away.

"Go," he said.

"I'll come back."

"You'll come back when you come back."

---

The approach was half an hour.

Davan walked point at a pace that was not his usual pace. He was
moving the way he moved when his skin had work to do and his legs
were trying to give his skin room for the work. His collar was green
now with the warm edge coming up from underneath — not green-gold.
Close to it. A green with a live under-register, as if the color
itself had a pulse, the pulse running at a rate that did not match
his heart.

Aldric came behind her. The bay had been left with the cart. Aldric
walked with a scholar's rolling stride, measured, the rhythm of a man
who had walked aisles.

Vane was behind Aldric. She had not changed position. The back line.

Maren walked the middle. She walked with one hand at her coat pocket
and the disc humming against her palm through wool and the other
hand at the knife at her belt, which she did not need and did not
take out, because the hand knew how to be there and she let the hand
do what the hand did.

The tree-line thinned.

The slope under her boots was the slope of a hillside but it was not
the slope of a hillside. It was too steady. Too even. The angle
varied within a range a natural slope did not vary within. The
ground was softer than it should have been where the growth had
pillowed over it, and harder than it should have been where the
growth had given way — and where it had given way, under a split of
moss knocked loose by some animal, something pale and flat showed
through, for the first time, that was not stone.

She did not stop. Davan, ahead, did not stop. He had seen it too.
There was seeing and there was stopping, and today they were going to
save their stopping for the thing Reth had described.

They climbed.

---

Davan's skin was green-gold by the time they came out onto the
shoulder of the hill.

Not sustained the way it had been at Edvar's. Deeper. A green-gold
that had a second color under it, the warmer color she had not seen
rise to the surface yet, the color she understood without language
was not going to surface until the disc was in the recess. His hand
at his side was trembling in the way Vane's had trembled at the cart
— not from cost, in his case. From the work of holding.

"Slow," he said. Not to her. To himself.

"All right," she said, because she had it to give him.

Aldric came up beside her. His breathing was the breathing of a
fifty-three-year-old man who had walked a slope. His hand at his coat
was closed on nothing. No tic. The hand was flat against the wool.

He looked along the hillside.

"There," he said.

She looked where he was looking.

At first only the green. The slope of the hill rolled away
from them under the dense overgrowth, vines ropey at the ankle,
deeper-rooted plants higher up, a thorn-cover in the middle-ground
that she would have taken for a briar-patch in any other country.
Under the green was the shape of the hill — curved, long, going down
and away to the southeast, the land folding to meet it in a way Maren
understood now was the land having folded *around* it, the way water
folded around a stone set in a stream.

She followed the curve with her eye down to the southeastern end.

Where the slope went down into the country, the overgrowth changed.
The thorn was sparser there. The grass ran in lines. Where the grass
ran in lines, there were longer lines underneath — longer than any
root, more even than any grass, more deliberate than the lines
erosion made in the sides of hills. The lines curved.

Three of them first. Three nested arcs, shallow, faint under the
grass, following the curve of the hillside's surface as it went down
into the ground.

She counted.

Not three. Four. The fourth arc was further out, drifting from the
three, the drift she had looked at on her own disc's edge for three
years and had thought was a flaw in the casting and had learned, at
the cooperage, was the shape the book of the world made when a
cartographer had stood far enough back from his own map to see it.

The inner orbit symbol. On the hill.

"Carved," she said, and as she said it she was already correcting
herself because it was the wrong word — her mouth had reached for it
before her eye had finished looking, and her eye was telling her a
thing her mouth had not caught up to.

"No," Aldric said. Quietly.

She looked longer.

The arcs were not cut into the surface. The grass had grown over
them. The roots of the larger plants had followed them — ran along
the lines, not across them, the way tree roots in an old orchard
followed the rows a plowman had cut two centuries ago. The plants
were growing along the lines because the lines had been there first.
The lines had been there before there was soil on the hill. The soil
had settled into the lines because the lines had been an *unevenness*
in the surface below the soil, a depression in whatever the hill was
made of, a shape pressed into the hull of the thing before there had
been any grass on Gaius at all.

The symbol was not on the hill.

The symbol *was* the hill.

Not the whole hill. The piece of the hill that showed. The material
of the hull had been marked at its making with the diagram of the
mission it had been part of, and the mark had been there before any
hand from this country had ever thought of making a mark, and ten
thousand years of roots and vines and sediment had grown across the
mark and into it and had become, to the eye looking from a valley
road in any ordinary light, *a hill.*

Her disc.

The inner orbit symbol on its edge. Three years of tracing it with
her thumb without knowing what she was tracing. A shape made at
manufacture — one thing to the maker, something else to all the
people who had come after. She
thought of her father, who had never seen the disc and the hill in
the same room, and who had walked east to try to put them in the
same room, and who had not.

Her eye went from the symbol on the hillside to the southeastern end
and down.

Down to where the curve of the hill went into the ground.

Down to where, in the quieter place below the drifting fourth arc,
the cleared ground Reth had described lay — a patch of earth not
overgrown, not by accident, where a hand had cleared sediment from
the surface a generation ago and left it cleared, and where the
surface below it showed.

A disc-shaped recess.

Dark. Small. Coin-sized at this distance, disc-sized when she got to
it.

Her breath went out.

She walked toward it.

---

She found him halfway there.

She was not looking for him. She was looking at the recess, and her
body was walking under the part of her that was looking, and her
foot at the fifth pace came down on something that was not the
springy compressed growth of the hillside. It was harder than that.
She looked down.

A leather strap. Flat under her boot. Cracked from years of weather,
the oil long gone out of it. She lifted her foot. The strap lay
against the ground curving slightly — the curve of a thing that had
once gone around another thing. A pack-strap. The kind a man took
east on a long journey.

She did not call out.

She went slowly down the slope from the strap, three paces, five, her
eye tracking what had been the line of the strap before it went
under the growth, and at seven paces the growth opened around a
scatter of dark objects arranged at distances no object placed itself
at.

A coat. Wool. Once grey, now the color weather made. The shoulders
of the coat were not in the shape of a body any more; the shoulders
of the coat were in the shape of the ground. Everything that had
filled the coat ten years ago had gone back to the hill at the rate
the hill took things back. Inside the coat — she did not look closely
into the coat — were the long pale shapes a body reduced to when the
country had worked on it for a decade. Bones. Not many, where there
should have been many. Animals had come. The animals had taken what
animals took. The rest had stayed because the coat had kept the rest
together while everything else went.

She knew the coat.

She had not seen it on him. She had been six. But she had heard Ulla
describe it the day Ulla had given her the letter — *your father's
coat was a soft grey, with dark facings at the cuffs, and a seam
along the shoulder where your grandmother mended it after the winter
of the deep snow.* The facings at the cuffs were black-weathered
almost beyond reading. The seam at the shoulder was a seam.

She went down to her knees beside the coat. She did not touch it.

The satchel was at what had been his hand.

A leather satchel, also gone dark with weather, the strap broken
where the hand had held it. She touched the strap. The leather came
apart in her fingers, not at the fracture but two inches above it,
like old bread. She drew her hand back.

Inside the satchel — she had to lean to see — were three things she
could make out without disturbing it. A small bound book wrapped in
what had been oiled cloth; the oil was gone and the cloth was not
protecting the book any more, but something in the shape of the
binding said the book had been kept dry for a long time. A fire-tin
with a flint. A stone.

The stone was the size of an egg. Round. River-smoothed. The kind of
stone a man picked up because the shape of the stone felt correct
under the thumb and the thumb wanted to keep it.

She looked at the stone and did not take it.

She looked at the coat and did not touch it.

She looked along the hand-line — the line of where his hands had
lain when he had settled, in whatever last minute of whatever last
hour, into the ground here — and she followed the hand-line down the
slope with her eyes.

The hand-line pointed at the recess.

He had been facing it. He had gone as far as he had gone, and he had
stopped where he had stopped, and he had stopped four paces short of
the recess, and he had lain down with his hands out in front of him
and his face toward the thing he had come across the country to put
the key into. The key had not been with him. The key had been in a
coal-miner's cottage in a hamlet called Ashwick, in the coat of an
infant nobody had named Maren yet, carried there by some route she
would not know the shape of for the rest of her life. The key had
been there, and the lock had been here, and the man who had known
about both had lain down four paces from the lock with his hands
out.

She did not say anything.

She did not weep. The weeping was a country she could not afford
today. She took a breath and did not take another, and then she took
another anyway because the body did what the body did, and she sat
back on her heels in the grass beside what was left of her father
and she put her hand inside her coat and she held the disc against
her palm and let it hum.

She had been carrying grief. The cooperage had taught her she was
carrying a test. The hill taught her what she had not had the words
for, which was that she had been carrying both, and both had been
true, and the reframe had not been an exchange. It had been an
addition. Her father had died here. Her father had also left her the
thing that opened the thing he had died beside. Both at once. No
contradiction.

*All right,* she thought. Not aloud.

She put her hand on the ground beside the coat — not on the coat —
and she held it there for the length of one breath. The ground was
warmer than the air. She did not know if that was the hillside or
the morning sun or the thing she was kneeling beside. She did not
look for an explanation.

She took her hand up.

She stood.

---

Aldric had come within a pace and had stopped.

His eyes were on the coat and the hand-line and the recess in one
long look that took them all in as a shape. He did not go closer. He
had the sense to know that this was not a room he entered.

"Ten years," he said. Quietly.

"Ten years."

"He knew where it was."

"Yes."

"He had nothing to put in it."

"He had me. He had me in Ashwick and the disc was with me. He came
east anyway."

Aldric closed his eyes for a breath. When he opened them he did not
look at her, which was a kindness.

"The recess," he said.

"The recess."

She stepped past the coat.

Davan was at the downslope, ten paces on. He had not come closer to
the remains. He had not had to — his skin had told him at the
distance his skin told him. The green-gold at his throat was no
longer green-gold. It was a color Maren could not name. It had the
green in it and the gold in it and something else under both, a warm
register coming up from the chest and the wrist both, held, not
pulsing, the way his skin had held the recognition at the face but
deeper and wider and at a register she had not known a body could
hold at. He looked like a lamp with a color inside it the lamp-maker
had never tried before.

"Davan."

He looked at her. His pupils were wide.

"Can you stand here," she said.

"Yes."

"All right."

She turned to the recess.

---

It was six feet below her. A short clear distance down the slope.

The cleared section was perhaps three paces across. The surface of
the hill in that cleared section was not green. It was not grass and
not soil. It was the dark material she had spent three months
learning the look of — the material of the face, the material of the
container, the material of her own disc — but at a scale that was
not possible for a thing to be. Not a coin. A continent of the
material, extending off under the soil in every direction, visible
only here where someone had scraped a window clear. The surface was
smooth. Not polished. Smooth the way the disc was smooth, at the
scale of a room.

The recess was in the middle of the cleared section.

Circular. Sunk perhaps a quarter-inch into the surface. The size of
the disc exactly. On the inner face of the recess — visible as she
stepped down into the cleared place and bent, because the angle
from above had not been the angle for reading it — was
the same symbol. The inner orbit. Four arcs. The fourth drifting
off-true.

Not carved into the inner face. Part of it.

She knelt.

The ground at the edge of the cleared section was firm under her
knee. The surface of the hill at the edge of the cleared section was
cool under her free hand — she put her hand flat against it, not
pressing, only resting, and the cool went into her palm and did not
travel further, the way stone took cool and kept it at the surface.
It was not stone. It felt like stone. It felt like nothing else she
had touched before.

She took her hand off.

She took the disc out of her coat.

It was warm. Almost hot. The hum in her palm had gone past the point
where she could call it a hum; it was a fine continuous pressure, a
thing-wanting-to-go-somewhere, and she had understood at the cooperage
that this was what the disc did when it was near the thing it had
been made to meet, but she had not understood *this*. The disc in her
palm was a thing asking to leave her palm. She had been a holder for
it for seventeen years and she was about to stop being the holder,
and the disc knew.

She looked at the symbol on the edge of the disc.

She looked at the symbol on the inner face of the recess.

The same symbol.

"Papa," she said. Very quietly. The old word. She had not used the
word since she was six. She used it as a small acknowledgement, the
way you tapped a door-frame as you went past a room somebody you had
loved had slept in. A courtesy
between her and the country.

She did not weep.

She held the disc over the recess.

---

The disc fit.

Her hand was steady. She had expected her hand to shake and her hand
did not shake, which was information she tucked in a pocket for
later. The disc went into the recess the way a key went into a lock
that had been made for the key. Not loose. Not tight. Exactly fitted.

The contact came before the seating. A moment where the material of
the disc met the material of the hill — not a click, not a sound at
all, but a joining, a thing-become-one-thing under her thumb — and
then the seating, a quarter-inch of settle, the disc sinking the
rest of the way into the recess and stopping at a depth that was
the correct depth.

Her thumb was on the symbol on the back of the disc. She pressed.

She had not been told to press. The pressing was what the hand knew.
The thumb went down on the inner orbit and the disc sat the last
fraction of its depth into the hillside and then the hum in her palm
that had been continuous all morning — *stopped.*

Not faded. *Stopped.*

The disc under her thumb went still. Completely. For the first time
since she had taken it out of her coat three years ago and held it
for the first time with a hand that knew what it was, the disc was
*quiet* in her hand.

A beat.

Her breath was out. She had not inhaled. She inhaled.

The quiet held for the space of that breath.

Then.

---

Under her knee, the hill.

Not a shake. Not a tremor. A *shift* at a register her body caught
before her mind caught it — a change in the way the ground was under
her, the way a room changed when a furnace came on in another part
of a house, before any sound from the furnace had reached you. The
change went through the hillside and through her kneecap and up her
thigh and stopped somewhere in her ribs.

The air changed.

Not the wind. The *air*. The air between her body and the hillside
went from morning-cool to a different cool — a dry held cool that
had no weather in it. The air an interior had. The air of a room
that had been closed for a very long time, and was no longer closed,
and was meeting the morning for the first time across a threshold
that had just gone un-sealed.

Somewhere below her — below the hillside, below the disc, below
depths she had no measure for — a sound started.

Not a sound at the ear. A sound at the bone. A low continuous
register at the edge of hearing, deeper than the disc's hum had
been, wider, the sound a thing the size of the hill made when the
thing the size of the hill began, after ten thousand years, to do
the work it had been built for.

Davan made a sound above her.

She did not look up. She could not look up yet. Her eye was on the
disc in the recess, and the disc in the recess was no longer dark.
Along the symbol on its face — along the four nested arcs, along the
drifting fourth — a faint light had come up. Not blue. Not the blue
the disc had given her in the woodcutter's shelter and the Stillwater
kitchen and the cooperage basement. A color she had not seen the
disc make before. Warmer. The color of morning sun through a leaf,
held at its brightest for a breath and then not fading but *settling*
— the color finding its working register and staying there.

The symbol lit under her thumb.

The hillside lit under her knee.

Not the whole hillside. The four arcs of the symbol she had seen
under the grass, the arcs that had been part of the surface before
any of this country's grass had ever grown — the arcs began, faintly,
to be visible through the growth. Not bright. Just a suggestion that
the material was no longer inert. That something under the hill
was waking to the signal at the recess and beginning, with the slow
patience of something that had been waiting, to answer.

Aldric, behind her, said one word.

She did not hear it. Her ear was full of the bone-sound. She
registered his voice at the level of vibration in the air and did
not turn.

Her hand was on the disc. The disc was seated. The seating was done.

The hillside had woken under her knee. The air had changed. The
bone-sound was sending. The symbol under her thumb was lit with a
color the disc had never produced in her hand before.

She knelt.

She kept her thumb on the symbol.

She did not take it off.

Behind her, down the hand-line that had pointed at this recess for
ten years, the coat lay in the grass with nothing in it, and a
father who had not reached this seating had reached, through her,
the thing he had walked east to reach. She did not look back at the
coat. She looked at the recess. She looked at the light coming up
under her thumb. She kept her thumb where her thumb was.

The hill answered under her.

She held on.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Eight — *Davan*
### What Was Inside

---

The hillside was doing something.

He stood three paces up-slope of Maren's knee and did not move. His
hand had gone to the side of his coat without his hand asking him,
and his hand was flat against his ribs the way a hand went flat
against something inside a coat that wanted to get out. He did not
name the thing inside his ribs. He kept his hand where his hand was.

Under her thumb the small symbol on the back of the disc was lit in
the warmer color. The hillside was lit where the four arcs lay, in
the same warmer color but very faint, like coals under ash from a
night fire that had been banked for a long time and was, now, being
asked a question about whether it was still a fire.

The coals were saying yes.

Not in a rush. A slow spread along the arcs — not brighter exactly,
but *more present*, the arcs becoming something the hill was
instead of something in the hill — and where the arcs ran under the
overgrowth the overgrowth was not burning, was not dying, was only
*moving aside*. Vines the thickness of a wrist went slack at their
anchor-points, slid a hand's-breadth, re-seated. A clump of
bracken that had been wedged between two lines in the surface for
who knew how long sagged sideways and lay flat. A low thorn
branch that had grown in the crook of the easternmost arc for long
enough to have the arc in its grain came away at the root, not
torn, not pushed — *released*, the way a thing was released when
the surface under it decided it did not need to hold that thing
there any longer.

He watched it and his mouth was a little open.

Across a pace of hillside the growth was quietly unbuilding itself.

He thought about the way a tide went out. He thought about the way
a shore appeared along the length of a beach, not by the water
leaving but by the sand admitting that the sand had always been
under the water. The hillside was doing that. What was under the
growth had always been under the growth, and the growth was, with a
courtesy he did not know how to place, stepping aside.

Aldric was behind him. He had not heard Aldric come up. The older
man's breathing was small and even and did not draw attention.
Davan kept his eye on the hill.

---

The breach opened.

Not a door. There was no door. There was an unmaking of what had,
for a long time, been hillside, along a seam that had always been
the seam of the breach — the original wound the crash had cut into
the hull, over-grown and sealed by ten thousand seasons of the
country working on it. The growth retreated the length of a stride
on either side of the seam. A darkness showed in the slope between
two curving edges of the smooth material, narrow at the bottom and
widening at about chest-height and narrowing again at the top — a
passage shaped — the shape arrived to him without his naming it —
like a place where a thing had once been torn and where the growth had, in
the ten thousand seasons of its patience, filled the tear. What had
been a sealed wound was being asked to remember that it was a wound,
and was remembering.

The tear stood open.

Cool air came out of it.

Not cold. He had known cold on this hillside all morning — wind off
the dew-wet slope, the cold of a stone you put your palm on in
autumn. This was not that. This was the air of a closed room — no
weather in it, no wind in it, the kind of cool that sat in a vault
or a cellar or the back of a long unvisited barn. A held air. The
air of an indoors that had been indoors since before anyone in this
country had a word for indoors.

The cool air met his face and did not travel past his collar. The
way the stone at the gate-wall had handed warmth down into the
earth — that was old cold. This was inside cold. It was not trying
to do anything to him. It was only being what it had been being,
for a very long time, behind the hill.

He inhaled.

The air smelled of nothing. Not dust. Not stone. Not the wet green
of the outside hillside. A clean absence of smell that was, he
found, its own smell — the way a room smelled when no one had
cooked or coughed or wept in it for longer than any person
remembered rooms.

Aldric, at his elbow, said a word. He did not catch the word. He
thought it might have been a name.

---

Maren was still on her knee.

She did not lift her thumb from the symbol. Her head had not
turned. Her hair had come loose at the left temple in the breeze
she had climbed through earlier and the loose hair was against her
cheek and she did not tuck it back. Her eyes were on the disc.

The disc was lit warmer-leaf-through-sunlight under her thumb,
holding steady.

He understood — and understood was the wrong word; his body knew —
that the disc's stillness and the hillside's opening were the same
motion. The disc had stopped humming because the hillside had taken
up the hum. The signal was going somewhere past the hill. His body
read it as an outgoing direction, the way you could tell from the
set of a person's shoulders whether they were speaking to someone
in front of them or over their shoulder. The hill was speaking over
its shoulder, upward, at something far enough away that it took the
full register of the hill to say anything at all.

A resonance came up through his boot-soles.

Slow. Not the bone-sound Maren was carrying under her knee. A thing
above that, a long low note that was not a note, a carrying. The
rock underfoot was carrying a sending, the way the exposed slab by
the verge yesterday had carried the older register under his palm —
only that had been a whisper and this was a word.

The word was being said very quietly.

The word was being said very far.

He did not try to hear it. He was not the one it was being said to.
He was only in the room where the saying was being done.

---

"Davan."

Maren's voice. Low. She had not turned.

"Here."

"Go in."

He looked at her. Her eyes had stayed on the disc. Her thumb had
not moved. The set of her shoulder told him she had decided
something before she had spoken, and the decision was that his
being here at her knee was not what this moment was for. His being
inside was what this moment was for.

"Maren —"

"I have this," she said. "You have that."

His body said yes before his mouth did. He nodded. She did not see
the nod. She was busy holding a hillside open for him.

"Aldric comes," he said.

"Yes."

"Vane outside."

"Yes."

"Edvar at the cart."

"Yes."

The exchange was the exchange they had built on the road — small,
functional, each word a placed stone. He did not cross to her. She
did not need crossing-to. He turned and looked at Aldric, who was
already looking at the breach, and the two of them stepped forward
together.

His hand had come off his ribs. The coming-off had done itself
without him.

---

He went in first.

Aldric was half a pace behind him and did not argue. The passage
was narrow enough that a man had to turn his shoulder slightly to
enter and broad enough that he did not have to duck. The curving
edges where growth had been were smooth in the way the outer
hillside was smooth — the disc-smooth at the scale of a room,
cool-that-did-not-travel, not stone, felt like stone but not. He
did not touch them with his hands. His body did not want to touch
them yet. His body wanted to learn what the air was, first.

The air inside was the air that had come out, only more of it.

Three steps in, the outside sound stopped. Not faded. Stopped. Wind
had gone. The small creak of Edvar's cart-harness down-slope was
gone. The small river-talk far at the valley floor was gone. The
world had closed behind him, not as a trap but as a completion — a
space that was its own space now, whole inside its own boundary.

His breath arrived at the front of his ears.

Aldric's breath, a pace behind, came quicker than yesterday —
Aldric was not in his body at this moment the way the Aldric at
the outcrop yesterday had been in his body, steady; this Aldric
