was in the body of a man approaching the thing he had travelled
thirty years to approach, and the body was doing what bodies did
when they arrived somewhere they had only thought about.

He did not turn to look at Aldric. He kept walking.

The floor had a give that was not the give of earth. A floor. It
had not been earth under his boot since two steps in. The change
had passed under his boot-heel and he had not marked it and now,
attending, he could feel the material through his sole — the same
cool density he had felt in Corvel's container at the cooperage
table, in Edvar's face the night of the green-gold, in the
hillside under Maren's knee. Floor of it. Walls of it. He was
inside a room made of the material the debris field had been
scattering pieces of across sixty miles of country for long enough
that no song still knew what the pieces were.

A piece of his mind, far back, said: *all of one thing*.

He did not chase the thought. He circled it and let it settle and
walked on.

---

The passage opened.

Not into a large room. Into a space the size of a winter byre —
low-ceilinged, roughly oblong, the air of it cool and held. The
walls were the dark material. The floor was the dark material.
There was light, and the light was coming from nowhere he could
name — not lamps, not windows. The light was in the walls in a way
he could not parse and did not try to. It was the cold-precise
light of the face, scaled up — light that had been *made* to the
specification of seeing-by, not the specification of warming.

The banks came to him first.

Along the two long walls, at about hip-height, ran low shelves.
Not shelves like the ones in Edvar's shop, made of wood and set
with pegs. Shelves as *part* of the wall — the wall deepened into
shelf at that register, the way stone in a country chapel
sometimes had a recess carved into it for a lamp. The shelves ran
the length of the room on both sides. On the shelves, row upon
row, sat objects.

He did not know the word for what they were.

Small. A man could have held one in two hands without strain. Flat
on one side and curved on the other, like a river-stone that had
been shaped by a hand that cared about its shape. Dark, the same
dark as the walls. A dozen and a dozen and a dozen of them, going
down the length of both walls in a pattern so regular that the
pattern was the first thing he read — not a row, a *system*. Each
object at the same distance from the next. Each at the same height.
Each oriented the same way.

He stopped at the first shelf and did not touch.

Aldric, a pace behind him, made a sound. It was not a word. It was
the sound a man made when a sentence he had been holding all his
life had just been shown the object it was about.

"Davan."

"Yes."

"Do you see."

"Yes."

They stood at the end of the first shelf and looked at the hundred
quiet objects on it, and the hundred quiet objects on the wall
opposite. Davan did not count. Counting was Aldric's work. His own
work was something else and he did not know it yet.

His shoulders opened.

He had not known his shoulders had been closed, on the road and at
the recess and in the passage. They had been. They opened now, by a
small margin, the way shoulders opened when a body discovered that
the room it had walked into contained nothing that wanted anything
from the body. The room did not want him. The room did not want
anyone. The room had been doing the work it had been set to do,
and the work it had been set to do was not a work that required
anyone.

This was, he understood slowly and without saying it, not a weapon
room. It was not a treasure room. It was not the room any song
about this hill had taught anyone to imagine.

It was a room full of kept things.

---

He walked to the nearest shelf.

He went slowly. Not out of fear. Out of a courtesy — the courtesy a
body found when it came into a room where a great deal of
attending had been done and the attending had been done by no one
for a very long time. He walked as he might have walked through a
chapel in a village where the chapel was not his, where the people
who had built it were under the earth, where the only thing left
of their work was the work itself and the work deserved not to be
walked on carelessly.

He stopped a hand's-breadth from the first object on the shelf.

He did not pick it up. He had no right to pick it up yet. Maybe he
never would. The picking up was Aldric's hand's work when Aldric's
hand arrived at it, and the hand had not.

He put his palm at the level of the object, close but not touching,
and he let his proximity-sense do its work.

The sense came up from his palm and from the underside of his
forearm and met the object. Not a heat. Not a cold. A *presence* —
the same presence he had read off the face in Edvar's back room,
off Corvel's container at the cooperage table, off the slab at the
verge yesterday, off the hillside under Maren's thumb. The same
quality. Only *different in kind*. The face had been an instrument.
The container had been sealed. The hill was an opening-outward.
This —

This was holding.

The object was holding something the way a barn held grain, or a
jar held seed, or a cellar held a winter's worth of roots.
Holding was what it was for. Whatever was in it had been put in it
to be there, and the putting had been done with the whole intent
of the being-there — the object was not a box that had happened to
be used for storage, it was a *storage*, made for storage, the
shape of its being was to hold.

His palm came lower. Not touching. The courtesy held.

Behind the holding was the quality he had been reading all morning
and did not have a name for. It came up at him from the whole
shelf — every object on the shelf at once, braided together so that
no single object was the source — a register his body read and
could not place.

It was not loneliness.

He tried the word and the word did not fit. The objects were not
lonely. Loneliness was a thing bodies felt when they were built
for company and were not in company. These objects had never been
built for company. They had been built for *being there*. The
difference was a whole language apart.

It was not patience, exactly, though patience was close. Patience
was what a person did when they were waiting and knew they were
waiting. The objects did not know they were waiting. The objects
were only *correct*, the way a well-set clock was correct in the
tick it was giving — not proud of the tick, not humble about the
tick, simply doing the tick — only without the clock's conceit of a
person checking it.

*Attending without attender*, a piece of his mind said.

He did not push the thought. He set it down at the back of the
shelf of his mind where the unfinished sentences lived.

The register pressed gently up at him and he let it press. He stood
in the full weight of it. His breath went out.

His skin began to light.

---

He did not see it. He had never seen his own skin-light the way
other people saw it. He read it by second-hand — the way a room's
walls took his color when he was lit, the way Maren sometimes
nodded at his collar, the way Lera on the barge had named her
colors for him. He knew it by how the shelf-material in front of
him changed tone under his palm.

The shelf-material changed tone.

The dark, at his palm-range, took on a warmth. Not the green-gold
of Edvar's back room. Not the held green of yesterday's road. Not
the blue of the shelter at Stillwater the disc had given Maren.
Something he did not have a word for.

It was, if he had to reach for the word — the color of honey held
up to a lamp, only cooler. The color of the edge of dawn a morning
nobody had seen, a morning the country was having for the first
time.

It was not green. It was not amber. It was not gold.

It was a color his skin had not produced before.

It was in him, at his wrists and the hollow of his throat and the
underside of his jaw, and he knew it was there because the walls
of the storage room knew it was there — the dark material held the
color for him, gave it back at his palm-range, carried it a hand's-
breadth into the material and then held it, *keeping* it, because
*keeping* was what the material did.

He did not pull his hand back. He did not step away.

He held where he was, and the storage room held where it was, and
for a beat the two of them were at the same register, lit in a
color that was neither fully his nor fully the material's but
something the two of them were making by standing this close to
one another.

Behind him, Aldric made a small sound.

---

"Davan."

Aldric's voice. Careful. Low.

"Here."

"Your skin."

"I know."

"I have not seen that color."

"No."

A pause. The pause was not awkward. The pause was the pause of a
man reading a new word on a page in a language he knew, taking the
moment the new word required, putting the word into the place in
his understanding that had been made for it.

"All right," Aldric said. The word was not Maren's word. In
Aldric's mouth it was a different instrument — an acknowledgment
of the page-edge, not a settling.

Davan lowered his palm to his thigh without turning. He took a
step back from the shelf. The color at his wrist did not fade. It
held, steady, at a register lower than the peak it had reached
while his palm was at the material, and the register it held at was
a register he had not held at before. The new register lived in
the tendons of his forearm. The skin there was, in the best-set
way a body could be, *working*.

Aldric crossed behind him to the long wall.

Davan did not watch. He listened. Aldric's steps were slow and
even and stopped at a point on the opposite shelf. A breath went
out of the older man that was the breath of a man lowering himself
to a bench. He was crouching. Davan heard the small sound of
knees, then the slower sound of hands — placed on thighs, palms
down, the gesture of a scholar sitting before a page he had not
yet earned the right to turn.

Aldric said one word.

He said it in the liturgical register.

Davan did not catch it. It was, he thought, the word for a place
in a liturgy — a fixed point, the point at which a reader laid
hands on the page before beginning — and Aldric had said it
because Aldric's body had reached the part of the room where that
word was the word his body had. He had not chosen the word. The
word had arrived because the room had asked for it.

Davan turned, slowly, at the shoulder.

Aldric was on both knees. His hands were flat on the tops of his
thighs. His back was straight. He was looking at the objects on
the shelf in front of him with the attention of a man whose whole
life had been instrument-building for this exact length of
looking. His lips moved once. Nothing came out. He closed his
mouth.

He was not reading. He was *seeing what was to be read*.

Davan recognized the quality in him. He had seen it in Edvar, in
the back room, across the lit face. He had seen it in himself. It
was the quality of a man at a threshold who knew that crossing
would require a different instrument than the one he had used to
get to the threshold, and who was, for a last held moment, being
the version of himself that had walked to the threshold, before
becoming the version of himself that would cross.

Aldric did not cross. Not yet.

Davan did not ask him to.

---

Behind them, the passage was open. The cool air was still moving
from the room outward and downward along the hillside. Davan could
feel the direction of it at his back — a breath from indoors going
out to meet the country — and the direction told him that the
storage was *venting*, that its air, held for as long as its air
had been held, was being given back to the hillside and the
hillside was giving the hillside's air in.

He walked a half-turn to the center of the room.

He stood there and he let his proximity-sense widen.

It did not feel like widening. It felt like *admitting*. The sense
had already been full — it had been reading the room since the
passage — and what widened was his willingness to let it read. He
stood at the center of the room and stopped fending.

The room came in.

Not at force. The room did not press. The room was *available*,
and his body took availability for what it was, and the register
that had been braided along the shelves became the register of
the space. He stood inside a held thing. He had been inside rooms
that had held feeling — a back bench, a barge cabin, the cooperage
basement — and every one of those rooms had held human warmth or
human cold, the leavings of bodies. This room held neither. This
room held the *shape* of waiting without any person in the shape.
It was a waiting that had gone on for so long that the waiting had
taken the shape of the material.

*What was inside*, a piece of his mind said.

He let the piece say it. He did not finish the sentence. The
sentence did not need finishing. What was inside was what was
inside. The body that was reading it was the body that was reading
it. The two facts sat in the middle of the room and did not ask
anything of him.

His skin was at the new color.

He knew it by the walls. The walls around him had taken a low
warmth from him. He was at the center of a room that was, in a
register small enough to miss and wide enough to fill the room,
the color he was making.

Somewhere above the hill — very far up, past the ceiling of the
chamber, past the crest of the hill, past the blue of the morning
the day was having outside — a resonance was happening that he
could not hear. The direction of it arrived in his back the way
the venting in his back had arrived. There was an outward. There
was a receiving. Somewhere far, very far, something was receiving
what the hill was sending, and was sending back. The sending-back
came in under the room as a pressure his inner ear caught and
dismissed before his inner ear would name it.

He did not name it.

He did not have the geography of it. He had only the register: a
saying and an answer, at a distance the word *distance* was not
built for.

He drew breath.

He stood.

---

Maren's voice arrived behind him, at the passage.

"Davan."

Small. Not stressed. Just placing him.

"Here," he said, over his shoulder.

"Aldric."

"Here," Aldric said, from the far shelf.

A pause. The pause was Maren at the outside of the passage,
reading the inside of the passage the way she read a room by
ear-and-light before stepping into it.

"Vane is on the slope," she said. "She has scent. West. One man.
Far yet."

"How far," Aldric said. Not a question he expected to get a number
on. A way of setting the fact in the room.

"Far enough to be a later thing. She wanted you to know."

"Yes," Aldric said.

"I am here," Maren said. "The disc is seated. I am not moving."

"Good," Davan said.

Another pause. Then:

"Is it a place."

"It is a place," Davan said.

"I thought it would be."

"It has —" he said, and let the word not finish because the word
that wanted to come was *holdings* and *holdings* was a word that
wanted to be Aldric's for a minute before it was his.

Maren did not push.

"All right," she said. Small. From the passage.

---

Aldric rose.

He rose slowly, as a man in his fifties rose from both knees when
the knees had been on a cool hard floor for longer than the knees
would have chosen. His hands pushed on his thighs and he came up
to a stand with the small involuntary sound a body made when it
straightened. He turned. He looked at Davan.

His face was the face he had worn at the camp at the edge of
Irongate country, on the other side of the small lamp. Only more
of it.

"They're records," he said.

"Yes," Davan said.

"All of them."

"Yes."

"Nothing else. No instruments. No —" his hand moved and stopped
and came back to his thigh. "No *control* of anything. No lever.
No dial. No — weapon, though I do not know why I expected a
weapon. No tool at all except these." He gestured at the shelves.
"The whole room is one thing."

"Yes."

"They are records."

He said it the third time the way a man said a thing when he was
making the saying itself a vessel, carrying the saying from the
place the saying had landed in his body to the place the saying
needed to go in order to become a thing he had said to another
person. Davan held the vessel with him. He did not interrupt it.
Aldric's hand had gone inside his robe, had found the cord of the
lamp he was not carrying, had not been there, had come back out
without the tic finishing. Davan registered this and did not say
it.

"It is," Aldric said, softer, "all of what was kept. Not what was
held to be used. What was held to be *kept.*"

"Yes."

"Oh," Aldric said.

It was, Davan thought, the smallest and most accurate sound a
priest had made in a long while.

---

Davan crossed back to the shelf where his palm had been.

He did not put his palm back against the material. He put it
near, again — the same hand's-breadth. The material did not give
his color back this time. It had given it back and the giving
was done and the color was in him, not in the wall. His skin
held steady at the register the wall had taught him, and the
wall held at its own register — quieter now, a little — and the
two of them were at separate registers and the separation was
correct.

He chose one of the objects on the shelf.

Not to pick up. To put a finger on. He chose the one nearest the
center of the row, because the center of a row was where a row's
attention was most evenly distributed and because he did not want
to be the person who chose an end. He extended one finger of his
right hand — his carving finger, the one that had known the
inner-orbit shape before his mind had — and he set the pad of it
against the flat side of the object. Very lightly.

The object was neither warm nor cold. It was at the same register
as the walls. It took his fingerprint's oil the way the wall would
have taken it, and gave nothing back, and was what it was.

His pad was on the object. He kept it there.

He did not lift it. He did not press harder. He kept the contact
at exactly the register at which two things could be, at once,
two things — the object still the object, his finger still his
finger, the material the material, the body the body.

A held point.

His skin at the wrist was at the new color and the new color was
not fading.

Aldric stood at the far shelf and did not cross to him and did
not speak. The room was the room.

Outside, through the length of the passage, through the breach,
through the morning where Maren's thumb was on the disc and Vane
was on the slope with a scent and Edvar was at the cart below with
the horses — the country was doing whatever the country did.
Davan did not go to find out.

The storage held.

He held with it.

And he held his pad on the object, and did not take it off, and
did not turn it over, and did not look to see if there was a
marking on the underside, and did not carry it back to the passage
for Aldric, and did not claim it, and did not pick it up.

He stayed at the shelf, with his finger on the one kept thing his
finger had chosen, and waited for Aldric to be ready to begin.

---


## Chapter Twenty-Nine — *Aldric*
### The Record

---

Davan's finger-pad was on the one kept thing his finger had chosen.

Aldric read the younger man's back the way he had read the backs of
novices at prayer — not the head, not the shoulder, but the set of
things below the shoulder blade where a body admitted the weight it
was carrying. Davan had gone past admitting. He was *in* the weight,
steady, his pad against the object and the object against the
material of the shelf, and the tension of the contact was a courtesy.

The courtesy had been offered.

He rose from the far shelf. The knees did what they did. He placed
his palms flat on the tops of his thighs, and the gesture arrived in
two languages at once — the hands-flat of a man approaching a book
whose cover had not been opened in his lifetime, and the hands-flat
of a priest lowering himself before the blessed sacrament. He let
them both be. He was tired of keeping them in different rooms.

He crossed the floor of the storage.

The dark material did not ring under his boot. His step made the
dense small sound of a floor made, at its making, to answer back
the way the floor of a library answered back — not more, not less.
He knew the sound of a floor that had been made for reading. This
was a reading floor.

He stopped a pace short of Davan and looked at the object under the
finger-pad.

It sat at the same height as its neighbors: flat on the upper side,
curved beneath. Small notation along one edge in a script Aldric did
not know. *Did not know* was the honest phrase — the letters, if
they were letters, were at the boundary where his eye reported
*written* without reporting *in what*. Not wholly foreign: he had
seen that quality once before, on the lintel-stone above the
undercroft doorway, in light he had been holding with the wrong hand.

"Davan."

"Here."

"May I."

"Yes."

The pad did not lift. The invitation had been there since before
Aldric had asked for it, and the asking had been a thing he needed
to do for himself — the last gesture of a man trained to ask before
he opened. The training was not wrong. It was, this once, not the
instrument the room required. But the gesture gave him the edge of
the page to put his hand on, and the edge was, for a man of his
vocation, the place reading began.

He put his palms at the sides of the object.

He lifted it.

The weight was the weight a maker chose when the maker had wanted a
thing carried without noting the carrying. Hand-weight. He brought
it a pace back so the wall-light fell across the upper face without
shadow of his own body, and he turned it so the edge-notation faced
him, and he waited for his eye to do what his eye did.

His eye did what his eye did. Not all at once. Of the seven
languages he had at different depths, none of them was this. But
the shapes along the edge were doing something familiar in an
unfamiliar alphabet. They were doing *numbering*.

A catalog number.

The word settled. Not a prayer. A catalog number — the mark a body
of kept material put on each of its items so the order could be
known without reading. He had spent thirty years in rooms where the
first act of respect for a shelf was to acknowledge its catalog
before picking up a single book.

He had begun to read.

---

He sat down on the floor.

Not with the dignity of a Keeper — with the practicality of a man
whose knees had been on the cold material of the far shelf longer
than the knees had asked to be. He sat cross-legged so that his eye
and the upper face of the object were at the same height. He put
his hands flat on his thighs.

The upper face was not blank. As his eye settled it resolved into
what he understood as *written* — dense, the way a page of close-set
type was dense, the letters in a careful hand running in lines. He
was not yet trying to read the lines. He was looking at their
arrangement. Paragraphs. A marker every fifteenth line that stood
out as a different sort of symbol, the way a scribe marked, in the
margin of a chronicle, the year.

This was a log. A record-in-sequence. The shape a document took
when its purpose was *to note, with date, what was true*, and not
to persuade any reader of any thing at all.

At the top of the upper face, set off from the body by the white
space a careful hand gave a heading, were two shapes. The inner-
orbit figure. And a mark he did not know.

He looked at the second mark for a long moment. The scholar did not
place it. But the body gave him a register the scholar had not
thought of: the mark was a *seal*. The shape above the opening of a
document when the document wished to announce the authority under
which it had been written.

The seal was not a church's seal. The Keepers' order had a seal; he
knew the weight of it. This was not that weight. This was the stamp
of an *office* — an enterprise. It had left this record under the
stamp of its enterprise, ten thousand seasons ago, and the stamp
had waited here to be seen.

He lowered his eye to the body of the text. He did not try to read
it. He noted that a four-letter unit appeared repeatedly, the first
letter of which was the inner-orbit figure itself, doing the work
of a letter the way a figure did the work of a letter when a
language had begun in a pictogram and moved on from the pictogram
without forgetting that the pictogram had once been the word.

*The word had once been the thing.*

He set the object back in its catalog position. He stood. The knee
protested; he let it.

He walked the shelf, establishing order. This long wall, written
registers. The opposite wall, at the same catalog distance — the
same register at different delivery. Voiced. Small indentations on
the upper faces, the sort of surface that lit when a hand settled
on it. The written on the left, the voiced on the right, at the
same dates, in the same hand.

An archive under two registers.

He did not cross to the voiced wall yet. He was not ready. He did
not want to hear a voice before he had read a document. The order
mattered to him — the written before the voiced — because the
written was the form his thirty years had prepared him for.

He chose a second object further along the first shelf.

---

He sat again.

The upper face was divided by ruling into a grid he recognized
across languages. A manifest grid: items left, quantities middle,
disposition right. His eye had looked at a thousand such grids in
estate inventories, temple-stock ledgers, pilgrim-supply accounts.
He read the columns by what the column did, not what it said.

First column: *items*. Second column: *number*. Third column:
*where assigned*.

The *where assigned* column had four distinct symbols, repeated
down the column in different combinations. Three were variants of
each other — small changes on a common shape. The fourth was a
shape apart.

He looked at the fourth symbol for a long time.

An oblong body, with a small notation at one extremity that resolved,
when his eye adjusted, into the figure of the descent line — the
drift of the fourth arc from the carving on the hillside outside,
rendered here at the scale of a column entry, doing the work of a
location-marker. The fourth symbol was a place.

The place was where he was sitting.

He counted the column. Most entries carried one of the three
variants. The remainder — a small percentage — had the fourth.
*Three of four, assigned to the three.* *One of four, assigned to
the lander.* Including, in the rows whose first-column entries he
could partly parse, rows that appeared to list people.

Crew.

The manifest was a crew manifest. It allocated crew to four
destinations. Three were orbital stations. One was the surface. The
count on the stations was greater than the count on the surface.

*Three for station, one for descent.*

He said the phrase in his head in English-of-the-country. The phrase
was what the manifest said, and the manifest was the source, and the
source was in his hand, and his hand was steady.

He had come to the mission profile.

He held it for a long breath. He had inferred this shape from the
diagrams, from the faces, from Brennan's *for unchanged hands*, from
Reth's *four hundred meters, no grain*, from the four arcs on the
hillside and what the light had done at the crest of the ridge three
mornings running. Inference had been the instrument his life had
built. But inference was always subject to the possibility that the
data were partial and the pattern, though coherent, was not the
pattern. This was not inference. The office had filed its own
manifest. The manifest said what the pattern had said.

A quieter sentence arrived under the first.

*The three that remained were not survivors. They were on post.*

He let it stand. He had been carrying, without exactly carrying, a
framing of the three moons as *the ones that made it* — the shape
the country had given itself over ten thousand years, because the
shape had the consolations a story made for the living wanted.
*Four fires in the sky. One came down to die.*

It was not the shape of the document.

The document did not say *the three survived*. The document said
*three for station*. The three had been tasked. They had gone to
their assigned places and had stayed there because staying was
their work. The work had not ended because the surface contingent
had not arrived to mark it ended. They were *performing protocol*.
They had been performing protocol for ten thousand seasons. The
protocol had held.

He closed his eyes a moment. Not prayer. The small motion of a man
accommodating a scale he had not built a room in his head for yet.
When he opened them, the manifest was still the manifest, and the
column was still the column.

His hand went inside his robe for the lamp-cord. It did not. The
hand started and stopped at the robe's shoulder and came back down
to his thigh, and he did not see the hand. His eye had gone back to
the manifest.

---

The third object was on the second shelf.

He crossed on his knees — the body had admitted its preference, and
the Keeper accepted — and chose the object at the second shelf's
start. A different format: not log, not manifest. Panels on the
upper face, each holding a diagram. Cross-sectional. The interior of
a vessel, seen from the side, compartments indicated.

He knew what he was looking at before the scholar named it. A ship.
The same shape the face in the undercroft had rendered as the
fourth of the four — the lander — but here intact, at original
design, before the damage. The compartments carried labels he could
not read. But some carried the stamp of the seal. Some carried the
four-arc figure. And at the ship's stern —

His breath caught.

A compartment label there carried a figure he had seen in his own
sealed chamber, on the inward-facing curve of the four smooth
containers. He had recorded it in his journal two months ago. He
had thought it a house-mark, a maker's sigil, a decoration.

Now it was a compartment label.

The containers in his sealed chamber had come from *that
compartment*. The mark on the containers was the mark of their
origin hold. Four storage holds at the ship's stern, each labeled
with its sub-figure — three of a family, one apart, the same grid
of differentiation as the manifest's destinations — and the fourth
hold had held the containers the Confluence temple had been built
over.

He sat with this. He had not assembled the last step before. He had
approached it — the temple-built-atop, the debris-field-under-the-
floor — in his own chamber, in a dry-stone fold, riding east. But
he had not put the containers on the manifest. He had not understood
that the objects he had knelt before for two months were
*inventoried*. That an office had filed them. That his life had been
spent tending a building that was standing on top of an inventoried
cargo hold.

He was not angry. He tested the anger; it was not there. Anger
required that someone had done something to him, and the office
whose stamp sat at the top of the first object had not done anything
to him specifically. The office had filed its documents. The office
had assumed its colony would arrive. The colony had not arrived. The
documents had stayed where they were filed. Two centuries of
Keepers at a time had done what their hands had been given them
for, without knowing that the hands were tending a floor built above
an archive.

His life had been what his life had been.

The register was — he tried the word and the word fit — *quiet*. He
was very quiet. He sat in the quiet, and the quiet was not peace;
it was the quiet of a long thing landing.

---

He stood.

He crossed to the voiced wall.

He did not yet lift one. He looked at the spacing of them. The
archive kept its forms in the forms that befit them. The written
were the written and the voiced were the voiced, at the same dates,
in the same hand. The voiced were what he had been afraid of. He
was still the Keeper whose life had been mostly text. A voice from
the office would make the office a *people*, and people, even dead
for ten thousand years, entered a room as people entered a room.

He took one register from the shelf.

Carefully. Hand-weight. The upper face carried a single panel of
indentation, and his thumb, held clear of it, felt the cool-precise
difference between panel and surrounding surface — the area that
invited contact. He was carrying the object to the floor where he
could set it down and begin, and the carrying itself was the
thinking he was doing about whether to make the contact.

He sat in the middle of the floor.

He set it down. He thought of Davan, still at the far wall, still
with his pad at the one object he had chosen. He thought of Maren
at the recess outside — the hum through the material of the hill,
the whole hillside held open because of the pressure of one finger
against a small mark. He thought of Brennan in Greenford, whose
eight-page letter had said *for unchanged hands* and had not, in
the letter, explained the phrase because the phrase had not needed
explaining between them. Brennan would have to be told. Aldric did
not know yet by what channel. The channels he had been trained to
use would suppress what he sent through them. The channel would
have to be elsewhere.

He set his palm on the panel.

---

A voice began.

He did not understand the words.

The voice was a man's. Middle-register. Tired. Not performing. Not
preaching. Not reporting to a superior who expected brevity. The
voice spoke at the cadence of a man who had sat down at the end of
a day he had not expected to have, saying what had to be said
because no one else would say it. The vowels were long. Some of the
consonants were sounds Aldric's language did not use. But the tone
was a tone Aldric had heard thirty years' worth of — in the cells
of dying pilgrims, at the bedsides of men setting down their work.
It was the tone of ending.

He closed his eyes.

He let the voice finish.

He understood nothing of the words. But in the middle, the same
four-letter unit he had read on the first object — the figure-word,
the inner-orbit figure doing the work of a letter — came up in the
man's speech as a name. The man said it with the care a man used
when saying a name. It was the name of the mission, or the ship, or
the office; he could not tell which. Saying it was something the
man had decided he was going to do before the ending.

After the name, the voice became quieter. A pause. Then a single
word with the shape of a sign-off.

The voice was finished.

Aldric lifted his palm.

He had never believed, even in his worst scholarly years, that the
people who had made the figure on the door of the Confluence had
been anything other than people. But he had held them as figures
from an *office* — a faceless enterprise, the sort of thing a
theology used to put scale under its story. The voice corrected
the scale. The office had been staffed by a man, among other men.
The man had been tired at a certain hour. The man had said what he
had said. The man had probably not known what would follow the
ending he was at. The man had done the work of speaking the name.

He put the voiced register back.

---

He crossed again to the written shelf and picked up the ship
diagram and carried it back to the manifest, and he reached for a
third written object further down the shelf — its upper face
glimpsed from the catalog, the format of a *study*, a report on a
subject. He carried that one back too and laid it beside the other
two. He was triangulating. He was doing what a Keeper did when the
documents had begun to talk to each other.

The third object.

The subject-heading used a word he had learned by its shape in the
first log's body: *native*. The body was denser here — paragraphs,
tables, diagrams at the page-foot. He took a long moment to
understand the diagrams as cellular, because he had never actually
seen a diagram of a cell, only descriptions of them in the natural-
philosophical treatises of the Greenford library. But these were
cells. The cells held the same kind of structure in both versions
of the side-by-side panel, and the difference between the two was
the difference he had been trying not to see.

The left version had a small figure inside the cell that the right
version did not.

The figure was the inner-orbit figure, rendered in the small.

He looked at it for a long time.

The office had used the same figure for many things. It had been,
for ten thousand years on Gaius, the emblem of the mission. It had
become, by a distance, the emblem of theology. It had become, by a
further distance, a carving on the doors of a temple, a mark on the
edge of a disc, the shape of an obsessive young man's knife in
pine. Here, at its earliest, it was the office's stamp for *mission-
related* — the notation an office put in a corner of a diagram to
say *this is ours*. The left version of the cell had been marked
*ours*. The right had not.

The left was the version the Chrysalis had made.

The right was the version before.

The cells were not different species. They were the same species at
two states, and the states were before-and-after contact with a
subject, and the subject was named in the heading by the word
*native*, and the native was the organism the report was on.

He laid the report down.

He did not move for a breath. The floor of the storage held him the
way a floor held a man, and he let it.

The Chrysalis was not a gift.

The Chrysalis was not a sacrament.

The Chrysalis had been waiting on Gaius when the colony ship came
down through the sky it had no business being in, and the Chrysalis
had entered the surviving colonists through soil and air and water.
The Chrysalis had not *chosen* them. The Chrysalis had done what
the Chrysalis did. Those who had not died of it had changed. The
ones who had not changed had been the *unchanged*. The office had a
word for them. The word was on the right version of the cell:
*native-resistant baseline*. He did not know the alphabet but he
knew the grammar and he knew the shape of a technical phrase.

*Native-resistant baseline.*

He said the phrase in English.

He said it in the Keepers' Latin.

He said it in the old ritual tongue of the Confluence, in which the
Chrysalis Rite's oldest prayers were chanted — and the phrase in
that tongue did not fit. The ritual tongue did not have a word for
*baseline*. The ritual tongue had a word for *chosen*.

*Chosen* had always been how the bloodline described itself. He had
never trusted the word; the word had always rung to him like a word
put into a theology by its beneficiaries. But he had trusted the
structure the word stood in — that the bloodline had a *capacity*
the other lines did not have, and the capacity was the foundation
of ten thousand years of doctrine about the administration of
Founding-era objects.

The capacity was in the right-hand cell.

The capacity was: the cell did not admit the organism. The cell was
unchanged. An unchanged body was the body the ship's systems had
been designed to recognize, because the ship's systems had been
designed before the colony had encountered the organism. An
unchanged body was the baseline the office had assumed. An
unchanged body was the body for whom the disc functioned as a crew
ID, because the disc had been made for the body that remained in
the condition the office had cataloged it in.

The bloodline were unchanged.

They had not been chosen. They had been immune.

Immune was a smaller word than chosen. Immune was a smaller word
than *gifted*. Immune described a property. The property was a
genetic trait the descendants had carried forward, and the trait
had been the key to the pre-crash tools, and the access to the
tools had built the whole theology their descendants' descendants
believed.

He thought about the Autumnal Rite. He had sat at the Confluence's
high altar for thirty Autumnal Rites and watched the participants
approach for the mark. He had named each mark by its coming. He had
read the readings and sung the canticles and invoked the gift-
givers. The gift-givers had never given anything. The gift-givers
had been a category a minority had built to describe their own
immunity as a blessing, so that they could live with having access
to the tools of the ship and with the rest of the population not
having that access.

The temple had been built over a debris-field. The altar had been
laid over the bloodline's immunity. The Rite he had administered
for thirty years had celebrated, as a gift, a biological event that
had killed most of the colonists and had changed the rest.

He did not feel the anger now either.

What arrived in its place he could only name as *tenderness*. Not
for the office.
Not for the bloodline. For the long slow shape of a story a people
had made out of their own biology because making the story had been
how those people had lived with their biology. The Chrysalis had
come. The Chrysalis had done what it did. The unchanged had held
the key and the key had worked, and the tools had worked for them
and not for their neighbors, and the unchanged had done what human
beings had always done when they had access and other people did
not — they had built a story in which their access was legitimate,
and the story had got more beautiful over time, and eventually the
liturgy had been what the country understood about itself. The
liturgy had been beautiful. He had loved it. He had *loved* it. He
did not now un-love it. He looked at it across the distance the
right-hand cell had put him on the other side of, and he saw it the
way he saw, at a remove, a thing he had been given as a child and
had carried a long way and was now setting down because the object
under his hand required both hands.

He set the study back.

---

Maren spoke from the passage.

"Aldric. Davan."

Low. Placing them.

"Here," Aldric said.

"Here," Davan said.

"A voice," she said. The word was careful. She had heard something,
or she had felt it through the material. "I felt it under my hand.
The pulse went shorter for a count."

"A voice," Aldric said. "The register carried. I did not think the
carrying would reach you."

"It did not reach. The hill answered something. I read the answer."

"Yes."

A pause. Then: "Is it bad."

He considered.

"No," he said. "It is true."

"All right," she said. The word arrived in her two-word register.
Something had settled at depth. He heard it settle.

"I will be longer," he said.

"Yes."

---

He reached for a fourth object.

The next in the row. Its catalog figure next in sequence. He
suspected a study — the format, from a distance, was the study-
format. He brought it to his lap and set it with the other three he
had laid out on the floor. Four objects in a line now, from four
shelf-positions. He had assembled in an hour the shape he had not
been able to see from the outside of the hull.

Its heading used two words he had learned.

*For the colony.*

And a third word he had not yet seen. A heading-word: centered,
larger, its letters spaced the way a title was spaced. The heading
said, if he had read it right — and he thought he had read it
enough right — *archive, colonial-genetic, Mission Office,
Hold-IV*.

He had stopped breathing for a moment. The body caught itself and
began again, because the body did what the body did, and he let it.

The object he held was the study-document whose subject was the
contents of the four smooth containers in his undercroft.

Hold IV. The hold at the stern of the lander whose four containers
had been found intact, at the Confluence, in the secondary debris
scatter he had not known until this month was a debris scatter. The
hold had carried an *archive*.

He read what he could read of the body. A description — short,
because a document describing a genetic archive did not need to be
long; the archive was the archive. He could not read most of the
caption-words, but one recurring figure was the figure for
*preserved*. Another was *viable*. A third was *human*. A fourth he
guessed from position as a unit-word for the kind of thing a biology
archive was made of — *sample*, or *material*.

Preserved. Viable. Human.

The four containers held preserved, viable, human material.

Material from the unchanged baseline.

Material the mission had brought with it to seed the colony's long-
term genetic health from, before the mission had encountered the
Chrysalis, before the encounter had made baseline-cells into
something else across most of the colonists. The colony had carried
with it the old version of itself, cryopreserved in holds, bound
for a thousand-year health plan whose plan-makers had not
anticipated a native organism that would make the plan a different
kind of plan by breakfast.

The plan had survived. In the holds. In the containers. Under the
floor of his temple.

A further entry near the bottom caught his eye. Smaller print. A
footnote — his eye knew the shape of a footnote across languages —
bracketed, formatted as a contingency clause. He did not try to
read the text word-by-word. He saw the figure-words he had been
collecting: *native*, *resistance*, *baseline*. And a further figure
whose position in the clause was the position of a *requires*.

He read the footnote as best he could.

The footnote said — in the shape he was certain of, even if the
grammar was not — that opening the archive containers required a
condition. The condition was the absence of the native organism
from the opener's biology. The requirement was absolute. The
archive had been sealed with a biological lock that recognized
baseline. A body carrying the organism could not open the hold. The
lock had been designed, in office-of-the-mission before-the-crash,
to guard against contamination of the preserved material by foreign
biology. Against contamination in the opener's direction — the lock
made no distinction.

The holds could not be opened by any living person on Gaius, because
every living person on Gaius carried the Chrysalis integrated into
their cell-structure, even those — the bloodline, Maren — whose
immune systems had kept the active organism out. The organism had
been present across the population for ten thousand years; the
bloodline's descendants were not the same pre-organism baseline
their ancestors had been, even if those descendants were immune.
The lock was set to the pre-organism baseline. The population the
lock had been designed for did not exist on this planet any more.

Opening the holds required making a person who was the pre-organism
baseline.

Undoing the Chrysalis in them first.

He said the word in his mind. He said *undoing*. He said it in
three languages. It sat in each of them like a stone — a stone a
man had lifted out of a stream-bed and had set on the bank. The
stream moved on. The stone was on the bank. The stone was now a
choice.

He did not yet have the choice in his hand.

He was at the threshold of the choice. He was not yet across it.

Across it was the next Keeper's ground. The next Keeper was going
to be the Keeper who took the floor up, which he had said to
himself three nights ago and which was, now, not a sentence he had
said but a description of the work in front of him. The floor was
already partially up. He was sitting on the floor of the storage
that was under the floor of the chapel. He had not *taken it up* in
the sense of a decision-taken; he had *walked through* it in the
sense of a route-walked. The taking-up would be the coming-back-
out, and the going-back-to-the-Confluence, and the saying-of-what-
had-been-found, and the channel-built-by-hand for saying it in.

That work was tomorrow's work. This hour's work was to receive what
was in front of him.

He received.

He set the archive-document back at its catalog position — the
others too, in order, respecting the row — though he did not yet
stand. He remained seated. He put his hands flat on his thighs. He
let his breath even.

He thought about the next object. He thought he might cross to the
voiced-register wall and let a second voice into the room. He was
composing his next motion in his head the way he composed the
order of a vigil — what came first, what came second.

---

Davan spoke.

"Aldric."

Low. The carrying register Davan used when he had something he did
not want to carry across the floor at volume. Aldric turned his
head. Davan was still at his shelf. He had not moved his pad. But
his face was at the passage.

"Vane."

Aldric had heard nothing at the passage. He listened and heard
nothing still, and then the scrape of a boot on the material at the
threshold that was not the scrape of a boot he knew yet, and then
Vane's voice, dry, at the mouth of the passage.

"Aldric. Davan. Come to the angle."

The voice a hunter used when a hunter was not yet telling you to
move fast. It was telling him to stop what he had been doing.

He stood. The knee protested; he let it protest.

"How near," he said.

"Near enough. His boot is on the slope, above the cart. He is going
quiet. He has not seen Edvar yet — Edvar is flat in the southern
lee and knows. One man. The only scent on the slope. The other
four are not here today."

"All right."

He heard his own voice in the storage's register and heard it carry
the way the material carried — close, warm, the voice held a hand's-
breadth by the wall before the wall gave it back. His voice
sounded older to him than he had heard it sound in some while. It
was the voice of a man who had just read what he had read and had
his hands flat on his thighs a minute ago and was going to walk
out because a follower of Horst's was at the breach.

He looked once at the shelves. He had put the four objects back in
their catalog places. The voiced register was back on its opposite
shelf. The row he had made on the floor was gone. A library paid
the courtesy of leaving a shelf as it found it. The courtesy he
owed was that courtesy, because he did not yet know whether he
would return, and if he did not return, the next reader should
find the catalog intact.

He turned toward the passage.

Davan had come off the shelf. His pad had lifted at a rate slower
than a lift — he had asked his pad to leave the surface and the
pad had done it, and the departure had been a courtesy. Davan's
color was low. His wrists lit faintly at the new register; the
peak was past.

"Ready," Davan said.

"Yes."

They crossed to the mouth of the passage together.

Aldric set his hand on the wall of the passage as he went out. He
had not touched it on the way in; he had not had permission yet. On
the way out, the wall gave a cool to his palm without his asking —
a cool that did not travel. The palm carried it the length of two
steps and then the palm was his palm again. The giving had been the
final beat of the reading. The archive, in the register it had, had
acknowledged that he had read.

The passage was narrow at top, widening at chest-height, narrow
again at the bottom, as it had been coming in, and the air moved
past him toward the outside. Ahead, a pale square of morning at the
end of the passage. Maren's silhouette at the recess, her thumb on
the disc, her back not turned, her hand not moving. She had not
moved in the time he had been inside. The hill was being held open
under her pad. The held-open-ness had been the condition of his
reading. It was still the condition.

Beyond Maren's knee, on the slope above the cart, a second
silhouette moved along the north shoulder — small at that distance,
the shape of a man who did not know he was visible yet, coming down
through the low scrub with the quiet of a professional. Edvar,
beyond Maren, was not visible from the passage angle; but a thin
line of horse-breath steamed up from the lee of the stone where the
cart was, and the breath was even, the breath of a horse being held
at its halter by a hand that knew the horse.

Aldric stopped at the angle Vane had named.

He did not step further. He did not step back.

Behind him the storage held. Ahead of him was the hillside with the
disc in its recess and Maren's thumb on the disc, and the follower
on the slope, and the morning, and the work of the day which had
not yet been done.

The reading was interrupted.

A boot scraped on scree above the cart. Quietly, not quietly enough.
He heard it at the angle the way he would have heard a footfall in
a chapel aisle during a vigil — the small sound that said the vigil
was no longer alone. Vane, invisible at the shoulder below, made no
sound at all. Davan, at his shoulder inside the passage, was
holding the new color low at his wrists.

The boot came down on the scree again. One pace nearer.

He breathed in. He breathed out.

The reading had not finished. The world had come in anyway.

---


## Chapter Thirty — *What the Moons Keep*

---

### *Vane*

The boot came down on scree a second time and she had his weight.

Not his face. His pace. Pace gave her a body's long habit the way a
voice gave her its owner's country. Toe placed before heel, knee
absorbing what the scree wanted to do to him. Horst's instructor
had been a woman named Jax in the Threshing Yard. Vane had taken
the same course in her third year. The carriage on the slope was
Jax's carriage.

*One of my cohort's, or one of the next.*

She catalogued without moving. Flat in the north-shoulder lee, a
knuckle of stone giving her a shadow the man would not break
against until he was three paces past. Wind off her right shoulder.
Breathing through her mouth — the bark grain at the nose-bridge had
dried to a register where nasal air whistled, and whistles carried.

Seventy paces to Edvar and the cart. Edvar flat in the southern lee,
the off-horse at the cheek-strap. The horse knew him and had not
blown.

*Sixty.*

Waxed grey coat short-cut at the hip. A sword — Horst's men always
wore a sword, and a sword set a civilian off by a hundred yards,
which was half its point. No bow. No Wound-maker.

*Fifty.*

Right hand on the knife, hilt turned so the blade came edge-up if
the drawing was what this became. Left hand with a loop of fine
cord bunched in the palm. She had decided on the cord in the five
minutes before he made the crest. She did not want blood on the
hill. The hill had been bled on enough.

Her left hand was steady. Withdrawal had redistributed the tremor
overnight — hands to shoulders, which the coat hid. The shoulders
were vibrating at the clavicle. The hands would do cord-work when
cord-work came.

*Forty.*

She heard Aldric step at the passage mouth. The knee that did not
come up clean made a small asymmetric sound a trained ear would
catch at ten paces. The contract man was at forty, carriage at
eighty percent of attention; Jax's course did not teach a man to
listen for a knee in a passage he did not know existed. The sound
did not reach him.

Maren, up at the recess, had not moved. Vane had checked her three
times from the lee angle — thumb on the disc, back to the slope,
the hillside under her knee in the low warmth Davan had said was
*waking up*. Vane did not know what was waking. Her job was a man
on a slope; other people had other jobs today, and the disciplines
held when the disciplines held.

The man paused.

Not at a wind-sign. At something he had heard through the surface.
The bone-sound. It had registered at the level his training told
him was not weather and not heart. He put his hand inside his coat.

He drew — not the sword. A small pale thing, palm-sized. Not the
inner orbit symbol; a different glyph, the one the bloodline's
domestic office used for field-report. He held it flat. He was
going to report.

*Before he engages, he confirms.* The right move for a contract
man who had walked three days and found a hillside humming under
its own grass. The thing he had found was outside his brief.

She came up off her knee.

Not fast. Fast came at the end. Up at the rate grass came up in
wind, foot placed at the marked toe-spot, three paces in the slide-
step she had been taught the year of her scarring, in at his back at
an angle past the carry of his sword-side, cord over his wrist and
twisted before he could take the hand-disc up to his mouth to speak.

He was good. He turned in the cord and did not lose the disc, and
his left was coming for the sword and had it half drawn when her
knee went into the back of his right knee and her free hand came up
under his jaw and her knife was at the soft hollow under his ear
before his weight transferred.

"Drop it," she said. Threshing Yard register. "Sword. Hand-disc.
Keep the voice out of it."

He let the sword re-sheathe. The move of a man taught that a
sheathed sword was still a sword, and a lost sword in contract
accounting was a cost. She approved. She did not remove the knife.

"Hand-disc."

He lowered it slowly between his boots and set it on the grass with
his fingertips.

"Name."

"Mara."

Contract name. Fine.

"Horst."

"Yes."

"Brief."

"Authenticate signature. Secure the disc. Prefer the holder alive.
Report before close."

"You were reporting."

"I was."

"Outcome."

"Signature confirmed. Holder at the recess. Company four. Request
instruction."

"Request instruction. Smart. What were you going to do if the
instruction was *close*."

"What I was trained to do."

"Alive."

"Preferred."

"If preferred failed."

He did not answer. The silence answered.

She had known this was the answer. She had known it since the ford.
The professional part of her had filed it. The other part — the one
that had been doing work in her for five days without her looking
at it — had filed it somewhere heavier.

"Not today," she said.

His jaw was flat against the knife.

"You are going to kneel. You are going to tell me where the other
four of Horst's team are. Then you are going to walk back to New
Plymouth and tell Horst *signature did not register*. You can say
you lost contact. You can say the disc was a decoy. Tell him
anything except the truth."

"The truth."

"What is on the other side of this hill. Horst's office does not
have a brief for it. If you tell him the truth, the domestic office
comes with five teams, and the five teams make the hill a place
where the question of what to do with what's inside it is decided
by people whose job is to decide questions that way. You are one
man. I am one woman. We decide this like one and one."

Her shoulders vibrated at the clavicle. She kept the knife where
it was.

"If that sounds like mercy, it is not. It is a contract. If Horst's
men come over this ridge in the next month, I find you first. Alone.
In a house at an hour of my choosing. You know I can."

"I know who you were."

"Then kneel."

He knelt.

---

She bound him at the wrist and ankle and pushed him gently onto
his side. She did not gag him — a gag on an unwilling man in open
country was a thing that killed men who had not been meant to be
killed.

"Where are the other four."

"One on the valley road with the horses. Two in New Plymouth with
Horst. One in Stillwater on a different matter."

"The one with the horses holds station until sundown. You will
return by sundown without a signal. You will ride for New Plymouth
with him. You will report *signature did not register* and take
your fee and not come east again."

"I will not come east again."

"Good."

She stood. The vibration had moved to a band across her ribs and
the band was the price. She walked up-slope keeping to the lee,
touched Edvar's shoulder in passing — a palm on the coat, the way
you touched an old dog that had held its stand. Edvar did not turn.
The horse did not blow.

"Clear," she said. For his ear.

"I'm here."

"The cart stays. I'll come for you when."

"I'll be here when."

She climbed the last twenty paces to the passage mouth. Maren still
at the recess, hair at her cheek, thumb on the disc. Had heard none
of it, or had heard all of it and done the work of not turning.

Vane put her palm on the wall at the angle — the cool came into her
palm and did not travel, the way Aldric had said — and she called
in, low.

"Aldric. Davan. The slope is held. Mine. The one who was coming is
off his feet."

A pause inside. Then Aldric, low: "Thank you."

She did not answer. *Thank you* was not a thing she took yet. She
stood at the angle with her hand on the wall and the vibration in
her ribs and the sword and the hand-disc at her hip, and the day's
work — in the sense it was ever done — was done.

Under her boots, through the hill, something was being said.
Something was being answered. Not her language. Not anyone's
language she had been hired to read.

She stood the angle anyway. Which was, she thought, the new thing.
You stood angles for languages that were not yours.

---

### *Davan*

He had not left the shelf.

His pad was still against the object. He had said *ready* at the
passage mouth and his body had turned with Aldric's toward the
passage, and then his body had turned back.

The one kept thing his finger had chosen had not asked him to stay.
The room had not asked him. The staying had been his.

Outside, on the slope, a man was being spoken to by Vane. He could
not hear the words. The passage at that angle did not carry voice.
But he had — in the underside of his forearms where the new color
lived — the quality of it. A man's fear, reined in. A professional
discipline holding the fear flat. The cost of the holding. Vane's
own pressure on the slope, which he had come to know at the cart
in the last three days — not anger. *Economy*. A woman doing the
least motion that produced the correct result, because the economy
was what had been left her that was still hers.

His wrists were lit low. He did not look at them. He had stopped
looking at them a long way back. He had come, by the slow route a
body took to its own instruments, to trust what his wrists said the
way an older carpenter trusted what his plane said.

He did not want to pick the object up.

He wanted — and the wanting was a new register — to ask the object
a question. Not in words. In the register his skin had made with
the wall's register, when the new color had settled in him and the
wall had held the color and the holding had been the thing the
material did because *keeping* was what the material did.

He put his palm against the flat side of the object. Not the pad of
a finger this time. The palm.

The object was at the same register as the walls. He had expected
this. He had not expected the difference — the object was *denser*.
The wall held what came near it; the object held what was already
inside it. The object was a wall in miniature, closed on a thing
that had been put in it to be there, and what had been put in it
was pressing gently outward against the inside of its own holding
the way a seed pressed gently outward against the inside of a seed.

He read the seed.

He did not try to read *what* was in the object. He had not earned
the what. The what was a different work — an opening work, a
beginning-of-a-line-of-people work, a work none of the four of them
was the instrument for. He read, instead, the quality of the
holding. The long patience of it. The absence of any hurry.

What was being held in this room, and in the four containers under
Aldric's temple, was — Aldric had said it last night through the
small lamp at camp — *preserved, viable, human*. The thing under
his palm was kin to those four.

*A whole line of people, kept.*

*Not kept like a coat kept. Not kept like a memory kept. Kept the
way a seed is kept — alive at a register where the life is paused,
and the life is still the life, and the life is waiting without
knowing it is waiting.*

*And if we were to wake them, we would have to become a thing we
are not, first.*

He took his palm off.

His wrist-light did not fade. It lived in the tendon. It had been
living there since yesterday morning, and it would live there for
the rest of his life. The color was not his mutation any more. The
color was what his mutation had been reaching for all twenty years,
and had reached.

He turned from the shelf.

Aldric was at the passage-mouth and past it, at the angle where
Vane had called him. Davan did not go to the angle. He had not been
called. He crossed to the center of the floor and stood there with
his hands at his sides and let his wrists hold their light, and he
said — not aloud; to the room — the thing he needed to say before
he left.

*I am not going to ask you to be opened.*

He stood a moment with the sentence in his chest.

*I am not going to ask anyone to be opened.*

The walls took his color and held it a hand's breadth and gave it
back. The color between his body and the material was a color
neither his nor the material's, and he had come, in the last half-
hour, to think of that color as the color *deciding* made when a
body decided against the grain of the thing it might, under other
pressure, have said yes to.

His mutation was a gift the world had given him. It was also a debt
the world had laid on him. He did not know, today, how to separate
them. He did not know whether separation was the right work. The
originals he was kin to — the right-hand cell in Aldric's study,
the holds at this ship's stern, the four containers under the
Confluence — were his ancestors. Kin across a distance his body did
not quite have the words for.

He owed them the sense not to open them to relieve himself of the
debt. He did not know what he would have come to think in a year,
or ten. Today, this hour, the answer his body gave was: *let them
rest. Let the Chrysalis be what the Chrysalis is. I am the
Chrysalis now. What I owe the originals is the attention, not the
un-making.*

He was not going to say this to Aldric yet. Aldric had another
instrument. Maren had another. Vane had another. He said it to the
room.

The room held it without answering, the way the room held everything
it was asked to hold. The lack of answer was not silence. The lack
of answer was what answering meant when you were a room whose work
was holding. The room's yes and no were the same thing: *held.*

He walked, slowly, toward the passage.

---

### *Aldric*

The passage carried his step and handed it back.

He stopped at the angle Vane had named.

Beyond the recess, beyond Maren's shoulder, down-slope — a flat
register of grass and rust and a man's boot in it at the rest-angle
a boot took when the boot was not planning to move again for a
while. Vane was coming up from the lee with the carriage of a worker
whose work had landed. The vibration in the set of her ribs he
caught at the second look. Not there at the cart in the grey
morning. There now. She had spent something to do what she had
done.

He had asked her to spend it, without asking.

He would keep the account.

The *mine* in her call went down like a stone in still water. He
did not step past it. The slope was hers. The storage behind him
was — he did not yet have a verb for it — *attended*. The hill was
Maren's knee. The cart was Edvar's hand. The breach was the
morning's. He was the one at the angle between the passage and the
slope, weight on the balls of his feet the way a man stood when his
body did not yet know whether the next move was forward or back.

His body did not have to know.

"Thank you," he said. Down the lee. She did not answer, which was
the correct not-answering.

He put his palm against the wall of the passage and let the cool
be, and he thought, for the second time in an hour, the sentence he
had been composing for three months.

*The next Keeper is going to be the one who took the floor up.*

He had been carrying it as something about a future Aldric — three
months older, five years older — who had gone back to the Confluence
and had, with the slow patience of institutional change, persuaded
the Temple to admit what the chamber under the altar held. The
composed thing was the wrong composition.

*The next Keeper is not a future me. The next Keeper is me, now.
The floor has been taken up. I am standing on the taken-up floor.*

It did not land as fear. He tried the word the way a man tried a
garment he had not worn for a season; the garment did not fit. What
he carried was closer to *consequence*. Thirty years of weight at a
certain balance, now being re-balanced, and the re-balancing was
the body learning the new load-point. The hand no longer went to
the lamp-cord because the lamp-cord was not the instrument.

Three things, then, in the register a man had three things when he
had stopped composing and had started moving:

*The records are not a find. The records are a floor. The floor is
under my life's work. The life's work does not continue the way it
was going.*

*The floor does not belong to me. It belongs to anyone who will have
to live with what the floor is — which is every living person on
Gaius. I am not the one who tells them. I am the one who does not
stop them from being told.*

*The channel is not the Temple's. The channel has to be built. It
will be built by the people who built the cooperage group, and by
Brennan, and by Reth, and by the young cartographer whose name I did
not catch, and by others I have not yet met. I will carry documents.
I will carry what I saw. I will, for a while, be a man who has lost
his frock and has not yet learned what a man without a frock does,
and I will learn.*

He thought of Crane — the danger of an officer of a company whose
shareholder was a theology, and whose shareholder had just taken a
loss of ten thousand years of dividend. He thought of the long
letter he was going to have to write. It would not be to Brennan.
Would not be to Cael. *The letter is to the next reader,* he
thought. *The next reader is not a Keeper. The next reader is the
country.*

He walked out of the passage into the morning.

Pale blue sky, wind-thinned, the overnight weather cleared by the
dry wind off the country. He did not look up. He was not ready. He
looked across the slope to Maren, stopped one pace short, put his
hands flat on the tops of his thighs the way a man put his hands
when his hands were honoring a work that was not his.

"The storage held," he said. Low. "Davan is coming out."

"Yes."

"The man."

"Vane."

"Yes."

A pause.

"The records say. I have read what I could read. I will read again.
It was as we suspected and more. There is something we will need to
decide about, and it is not a decision I am going to decide for the
four of us."

"All right."

Her thumb had not moved. Her shoulder was the shoulder of a girl who
had knelt at a recess for two hours and would kneel at it for
another two if the hill asked her. He could not tell, from where he
stood, whether she was breathing. Then he could. She was.

"When you are ready," he said.

"When I am ready."

His hand at his side did not move toward any cord. The hand was at
his thigh. The thigh was his thigh. The hand was his hand.

He stepped back two paces and turned toward where the passage met
the morning, and he waited for Davan to come out.

---

### *Maren*

Something was beginning under her thumb.

Not new. Continuing. The hum had stopped and the bone-sound had
begun and the saying-and-answer — Davan's phrase, from the outcrop
at dusk — had been running under the hill since the seating. Her
knee had gone to the kind of numbness where the numbness was its
own sensation. Her thumb was on the symbol.

The disc was warm and lit and not hers any more and still hers.
*Hers* was not a category of having. *Hers* was a category of
carrying. She had carried the disc for seventeen years and the disc
had carried her for seventeen years, and now it was carrying her
and the hill both, and the carrying was the relation, and the
relation did not belong to anyone.

She had not wept at her father's coat. She was not going to weep at
the recess either. The weeping country was a country for later.
Her body was doing a different work today.

Behind her, on the slope, she had heard Vane. Not the words. The
pace. The slide-step Vane had practiced on the hotel floor in New
Plymouth. The pitched-low professional register Vane had used once
on the road from the woodcutter's shelter when she had been talking
a bolted trail-mare down. Maren had not turned. Turning would have
been a question, and she had not had a question to ask.

Aldric had come out and stood beside her and not touched her, and
had said *when you are ready*, and had stepped back two paces to
give her the recess. The giving of the recess was the same thing
Edvar had done at the shoulder of bare stone when he had put his
hand on her hair and taken it away. Two older men had said *go*
this morning in the only word each had for *go*, and both had let
her go.

She was still at the recess.

Under her thumb a second resonance had arrived.

The bone-sound was the continuous condition, the tide's pull of
being at the recess. The second came up through the first at about
the height of her hip — Davan's *far*, the saying-and-answer at a
distance the word *distance* was not built for. It was in the
underside of her jaw where jaw met neck. A place her body had used,
when she had been a child, to tell her when a frog had moved in the
rushes on the other side of the pond at Ashwick before her eye had
caught it.

Something far was answering.

The answer had been coming for two hours. It was close now, to
whatever *close* meant at this distance.

She kept her thumb on the symbol. She waited.

---

The answer arrived as voice.

Not at her ear. At the hill. The storage behind her took a breath
the way rooms took breaths, and into the breath the voice came —
not a new voice; the one Aldric had heard at the voiced wall and
had told them about by the camp lamp last night. A middle-register
man's voice, tired, at the cadence of a man who had sat down at the
end of a day he had not expected to have. Aldric had said: *the
words did not resolve*.

The words resolved. Not because any of them had learned the
language. Because the saying-and-answer — the *far* Davan had been
reading — had arrived at *close*, and in the arriving it had
brought with it the matching half of the record that had been
locked against the voice in the wall for ten thousand seasons. The
answer laid itself over the saying the way a transparent page laid
over a page it had been drawn to fit.

Not translated. *Paired.* Maren heard, with one ear, the long
vowels and the consonants her country's tongue did not use; with
another ear — if a body had an ear for this — she heard the sense.

The man was saying: *This is the captain of the fourth ship.*

A pause. A breath. The breath of a man who had just set something
down on a desk he was not going to pick up again.

*We failed our descent. The lander is down in the eastern foothills.
Most of us did not reach the ground. Those of us who did are not, I
think, going to remain who we are for very long. The world is in us.
There is no way out of it.*

Another pause.

*We have moved what could be moved from the holds to the secondary
cache at the river-meeting site. The archive goes with it. The
archive does not open with the hands we have now. It is not meant
to. It is meant for whoever comes after, if there is a whoever, if
the after is kind.*

*To the three still on station: we did what we could. You are not
coming for us. I am sorry. Hold your post as long as the post will
hold you. When it does not hold you any longer, go home. We are
home already, in a way. The children will be a new people. I think
they will be a people worth the coming.*

A longer pause.

*This is the last of the log I am going to record. There is a
morning outside and I am going to go sit in it. I am told there is
a hillside to the east that moves in the light. It is our own hull.
Imagine. I am going to go look at it.*

The man said the mission name — the four-letter unit, the inner
orbit figure doing the work of a letter. Aldric had told them about
this. The man said it the way a man said a name. Then a single word
with the shape of a sign-off. A man getting up from a desk.

The voice was finished.

Maren did not move.

She had heard the whole of it on her knee at the recess, and the
hearing had gone through her like a bell rung at a distance of ten
thousand seasons, and the bell had rung as a bell rings when the
hand that strikes it is, just before striking, alive.

Aldric, behind her, had made no sound.

Davan, at the passage mouth, had made no sound.

Vane, at the angle, had made no sound.

She heard them, through the saying-and-answer, being four people who
had heard the same voice at the same moment and had been, each of
them, the only person in the world who had heard it, because each
of them had heard it in the register their own body had made to
catch it.

The captain had looked at a hillside in the morning and had gone out
to sit in the light. Ten thousand years ago. Before the word
*Ashwick*. Before the word *Keeper*. Before the word *bloodline*.
The captain had gone out to sit.

She was a girl at a recess with her thumb on a disc, and the captain
had been a man her body was kin to, and the man had gone out to sit
in the morning, and she was — had always been, without the word for
it — one of the children he had said might be a people worth the
coming.

---

The answer arrived as light.

Above her. A thinning of the morning across the whole east sky, the
way a cloud thinned when a cloud was about to give up. There was no
cloud. The morning had been clear for an hour. For three seconds
the color of daylight acquired an under-register — a second color
under the pale blue — that did not belong to morning.

She lifted her eye from the disc.

Her knee was past caring about knees. She only lifted her eye — from
the recess to the slope-line, from the slope-line to the crest of
the eastern ridge, from the crest of the ridge to the sky above the
ridge.

The sky above the ridge held three moons.

---

She had been looking away from the moons for three years.

She had known they were in the sky. She had looked at her boots.
She had looked at the fire. She had looked at Davan's face or
Vane's back or Aldric's hands. She had not looked up. Looking up
had been a thing some version of herself had done when she had been
a child and had not yet known the moons were a country she had not
been invited into, and that version had been, for three years, one
she had kept at a distance while the version with the disc in her
coat had walked.

Now she looked up.

Three moons, full-bright together, over the eastern hills. The
ridge of the country ran under them in a long dark line. The sky
between them was the dry pale blue of the eastern weather, and the
moons were each what they were and were also, as she looked, three
of a thing.

Her eye gave her what her eye had not given her in three years. Not
that they were ships. Her eye did not know ships. Her eye gave her
that they were a set. That the sky was holding them at the positions
a sky held things at when the things had been placed at the
positions and had stayed. They were not drifting. They were *at*.
At the places they were at. At the work they were at. Doing what
they had been doing, which was — because her body had learned the
word in the passage, and at the recess, and in the captain's quiet
voice a minute ago — *holding*.

They were holding their stations.

They had been holding them the whole time she had been walking.
They had been holding them when her father had walked east and had
lain down four paces short of the recess. They had been holding
them when her mother had died in the winter and she had held her
mother's comb the first time with the full understanding it was her
mother's.

A memory she had not carried came up under the moons-look.

She had been five. Her father in the courtyard at dusk. He had
lifted her onto his shoulder and pointed at the sky and said *look*,
and she had looked, and the moons had been three of a set even
then, and he had not said what they were, but he had made her look.

He had been making her look.
